## S2 · Q4 — Weaken

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Economist: Our country needs as much capital as possible from overseas investors in order to sustain our economy. Hence, we cannot afford any reduction in the amount of capital that overseas investors have invested here. Therefore, to sustain our economy, we should pass laws making it much more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the economist’s reasoning?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Our country needs as much capital as possible from overseas investors in order to sustain our economy.
- **Intermediate Conclusion (Hence):** We cannot afford any reduction in the amount of capital that these investors have invested here.
- **Main Conclusion (Therefore):** To sustain our economy, we should pass laws making it much more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital.
- **Evaluate:** There isn't a huge gap in logic between needing as much capital as possible (the evidence) and not wanting any reduction in the existing amount of capital (the intermediate conclusion).

  But there is a significant gap between the intermediate conclusion and the main conclusion. Wanting to avoid any reduction in investments does not automatically support the idea that we should pass laws making it more difficult for investors to remove their capital.

  This main conclusion is a recommendation. Plans and recommendations tend to be vulnerable to a variety of objections, and the correct answer to a Weaken question might focus on any one of them. It helps to think about the kinds of objections that this argument is particularly vulnerable to.

  For example, a recommendation might have the opposite effect. Maybe these laws would discourage investors from making future investments, which would prevent us from acquiring "as much capital as possible." Or there might be some reason why this particular recommendation can't be implemented, or won't be effective. Maybe these types of laws are almost impossible to enforce, and therefore tend to be ineffective.
- **Goal:** Look for an answer that suggests the recommendation might backfire or be impossible to implement correctly. But, since objections can take many forms, read answers with an open mind. We want to notice if one attacks the recommendation in another way.

**Answer choices**
**A. To sustain its economy, the country needs to diversify its investments more evenly across the country’s industries.** — Out of Scope: diversify (2% of students)
This answer discusses diversifying investments across the country's industries. The argument is about attracting and retaining overseas investment capital, not about how that capital is distributed across industries. Whether the country needs to diversify its own investments is a separate issue from whether it should restrict overseas investors' ability to remove their capital. This answer addresses a different economic question entirely.

**B. Laws that would make it more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital would strongly discourage them from investing any additional capital.** — Correct (83% of students)
This is a classic "the recommendation backfires" weakener. The economist argues for laws that would make it harder for overseas investors to remove their capital, in order to retain as much overseas capital as possible. But this answer reveals a devastating unintended consequence: such laws would strongly discourage overseas investors from investing any additional capital. If investors know that once they put money in, they cannot get it out, they will simply not invest in the first place. The country needs "as much capital as possible" from overseas investors, but the restrictive laws would reduce the total pool of overseas capital by deterring new investment — directly undermining the stated goal of the policy.

**C. The historical periods during which the country’s economy had the highest rate of growth were those periods during which the amount of capital invested by overseas investors was highest.** — Premise Support (1% of students)
This answer provides historical evidence that overseas investment is correlated with economic growth. But this merely supports the premise that the country needs overseas capital to sustain its economy — something we are already given as evidence. Reinforcing a premise that is not in dispute does not weaken the argument. If anything, this makes the economist's concern about capital flight seem more justified, which would tend to strengthen rather than weaken the recommendation.

**D. In countries other than the economist’s, passage of laws that made it very difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital have not entirely prevented the removal of capital invested by overseas investors.** — Weaker Impact (14% of students)
This answer notes that similar laws in other countries have not "entirely prevented" the removal of overseas capital. While this suggests the laws might not be fully effective, it does not tell us they were wholly ineffective either. The laws might have prevented most capital removal while failing to stop all of it, which would still make them a useful policy. "Not entirely prevented" leaves open the possibility that the laws prevented a significant amount of capital removal. The correct answer provides a much stronger and clearer reason to reject the policy — it does not merely suggest partial ineffectiveness but shows the policy would actively backfire.

**E. Two years ago, the country enacted laws that place some restrictions on the removal of capital by overseas investors.** — Unclear Impact (1% of students)
This answer states that two years ago, the country enacted some restrictions on overseas capital removal. But we have no information about the effects of those laws. Were they helpful? Harmful? Neutral? Did they deter new investment? Did they prevent capital flight? This answer provides a bare fact without any indication of its consequences. It could potentially strengthen the argument (if the existing restrictions helped), weaken it (if they backfired), or have no effect at all (if they were insignificant). Without knowing the outcome, this answer's directional impact is completely indeterminate.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Our country needs as much capital as possible from overseas investors to sustain the economy.
- **Intermediate Conclusion (Hence):** We cannot afford any reduction in overseas investment capital.
- **Main Conclusion (Therefore):** We should pass laws making it much more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital.
- **Evaluate:** The main conclusion recommends a specific policy. The gap lies between wanting to keep investment capital and the specific remedy of restrictive laws. Such laws could backfire — if investors cannot easily remove capital, they might not invest in the first place.
- **Goal:** Find an answer showing the recommended policy would backfire or be counterproductive.

**Answer choices**
**A. To sustain its economy, the country needs to diversify its investments more evenly across the country’s industries.** — Out of Scope: diversify (2% of students)
The argument is about overseas capital retention, not about diversifying domestic investments across industries. This addresses a different economic question.

**B. Laws that would make it more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital would strongly discourage them from investing any additional capital.** — Correct (83% of students)
The laws meant to retain capital would deter new investment. If investors know they cannot easily remove capital, they will not invest in the first place — directly undermining the goal of attracting as much overseas capital as possible.

**C. The historical periods during which the country’s economy had the highest rate of growth were those periods during which the amount of capital invested by overseas investors was highest.** — Premise Support (1% of students)
This reinforces the premise that overseas capital is important for the economy. Supporting an undisputed premise does not weaken the argument — it strengthens it, if anything.

**D. In countries other than the economist’s, passage of laws that made it very difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital have not entirely prevented the removal of capital invested by overseas investors.** — Weaker Impact (14% of students)
"Not entirely prevented" means the laws might still have been mostly effective. This weakens the argument somewhat but not decisively, since partial prevention of capital removal could still be beneficial.

**E. Two years ago, the country enacted laws that place some restrictions on the removal of capital by overseas investors.** — Unclear Impact (1% of students)
The country already has some capital restrictions, but we do not know their effects. This fact could strengthen, weaken, or have no effect on the argument, making its impact entirely unclear.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The country is hungry for overseas investment capital. The more the better.
- **Intermediate Conclusion (Hence):** So the country really cannot afford to lose any of the capital already invested there.
- **Main Conclusion (Therefore):** Solution: trap the money. Pass laws making it nearly impossible for investors to pull their capital out.
- **Evaluate:** This is the economic equivalent of locking the restaurant doors so diners cannot leave without paying — technically it keeps people inside, but nobody new is going to walk in. If overseas investors hear that their money will be held hostage, they might decide to invest somewhere else entirely. The argument is so focused on keeping existing capital that it ignores the effect on future capital.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that describes this backfire effect.

**Answer choices**
**A. To sustain its economy, the country needs to diversify its investments more evenly across the country’s industries.** — Out of Scope: diversify (2% of students)
The argument is about keeping overseas investors' money from leaving the country, and this answer is talking about spreading investments across different industries. These are completely different conversations. It is like arguing about whether to lock the front door and getting advice about rearranging the furniture.

**B. Laws that would make it more difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital would strongly discourage them from investing any additional capital.** — Correct (83% of students)
The economist built a roach motel for money — capital checks in but it cannot check out. The problem? New capital reads the reviews and decides to stay at a different hotel. If overseas investors hear that their money will be trapped behind legislative walls, they are going to invest somewhere friendlier. The country wants "as much capital as possible," but this law would scare away far more new money than it would retain in old money. The recommendation does not just fail — it backfires spectacularly.

**C. The historical periods during which the country’s economy had the highest rate of growth were those periods during which the amount of capital invested by overseas investors was highest.** — Premise Support (1% of students)
This answer is basically saying "overseas investment really is important!" Well, nobody was arguing that point. The economist already established that the country needs as much overseas capital as possible. Learning that history confirms this just makes the economist's premise look better, not worse. It is like trying to weaken an argument for wearing seatbelts by pointing out that car crashes really are dangerous.

**D. In countries other than the economist’s, passage of laws that made it very difficult for overseas investors to remove their capital have not entirely prevented the removal of capital invested by overseas investors.** — Weaker Impact (14% of students)
"Not entirely prevented" — so the laws in other countries stopped some capital from leaving, just not all of it. That is like saying a dam did not entirely stop the river. How much leaked through? A trickle? A torrent? This answer is frustratingly vague. Maybe the laws worked great and just missed a few edge cases. Maybe they were a complete disaster. The correct answer does not leave any doubt — it says the policy would actively scare away new investment, which is a clear and devastating problem.

**E. Two years ago, the country enacted laws that place some restrictions on the removal of capital by overseas investors.** — Unclear Impact (1% of students)
The country already put some restrictions in place two years ago. Great — how did that go? This answer is all setup and no punchline. If those restrictions worked beautifully, that supports the economist. If they backfired, that undermines the economist. If nobody noticed them, they are irrelevant. Without knowing the outcome, this is just a fact floating in space, equally available to both sides of the debate.

---

## S2 · Q5 — Main Conclusion

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Columnist: Many people with access to the Internet express a longing for emotional connection to a global human community. This longing often leads them to use the Internet to learn about other cultures. However, learning about other cultures probably will not satisfy their longing, for the Internet is utilized primarily for acquiring information rather than developing feelings of interconnectedness.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion drawn in the columnist’s argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Ideas:** Many people with access to the Internet express a longing for emotional connection to a global human community, which often leads them to use the Internet to learn about other cultures.
- **Conclusion:** Learning about other cultures probably will not satisfy their longing [for emotional connection].
- **Evidence (for):** The Internet is utilized primarily for acquiring information rather than developing feelings of interconnectedness.
- **Evaluate:** This argument follows a common pattern seen in Main Conclusion questions. It starts with an Opposing Idea, followed by a pivot word. The pivot word is followed by the Conclusion, which is then followed by the Evidence. The pivot word "however" helps us spot that pattern in this argument. Also notice how the statement after "however" is a rebuttal of the statements before "however." The Conclusion of an argument is frequently a rebuttal of an Opposing Idea.
- **Goal:** Expect the correct answer to state that learning about other cultures will not satisfy a longing for emotional connection.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is common for people who have access to the Internet to express a longing for emotional connection to an international human community.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This answer restates the first sentence of the stimulus — that many Internet users express a longing for emotional connection to a global community. This is the opposing idea, the background context that the conclusion pushes back against. The conclusion is not that people have this longing; the conclusion is that the Internet-based approach to satisfying it will probably fail. This answer identifies a premise, not the conclusion.

**B. The longing for emotional connection to a global human community frequently leads people to use the Internet in order to acquire information about other cultures.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
This answer combines elements from the first and second sentences of the stimulus: the longing for emotional connection leads people to use the Internet to learn about other cultures. Like answer A, this is part of the opposing idea — the background that establishes what people are doing and why. The conclusion reacts to this behavior by predicting it will not work. This answer describes the setup, not the argument's response to it.

**C. People who have access to the Internet tend to use it primarily for acquiring information and only secondarily for developing feelings of interconnectedness.** — Premise (2% of students)
This answer closely paraphrases the evidence: the Internet is used primarily for information acquisition, with developing feelings of interconnectedness as a secondary use. This is the reason the columnist gives for the conclusion, not the conclusion itself. In the argument's structure, this statement appears after the word "for" — a classic premise indicator. The conclusion is the claim this evidence supports: learning about other cultures probably will not satisfy the longing for emotional connection.

**D. For people desiring connection to a global human community, learning about other cultures through the Internet probably will not provide emotional connection to those cultures.** — Correct (84% of students)
This accurately captures the argument's main conclusion. The columnist argues that people who want emotional connection to a global community will probably not get it from learning about other cultures through the Internet. This answer says essentially the same thing: for people seeking global community connection, Internet-based cultural learning probably will not provide emotional connection. It correctly targets the claim that appears between "however" (the pivot from opposing idea) and "for" (the pivot to evidence). It appropriately includes the qualifier "probably" and specifies the mechanism (learning through the Internet) without broadening to other activities.

**E. When people long for connection to a global human community, they tend to engage in behavior that results in the acquisition of knowledge about other cultures but rarely produces genuine emotional connection to those cultures.** — Too Broad (11% of students)
This answer broadens the argument significantly. The columnist specifically discusses using the Internet to learn about other cultures. This answer talks more broadly about people who "engage in behavior that results in the acquisition of knowledge about other cultures" — which could include traveling, attending cultural events, reading books, and countless other activities. The argument's reasoning depends on the specific limitations of the Internet as a tool (primarily for information, not interconnectedness), so broadening beyond the Internet removes the logical foundation. Additionally, this answer does not capture the idea that the Internet's information-focused nature is the reason for the predicted failure.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Ideas:** Many Internet users long for emotional connection to a global community and use the Internet to learn about other cultures to satisfy this longing.
- **Conclusion:** Learning about other cultures probably will not satisfy their longing for emotional connection.
- **Evidence (for):** The Internet is used primarily for acquiring information rather than developing feelings of interconnectedness.
- **Goal:** The correct answer should state that learning about other cultures through the Internet will not satisfy a desire for emotional connection.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is common for people who have access to the Internet to express a longing for emotional connection to an international human community.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This restates the opening context — that people long for emotional connection. The conclusion is about why the Internet will not satisfy that longing, not about the longing itself.

**B. The longing for emotional connection to a global human community frequently leads people to use the Internet in order to acquire information about other cultures.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
This combines the first two sentences — people's longing drives them to learn about cultures online. This is the background behavior the conclusion responds to, not the conclusion itself.

**C. People who have access to the Internet tend to use it primarily for acquiring information and only secondarily for developing feelings of interconnectedness.** — Premise (2% of students)
This paraphrases the evidence after the premise indicator "for." The Internet is mainly for information, not connection — that is the reason for the conclusion, not the conclusion itself.

**D. For people desiring connection to a global human community, learning about other cultures through the Internet probably will not provide emotional connection to those cultures.** — Correct (84% of students)
This matches the conclusion: learning about other cultures through the Internet probably will not satisfy the desire for emotional connection to a global community. It correctly identifies the claim between "however" and "for."

**E. When people long for connection to a global human community, they tend to engage in behavior that results in the acquisition of knowledge about other cultures but rarely produces genuine emotional connection to those cultures.** — Too Broad (11% of students)
The argument is specifically about the Internet, but this answer broadens to any behavior producing cultural knowledge. The reasoning depends on the Internet's specific limitations, so expanding beyond it removes the logical basis.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Ideas:** People want to feel emotionally connected to the world, so they hop on the Internet and start reading about other cultures. A reasonable strategy, it would seem.
- **Conclusion:** "However" — the columnist's favorite word for "nice try, but no." Learning about other cultures is probably not going to scratch that emotional itch.
- **Evidence (for):** The Internet is basically a giant information vending machine. People want feelings of interconnectedness, but the Internet dispenses facts and data. Ordering an encyclopedia when you wanted a hug.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that says "cultural learning via the Internet will not satisfy the emotional longing." Not the setup, not the evidence — the actual point.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is common for people who have access to the Internet to express a longing for emotional connection to an international human community.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This is the setup, not the punchline. The columnist starts by describing how people want emotional connection — that is the "once upon a time" of this argument. The conclusion is the "but they are not going to get it this way" part. Picking the opening context as the conclusion is like identifying the problem statement in a research paper as the thesis.

**B. The longing for emotional connection to a global human community frequently leads people to use the Internet in order to acquire information about other cultures.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
People feel lonely, so they go online and learn about other cultures. That is the story so far — the behavior the columnist is reacting to. The columnist's actual point comes after "however": that strategy probably will not work. This answer just describes what people are doing; the conclusion says it is not going to help.

**C. People who have access to the Internet tend to use it primarily for acquiring information and only secondarily for developing feelings of interconnectedness.** — Premise (2% of students)
The word "for" is doing its job here as a giant neon sign reading "THIS IS THE REASON." The Internet is mainly about information, not feelings — that is why the columnist thinks learning about cultures online will not work. But "why X is true" is different from "X." This answer is the supporting act; the conclusion is the headliner.

**D. For people desiring connection to a global human community, learning about other cultures through the Internet probably will not provide emotional connection to those cultures.** — Correct (84% of students)
This is the columnist's actual point, accurately paraphrased. People want emotional connection, they try to get it by learning about cultures online, and the columnist says: probably not going to work. This answer captures that verdict without broadening it into a universal claim about human behavior or narrowing it into just the evidence. It sits right in the sweet spot between the "however" and the "for" — exactly where conclusions in this argument pattern always live.

**E. When people long for connection to a global human community, they tend to engage in behavior that results in the acquisition of knowledge about other cultures but rarely produces genuine emotional connection to those cultures.** — Too Broad (11% of students)
The columnist is talking about the Internet. This answer is talking about every possible way a person could learn about another culture — reading, traveling, taking a class, watching documentaries, you name it. The argument works because the Internet has a specific limitation: it is mainly for information, not feelings. Take away that specific limitation and the argument falls apart. A person who moves to another country and immerses themselves in the culture might develop plenty of emotional connection. The columnist's argument is about one tool's inadequacy, not a universal truth about cultural learning.

---

## S2 · Q6 — Most Supported

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Democratic societies in which there is widespread discontent more often blame their politicians than they do other powerful figures who are at least as responsible for those societies’ woes. This is not primarily because politicians are more familiar to people than are other powerful figures; rather it is because people in democratic societies feel that they have more power over politicians than they have over other powerful figures.

### Prompt
The case described above best illustrates which one of the following generalizations?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Democratic societies in which there is widespread discontent more often blame their politicians than they do other powerful figures who are at least as responsible for those societies’ woes.
- **Conclusion:** This [blaming of politicians] is not primarily because politicians are more familiar to people than are other powerful figures. People blame politicians because they feel that they have more power over politicians than they have over other powerful figures.
- **Evaluate:** Both of these explanations in the conclusion—politicians being more familiar to people, or the feeling of having more power over politicians—seem equally plausible. Why is one a better explanation than the other?
- **Goal:** We're looking for a generalization (assumption) indicating that having more power over politicians is more likely to lead us to blame them, or one indicating that being familiar isn't what makes us blame them.

**Answer choices**
**A. Often the people who have the most power in a society are not the people who are generally perceived by the public as having the most power.** — Unsupported Distinction (4% of students)
This answer discusses the difference between who actually has the most power and who is perceived to have the most power. The stimulus is not about perceptions of how much power someone holds — it is about how much power people feel they have over that person. These are different concepts. Believing a politician has a lot of power is different from believing you have power over a politician. The case in the stimulus illustrates the latter, not the former.

**B. People tend to ascribe more power to a person with whom they are familiar than to one with whom they are unfamiliar.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
This answer says people ascribe more power to familiar people than to unfamiliar ones. The stimulus is about the relationship between feeling power over someone and blaming them, not about how familiarity affects power perception. The stimulus explicitly rejects familiarity as the primary explanation for blame patterns. This answer reintroduces familiarity as a relevant variable in a way the stimulus does not support and connects it to a concept (ascribing power) that is not part of the argument's framework.

**C. To the extent that a person is well known to the public, that person is more likely to be blamed for the woes of society.** — Opposite (2% of students)
This answer says that the more well-known a person is, the more likely they are to be blamed. But the stimulus explicitly rejects familiarity as the primary reason for disproportionate blame. The stimulus argues that people blame politicians more because they feel they have power over politicians, not because politicians are more familiar. This answer endorses the explanation the stimulus dismisses and ignores the one it accepts. It captures the opposite of the argument's conclusion.

**D. Publicly known people are usually not held sufficiently responsible for the problems they cause.** — Out of Scope: sufficiently responsible (1% of students)
This answer discusses whether publicly known people are held "sufficiently responsible" for problems they cause. The stimulus does not address whether the level of blame directed at politicians is appropriate, excessive, or insufficient. The argument explains why politicians receive disproportionate blame compared to other powerful figures, not whether anyone is held sufficiently responsible. The concept of sufficient responsibility is an evaluative judgment that goes beyond the descriptive and explanatory framework of the stimulus.

**E. People are more inclined to blame a publicly known person if that person is someone over whom they feel they have power.** — Correct (90% of students)
This answer captures the generalization the case illustrates. The stimulus shows that people in democracies blame politicians more than other equally responsible figures because they feel they have power over politicians. Generalized: people are more inclined to blame a publicly known person when they feel they have power over that person. This correctly identifies perceived power as the variable that drives blame, which is exactly the causal mechanism the stimulus endorses. The answer uses "publicly known person" (broad enough to cover politicians and other public figures) and "someone over whom they feel they have power" (the specific mechanism the argument identifies).

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Discontented democratic societies blame politicians more than other equally responsible powerful figures.
- **Conclusion:** This happens not because politicians are more familiar but because people feel they have more power over politicians than over other powerful figures.
- **Evaluate:** The argument identifies two possible explanations (familiarity vs. perceived power) and endorses perceived power as the primary one.
- **Goal:** Find a generalization connecting perceived power over someone to willingness to blame them.

**Answer choices**
**A. Often the people who have the most power in a society are not the people who are generally perceived by the public as having the most power.** — Unsupported Distinction (4% of students)
This is about perceived power of a person, not perceived power over a person. The stimulus concerns feeling you can influence politicians, not judging how powerful they are.

**B. People tend to ascribe more power to a person with whom they are familiar than to one with whom they are unfamiliar.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
The stimulus rejects familiarity as the primary explanation. This answer links familiarity to power attribution — a connection the stimulus does not make and a concept it does not address.

**C. To the extent that a person is well known to the public, that person is more likely to be blamed for the woes of society.** — Opposite (2% of students)
The stimulus explicitly rejects familiarity as the primary driver of blame. This answer endorses familiarity as the driver — the opposite of the argument's point.

**D. Publicly known people are usually not held sufficiently responsible for the problems they cause.** — Out of Scope: sufficiently responsible (1% of students)
The stimulus explains why politicians get disproportionate blame, not whether anyone is held sufficiently responsible. "Sufficiently responsible" introduces an evaluative concept the argument does not address.

**E. People are more inclined to blame a publicly known person if that person is someone over whom they feel they have power.** — Correct (90% of students)
The stimulus shows that perceived power over someone drives blame. This generalizes: people are more inclined to blame public figures they feel they can influence. It correctly captures the causal mechanism the argument endorses.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** When things go wrong in a democracy, politicians catch most of the flak — even though other powerful people are equally responsible.
- **Conclusion:** The argument says this is not because politicians are more familiar faces. It is because people in democracies feel they actually have some leverage over politicians. After all, politicians need votes.
- **Evaluate:** Two suspects are on the board: familiarity and perceived power. The argument eliminates familiarity and pins it on perceived power. The general principle: people are more willing to blame someone they feel they can influence.
- **Goal:** Find the generalization that captures "perceived power over someone leads to blaming them more."

**Answer choices**
**A. Often the people who have the most power in a society are not the people who are generally perceived by the public as having the most power.** — Unsupported Distinction (4% of students)
This answer is about misjudging who the most powerful people are. The stimulus is about blaming the people you feel you have leverage over. Those are two different things entirely. Thinking your senator is powerful is not the same as thinking you can vote your senator out of office. The stimulus is about the second thing, and this answer is about the first.

**B. People tend to ascribe more power to a person with whom they are familiar than to one with whom they are unfamiliar.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
This answer says familiarity makes people think someone is powerful. But the stimulus said familiarity is NOT the reason people blame politicians — feeling power over them is. This answer brings familiarity back from the dead and connects it to a concept (how powerful someone seems) that the stimulus never discusses. Double miss: wrong cause, wrong effect.

**C. To the extent that a person is well known to the public, that person is more likely to be blamed for the woes of society.** — Opposite (2% of students)
The stimulus spent two sentences making this exact point and then immediately said "nope, that is not the reason." Familiarity is the rejected explanation. Perceived power over the target is the accepted explanation. This answer picks up the rejected explanation, dusts it off, and presents it as the generalization. That is the argumentative equivalent of choosing the answer the test explicitly told you was wrong.

**D. Publicly known people are usually not held sufficiently responsible for the problems they cause.** — Out of Scope: sufficiently responsible (1% of students)
The stimulus explains why politicians get blamed more — not whether they are blamed enough, or whether anyone else is blamed enough. "Sufficiently responsible" is a judgment call the argument never makes. It is like explaining why some restaurants get more Yelp reviews and being asked whether the reviews are fair. Different question.

**E. People are more inclined to blame a publicly known person if that person is someone over whom they feel they have power.** — Correct (90% of students)
This is the general rule hiding behind the specific example. Politicians get blamed more because people feel they have leverage over politicians — generalized, people blame the public figures they feel they can push around. Not the ones they know best (that was rejected), not the ones they think are most powerful (that was never raised), but the ones they feel they have power over. This answer extracts exactly the right principle from the case study.

---

## S2 · Q7 — Most Supported

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
The mu mesons generated by cosmic rays just outside Earth's atmosphere travel to Earth at speeds approaching the speed of light. Mu mesons generated in the laboratory, however, are nearly at rest. Mu mesons generated in the laboratory typically decay in much less time than it takes for a mu meson to travel from just outside Earth's atmosphere to our detection apparatus on Earth. If mu mesons traveling through the atmosphere at speeds approaching the speed of light typically decay as fast as they do in the laboratory, then we should detect only about one one-hundredth of the number we actually do detect. Apparently, mu mesons moving at speeds near the speed of light _______.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most logically completes the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:**
  - *contrast:* Mm's generated by cosmic rays just outside Earth's atmosphere travel at speeds approaching the speed of light, **whereas** mm's in the lab are nearly at rest.
  - *math-y contrast:* Time it takes for lab-mm to decay < time it takes for space mm to reach our detection apparatus.
  - *conditional + **implicit fact** that triggers contrapositive:* If mm's traveling through the atmosphere at speeds approaching the speed of light typically decay as fast as lab-mm's, then we should detect only about one one-hundredth of the number **we actually do detect.**
- **Evaluate:** It's helpful to be clear about the information we've been given and how the different statements connect to one another. We're told that mu mesons—let's just call those MMs—are created just outside of Earth's atmosphere and travel to Earth at close to the speed of light. That's fast. We know something about the speed of MMs created in the laboratory: they're nearly at rest—so, almost not moving. We're told that these resting MMs in the lab typically decay faster than the time it takes for an MM to travel from outside the atmosphere to a detector on Earth. If you notice some connections between these statements, that's great. But there's something very interesting to notice about the fourth sentence. It contains a conditional statement: "If mu mesons traveling through the atmosphere at speeds approaching the speed of light typically decay as fast as they do in the laboratory, then we should detect only about one one-hundredth of the number we actually do detect." This statement is already telling us that the necessary condition—the "then" part—is not true. We are not detecting one one-hundredth of the number we actually do detect. We are detecting the number that we actually do detect. Think about the contrapositive: If we don't detect one one-hundredth of the number that we actually do detect, then MMs traveling through the atmosphere do not decay as fast as they do in the laboratory. The sufficient condition—the "if" part—is automatically triggered. We don't detect one-one hundredth of the number that we actually detect. We detect the number that we actually detect. This means that the necessary condition—the "then" part—must be true. MMs traveling through the atmosphere do not decay as fast as they do in the laboratory. The final sentence in the stimulus, the one we need to complete, mentions MMs "moving at speeds near the speed of light." Those are the ones in the atmosphere. We can expect the rest of this sentence to state that they don't decay as fast as ones in the laboratory.
- **Goal:** If we can look past the scientific terms and get a handle on the essence of what these statements are telling us, we can make a very good prediction. We expect a correct answer to indicate that MMs traveling through the atmosphere decay more slowly that ones created in the lab.
- **Helpful Tip:** Don't let the LSAT distract or intimidate you with fancy science terms. You don't need to know what a mu meson is. It's a thing. You don't need to know the exact speed of light. It's fast. We don't even need to know exactly what "decay" means in this context. Whatever it is, we're told it's something that can happen at different speeds. That's all we really need to understand. You could literally replace "decay" with "change color" or "turn into elephants" and still get the correct answer. Try it. The science might not make sense, but the logical connections would stay the same. And we're being tested on our understanding of the logical connections, not the science.

**Answer choices**
**A. take longer to reach Earth than was supposed** — Opposite (if anything) (5% of students)
The evidence does not support the idea that mu mesons take longer to reach Earth than expected. The problem the stimulus presents is not about travel time — it is about survival time. We detect far more atmospheric mu mesons than the lab decay rate predicts, meaning they survive longer than expected, not that they travel slower. If they took longer to reach Earth, we would expect to detect even fewer, not more. This answer moves in the wrong direction.

**B. are quite difficult to detect with available equipment** — Out of Scope: difficult (7% of students)
The stimulus is about the number of mu mesons we detect relative to expectations, not about the difficulty of detection. The evidence discusses decay rates, travel speeds, and detection counts. Whether mu mesons are easy or hard to detect with available equipment is a completely separate issue. Even if detection were difficult, that would not explain why we detect more mu mesons than the lab-based predictions indicate — difficulty would suggest fewer detections, not more.

**C. are much less numerous than previously thought** — Opposite (if anything) (9% of students)
This answer suggests there are fewer atmospheric mu mesons than previously thought. But the puzzle is that we detect far more than predicted by lab-based decay rates. If mu mesons were less numerous, that would make the detection surplus even more surprising — we detect 100 times what decay rates predict, and there are even fewer of them to begin with? That deepens the mystery rather than resolving it. Additionally, the evidence is about decay rates and speed, not about how many mu mesons are generated. The conclusion should address why mu mesons survive longer at high speed.

**D. decay more slowly than mu mesons almost at rest** — Correct (77% of students)
This is the conclusion the evidence supports. Atmospheric mu mesons travel near the speed of light and are detected in far greater numbers than lab-based decay rates predict. Lab mu mesons are nearly at rest and decay quickly. The only way to reconcile the detection data with the decay data is to conclude that mu mesons moving at high speed decay more slowly than those at rest. The contrapositive confirms this: if atmospheric mu mesons decayed as fast as lab ones, we would detect only 1/100 of the actual number. Since we detect far more, they must not decay as fast. The distinguishing variable between the two groups is speed, so the conclusion links high speed to slower decay.

**E. are probably not generated by cosmic rays** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The very first sentence of the stimulus states that mu mesons are "generated by cosmic rays just outside Earth's atmosphere." Nothing in the subsequent evidence undermines this statement. The information about decay rates and detection counts has no bearing on how mu mesons are generated — it only addresses how quickly they decay at different speeds. This answer contradicts an explicit premise without any supporting evidence.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Atmospheric mu mesons travel near the speed of light; lab mu mesons are nearly at rest. Lab mu mesons decay faster than it takes atmospheric ones to reach detectors. If atmospheric mu mesons decayed as fast as lab ones, we would detect only 1/100 of what we actually detect.
- **Evaluate:** We detect far more atmospheric mu mesons than the lab decay rate predicts. The contrapositive tells us atmospheric mu mesons must not decay as fast as lab ones.
- **Goal:** The conclusion should state that mu mesons moving near the speed of light decay more slowly than those at rest.

**Answer choices**
**A. take longer to reach Earth than was supposed** — Opposite (if anything) (5% of students)
The problem is that mu mesons survive longer than expected, not that they travel slower. Taking longer to reach Earth would mean detecting fewer, not the observed surplus.

**B. are quite difficult to detect with available equipment** — Out of Scope: difficult (7% of students)
Detection difficulty is not discussed. The issue is that we detect more mu mesons than expected based on lab decay rates, and detection difficulty would not explain that surplus.

**C. are much less numerous than previously thought** — Opposite (if anything) (9% of students)
We detect more mu mesons than expected. Saying they are less numerous makes the surplus harder to explain, not easier. The issue is survival rate, not population size.

**D. decay more slowly than mu mesons almost at rest** — Correct (77% of students)
We detect 100 times more atmospheric mu mesons than lab decay rates predict. Since the only difference is speed, fast-moving mu mesons must decay more slowly than nearly stationary ones. The contrapositive confirms this conclusion.

**E. are probably not generated by cosmic rays** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The first sentence explicitly states mu mesons are generated by cosmic rays. Nothing in the argument contradicts or undermines this. The evidence about decay rates has no bearing on origin.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Mu mesons from space are speed demons — nearly light speed. Mu mesons in the lab are couch potatoes — barely moving. Lab mu mesons fall apart very quickly. Based on that decay rate, almost none of the space ones should reach Earth. But the detectors catch way more than expected — about 100 times more.
- **Evaluate:** The stimulus is basically describing time dilation from Einstein's relativity (though no one needs to know that for the LSAT). The math does not add up unless the space mu mesons are living longer than the lab ones. The only difference between them? Speed. The fast ones seem to decay slower. The contrapositive seals it: if they decayed at the lab rate, barely any would be detected. Yet plenty arrive. So they do not decay at the lab rate.
- **Goal:** The answer should say mu mesons moving fast decay slower than ones sitting still. Do not get intimidated by the physics vocabulary — "mu meson" is just a thing, "decay" is just a process, and speed is the variable.

**Answer choices**
**A. take longer to reach Earth than was supposed** — Opposite (if anything) (5% of students)
If mu mesons took longer to reach Earth, that would make the problem worse, not better. More travel time means more time to decay means fewer mu mesons reaching the detectors. But the detectors are picking up way more than expected. Saying they take longer to arrive is like explaining a surprisingly full restaurant by saying the food takes longer to cook. Wrong direction entirely.

**B. are quite difficult to detect with available equipment** — Out of Scope: difficult (7% of students)
The detectors are catching 100 times more mu mesons than expected, and this answer suggests maybe they are hard to detect? If they were hard to detect, the count would be lower, not higher. The puzzle is an embarrassment of riches, not a detection problem. And the stimulus never discusses equipment limitations anyway — it is purely about decay rates and counts.

**C. are much less numerous than previously thought** — Opposite (if anything) (9% of students)
The stimulus says the detectors are catching way more mu mesons than expected, and this answer says there are actually fewer of them out there? That makes the mystery bigger, not smaller. It is like solving "how did you eat 100 cookies?" with "actually, there were only 50 cookies in the jar." That does not help. The real explanation has to address why these things are surviving longer than lab rates predict.

**D. decay more slowly than mu mesons almost at rest** — Correct (77% of students)
This is the answer the evidence has been building toward. The space mu mesons travel fast and the detectors catch way more of them than expected. The lab mu mesons sit still and decay quickly. If the space ones decayed at the same rate, barely any would reach the detectors. But tons arrive. The only variable that is different is speed. Therefore: going fast makes you decay slower. Einstein would be proud — though for LSAT purposes, all that matters is following the logical chain to its inevitable conclusion.

**E. are probably not generated by cosmic rays** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The first sentence literally says mu mesons are generated by cosmic rays. Nothing that follows even hints otherwise. The argument is about how fast they decay, not where they come from. This answer tries to rewrite the first line of the story in the last chapter — and nothing in the plot supports the revision.

## S2 · Q8 — Parallel

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Buyer: As a buyer for a large chain of department stores, I will buy a garment only if it is fashionable and not too expensive for our clientele. The evening dress from the fall collection by Peruka is certainly fashionable, but it is far too expensive for our clientele. Therefore, I will not buy that dress.

### Prompt
The pattern of reasoning in which one of the following arguments most closely resembles the pattern of reasoning in the buyer's argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The buyer will buy a garment only if it is fashionable and not too expensive for our clientele. The evening dress from the fall collection by Peruka is certainly fashionable, but the dress is far too expensive for our clientele.
```
BG → F and ~TE
F
TE
```
- **Conclusion:** The buyer will not buy that dress [garment].
```
~BG
```
- **Evaluate:** This is a valid argument. The first premise has two necessary conditions that both must be met in order to buy a garment. One of these necessary conditions will be met but the other will not. Based on this, the conclusion states that the evening dress [a garment] will not be bought. It can also help to look at the contrapositive of the first premise: If a garment is not fashionable or it is too expensive then the buyer will not buy the garment.
```
~F or TE → ~BG
```
The third premise triggers one of these sufficient conditions (TE), so we can conclude that the buyer will not buy this garment (~BG).
- **Goal:** A correct answer will probably use this same type of contrapositive logic to reach its conclusion. In all likelihood, it will also have the other key features of this original argument: a compound necessary condition in which one item is fulfilled and the other is not.

**Answer choices**
**A. A snowflake will melt if it is in a warm place and not protected by insulation. This snowflake is in a warm place, but it is protected by insulation. Therefore, this snowflake will not melt.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (16% of students)
This answer places the compound condition in the sufficient position ("if warm AND not insulated, then melts"), not the necessary position. In the original, the compound condition is necessary (buy only if fashionable AND affordable). Additionally, this answer concludes the snowflake will not melt because it is insulated, which constitutes denying part of the sufficient condition — an invalid inference (illegal negation). The original argument validly denies one of the necessary conditions. Both the structural placement of the compound condition and the validity of the reasoning differ from the original.

**B. A stuffed animal, in order to pass inspection, must be free of sharp edges and completely sealed. This stuffed hippo is free of sharp edges, but it is not completely sealed. Therefore, this hippo will not pass inspection.** — Correct (79% of students)
This answer perfectly replicates the original's structure. The first premise establishes a compound necessary condition: to pass inspection, a stuffed animal must be free of sharp edges AND completely sealed (Pass -> Free of sharp edges AND Sealed). The second premise states the hippo is free of sharp edges (one necessary condition met) but not completely sealed (other necessary condition unmet). The conclusion: the hippo will not pass inspection. Via the contrapositive: Not free of sharp edges OR Not sealed -> Not pass. Since the hippo is not sealed, the contrapositive is triggered, and the conclusion follows validly. Every structural element matches: compound necessary condition, one met and one unmet, valid denial.

**C. A sidewalk is accessible if its edges are fully graded and if it has no sizable bumps. This sidewalk's edges are fully graded, and it has no sizable bumps. Therefore, this sidewalk is accessible.** — Bad Evidence Match (1% of students)
This answer has a compound condition in the sufficient position ("if fully graded AND no bumps, then accessible"), and both conditions are met, leading to a conclusion that the sidewalk is accessible. The original has a compound necessary condition with one condition unmet. Here, both are met and the conclusion follows by affirming the sufficient condition — a valid inference, but a completely different logical pattern. The original concludes something will not happen because a necessary condition is unmet; this answer concludes something will happen because all sufficient conditions are met.

**D. A poetic translation is accurate only if it adequately expresses the meaning of the original poem and gives an idea of the meter. This translation adequately expresses the meaning of the original and gives an idea of the meter. Therefore, this translation is accurate.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (3% of students)
This answer has the compound condition in the necessary position (accurate only if expresses meaning AND gives idea of meter), matching the original structurally. However, both necessary conditions are met, and the conclusion states the translation is accurate. This is an illegal reversal — affirming the necessary condition does not establish the sufficient condition. In the original, one necessary condition was unmet and the conclusion validly denied the sufficient condition. Here, both are met and the conclusion invalidly affirms it. The evidence pattern (both conditions met vs. one unmet) and the validity (invalid vs. valid) both differ.

**E. An assembly may call a meeting of the executive board only if the assembly meets quorum requirements and the motion passes by two-thirds. This assembly meets quorum requirements, so unless the motion does not pass by two-thirds, this assembly may call a meeting of the executive board.** — Bad Evidence/Conclusion/Validity Match (1% of students)
Multiple mismatches disqualify this answer. First, only one necessary condition is confirmed (meets quorum requirements), while the status of the other (two-thirds vote) is left open — unlike the original, where one is confirmed and one is definitively denied. Second, the conclusion is a conditional statement ("unless the motion does not pass by two-thirds, this assembly may call a meeting"), whereas the original's conclusion is a simple categorical statement. Third, the conclusion commits an illegal reversal: from ACM -> Q AND MP2/3, plus Q, it concludes MP2/3 -> ACM. The structure, conclusion form, and validity all differ from the original.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The buyer will buy a garment only if it is fashionable and not too expensive. The dress is fashionable but too expensive.
- **Conclusion:** The buyer will not buy the dress.
- **Evaluate:** Valid reasoning: the first premise has two necessary conditions (fashionable AND not too expensive). One is met, one is not. Via the contrapositive, if either necessary condition is unmet, the garment will not be bought.
- **Goal:** Match the pattern: compound necessary condition, one met and one unmet, valid denial of the sufficient condition.

**Answer choices**
**A. A snowflake will melt if it is in a warm place and not protected by insulation. This snowflake is in a warm place, but it is protected by insulation. Therefore, this snowflake will not melt.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (16% of students)
The compound condition is in the sufficient position (if X and Y, then Z), not the necessary position as in the original. The conclusion also relies on an illegal negation — an invalid inference, unlike the original's valid reasoning.

**B. A stuffed animal, in order to pass inspection, must be free of sharp edges and completely sealed. This stuffed hippo is free of sharp edges, but it is not completely sealed. Therefore, this hippo will not pass inspection.** — Correct (79% of students)
Perfect match. Compound necessary condition (free of sharp edges AND sealed), one met and one not, valid conclusion that the hippo will not pass inspection. Identical structure and valid reasoning.

**C. A sidewalk is accessible if its edges are fully graded and if it has no sizable bumps. This sidewalk's edges are fully graded, and it has no sizable bumps. Therefore, this sidewalk is accessible.** — Bad Evidence Match (1% of students)
Both conditions are met here, leading to a positive conclusion. The original has one condition unmet with a negative conclusion. Different pattern entirely.

**D. A poetic translation is accurate only if it adequately expresses the meaning of the original poem and gives an idea of the meter. This translation adequately expresses the meaning of the original and gives an idea of the meter. Therefore, this translation is accurate.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (3% of students)
Both necessary conditions are met and the conclusion affirms accuracy — an illegal reversal. The original had one condition unmet and validly denied the outcome. Different evidence pattern and different validity.

**E. An assembly may call a meeting of the executive board only if the assembly meets quorum requirements and the motion passes by two-thirds. This assembly meets quorum requirements, so unless the motion does not pass by two-thirds, this assembly may call a meeting of the executive board.** — Bad Evidence/Conclusion/Validity Match (1% of students)
Only one condition is addressed, the conclusion is conditional rather than categorical, and the reasoning relies on an illegal reversal. Multiple structural elements fail to match the original.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The buyer has two non-negotiable requirements: fashionable AND affordable. The dress passes the fashion test with flying colors but spectacularly fails the price test.
- **Conclusion:** No purchase. If you need both conditions met and one fails, the deal is off.
- **Evaluate:** This is clean contrapositive logic. Buy -> Fashionable AND Not Expensive. The dress is expensive, so: Expensive -> Not Buy. One out of two is not good enough when the original statement requires both. The correct parallel answer needs to copy this exact blueprint: two requirements, one met, one failed, valid rejection.
- **Goal:** Find the answer with the same DNA: compound necessary condition, split outcome, valid conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. A snowflake will melt if it is in a warm place and not protected by insulation. This snowflake is in a warm place, but it is protected by insulation. Therefore, this snowflake will not melt.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (16% of students)
Spot the difference: the original says "buy ONLY IF fashionable and affordable" (compound necessary condition). This answer says "IF warm and uninsulated THEN melts" (compound sufficient condition). Those are structurally different. Then it gets worse: the conclusion says the snowflake will not melt because it is insulated, which is like saying "it is warm but insulated, so no melting." That is denying the antecedent — an invalid move. The original's reasoning was valid. Wrong structure, wrong logic.

**B. A stuffed animal, in order to pass inspection, must be free of sharp edges and completely sealed. This stuffed hippo is free of sharp edges, but it is not completely sealed. Therefore, this hippo will not pass inspection.** — Correct (79% of students)
This is the original argument wearing a stuffed hippo costume. Two requirements to pass (sharp-edge-free AND sealed), one passes and one fails, hippo does not pass inspection. The buyer had two requirements (fashionable AND affordable), one passed and one failed, dress does not get bought. Same structure, same logic, same validity. If you diagram both, they are identical except for the nouns.

**C. A sidewalk is accessible if its edges are fully graded and if it has no sizable bumps. This sidewalk's edges are fully graded, and it has no sizable bumps. Therefore, this sidewalk is accessible.** — Bad Evidence Match (1% of students)
Everything checks out in this answer — both conditions are met, sidewalk is accessible. That is the opposite situation from the original, where one condition fails and the conclusion is negative. The original is a story about rejection; this is a story about approval. They use different logical moves to reach opposite types of conclusions.

**D. A poetic translation is accurate only if it adequately expresses the meaning of the original poem and gives an idea of the meter. This translation adequately expresses the meaning of the original and gives an idea of the meter. Therefore, this translation is accurate.** — Bad Evidence/Validity Match (3% of students)
This looks similar on the surface — "only if" with two conditions, just like the original. But look closer: both conditions are met, and the conclusion says the translation is accurate. That is like saying "you need a passport AND a visa to enter the country; I have both; therefore I will enter." Having both necessary conditions does not guarantee the outcome. The original had one condition fail and correctly concluded the outcome would not happen. This one has both conditions pass and incorrectly concludes it will. Same-looking setup, completely different logical move.

**E. An assembly may call a meeting of the executive board only if the assembly meets quorum requirements and the motion passes by two-thirds. This assembly meets quorum requirements, so unless the motion does not pass by two-thirds, this assembly may call a meeting of the executive board.** — Bad Evidence/Conclusion/Validity Match (1% of students)
This answer has problems on multiple levels. One condition is confirmed, but the other is left hanging with an "unless" — unlike the original, where one clearly passes and one clearly fails. The conclusion is a conditional ("unless X, then Y") instead of a flat statement. And the logic is an illegal reversal. The original was a clean, valid, categorical argument. This is a conditional, invalid, incomplete mess. Not even close.

## S2 · Q9 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Psychologists report that children in nine-month schools typically forget a significant amount of schooling during summer breaks. So, some educators have proposed a twelve-month schedule in which there are three month-long breaks spread throughout the year. We should conclude, on the basis of the psychologists' research, that the twelve-month schedule is to be preferred insofar as academic learning is concerned, since this schedule will insure that students will not forget their schooling during their breaks.

### Prompt
The reasoning above is most vulnerable to the criticism that it

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Psychologists report that children in nine-month schools typically forget a significant amount of schooling during summer breaks.
- **Conclusion (We should conclude):** The twelve-month schedule is to be preferred insofar as academic learning is concerned.
- **Intermediate Conclusion:** [The twelve-month schedule with three month-long breaks] will insure that students will not forget their schooling during their breaks.
- **Evaluate:** This argument compares one plan [12-month schedule] to another [9-month schedule] and states that the 12-month schedule is preferable. We should consider factors that might have been left out of this comparison. For example, what if a longer, 12-month schedule with three month-long breaks will somehow make students even more forgetful? The argument claims that the 12-month schedule "will insure" that students will not forget their schooling during breaks. But that's a prediction about the future. That's what makes it an intermediate conclusion, not just a premise. It's a claim that might or might not be true. The conclusion is about "academic learning," not just remembering or forgetting. Could the 12-month plan have some unintended negative consequences that would make it worse for learning overall, even if it was better for retention? Comparative arguments frequently overlook these types of potential objections. They'll tell us that one of two options has a certain good quality. Based on that, they'll conclude that this option is better overall. It's a classic example of an "incomplete comparison."
- **Goal:** A correct answer might describe an objection like the ones we've considered here, pointing out that the argument has "failed to consider" or "overlooked" this possible objection. Beware of answers that describe something which isn't actually happening in the argument, even if it seems related at first glance.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies on an unsubstantiated assumption about the comparative worth of academic and nonacademic learning experiences** — Irrelevant Comparison (5% of students)
The argument's assumptions are about whether students will forget schooling during breaks, not about the comparative value of academic vs. nonacademic learning. The argument explicitly states its conclusion applies "insofar as academic learning is concerned," so it is already limiting itself to academic considerations. An assumption about the relative worth of academic and nonacademic experiences is not what makes this argument vulnerable.

**B. draws on an arbitrary distinction between two groups** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
This answer describes an arbitrary distinction between two groups. But the argument compares two scheduling plans, not two groups of students. No actual group of twelve-month-schedule students is mentioned — the argument is making predictions about a proposed schedule. Furthermore, the distinction between the two schedules (nine months with summer vs. twelve months with shorter breaks) is not "arbitrary" — it is the substantive basis for the comparison. The argument's flaw is not that it makes an arbitrary distinction but that it assumes an unproven advantage for one of the compared options.

**C. takes for granted, in comparing two situations, that a certain undesirable result is correlated with only one of them** — Correct (80% of students)
This precisely describes the argument's flaw. The argument identifies an undesirable result — students forgetting their schooling during breaks — and presents evidence that this result occurs in the nine-month schedule. It then assumes this result is correlated only with the nine-month schedule, concluding that the twelve-month schedule will prevent forgetting. But no evidence is offered about whether students in twelve-month programs also forget during their month-long breaks. The argument "takes for granted" that the negative outcome is unique to one option when it could easily affect both. This is the incomplete-comparison flaw at the heart of the argument.

**D. fails to show that the data on which the psychologists' conclusions were based was adequately representative of children in the population as a whole** — Bad Evidence Match / Sampling (12% of students)
This answer suggests the data on nine-month schools might not be representative of children generally. But the argument does not need the data to be representative of all children — it only needs the data to be accurate about children in nine-month schools, which is what the psychologists studied. Furthermore, the argument's flaw is not about the quality of the evidence about nine-month schools; it is about the absence of evidence about twelve-month schools. The data could be perfectly representative, and the argument would still be flawed for assuming the twelve-month schedule avoids the forgetting problem.

**E. claims to accept a view, but then rejects it in the course of argument** — Self-Contradiction (1% of students)
This answer describes a self-contradiction flaw: accepting a view and then rejecting it. No such contradiction exists in this argument. The argument presents the psychologists' finding about nine-month schools and uses it as evidence — it never rejects or contradicts it. The argument consistently treats the finding as reliable and builds upon it. If anything, the problem is that the argument over-relies on the finding by applying it selectively to one schedule while assuming the other is immune.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Children in nine-month schools typically forget a significant amount during summer breaks.
- **Conclusion:** The twelve-month schedule (with three month-long breaks) is preferable for academic learning.
- **Intermediate Conclusion:** The twelve-month schedule will ensure students do not forget their schooling during breaks.
- **Evaluate:** The argument assumes the forgetting problem is unique to the nine-month schedule. But students in a twelve-month schedule also have month-long breaks, and might forget schooling during those breaks too.
- **Goal:** Find the answer describing the flaw of assuming forgetting is only associated with one schedule when it could affect both.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies on an unsubstantiated assumption about the comparative worth of academic and nonacademic learning experiences** — Irrelevant Comparison (5% of students)
The argument limits itself to academic learning and never compares academic to nonacademic learning experiences. The flaw is about assuming forgetting is unique to one schedule, not about valuing different types of learning.

**B. draws on an arbitrary distinction between two groups** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
The argument compares two plans, not two groups. No twelve-month students are discussed. And the distinction between schedules is substantive, not arbitrary — the flaw is assuming one plan is problem-free without evidence.

**C. takes for granted, in comparing two situations, that a certain undesirable result is correlated with only one of them** — Correct (80% of students)
The argument shows forgetting occurs in nine-month schedules and assumes it will not occur in twelve-month schedules. No evidence is provided for the latter claim. The flaw is assuming the problem is unique to one schedule.

**D. fails to show that the data on which the psychologists' conclusions were based was adequately representative of children in the population as a whole** — Bad Evidence Match / Sampling (12% of students)
The flaw is not about data quality or representativeness. Even perfectly representative data about nine-month schools would not fix the argument — the problem is the absence of any evidence about twelve-month schools.

**E. claims to accept a view, but then rejects it in the course of argument** — Self-Contradiction (1% of students)
The argument never accepts a view and then rejects it. It consistently treats the nine-month-school data as valid evidence. No self-contradiction is present.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Kids in nine-month schools forget stuff over the summer. Psychologists confirmed it.
- **Intermediate Conclusion:** A twelve-month schedule with three month-long breaks will definitely prevent forgetting.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore, the twelve-month schedule is better for learning.
- **Evaluate:** Hold on — why would three month-long breaks be any different from one three-month break? The argument just assumes the forgetting problem magically disappears with a schedule change, without any evidence about what happens during month-long breaks. Maybe students forget during those too. The argument looked at one schedule, found a problem, and assumed the other schedule is problem-free. That is like declaring your new apartment is quieter than your old one without ever actually visiting it.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that calls out this one-sided assumption.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies on an unsubstantiated assumption about the comparative worth of academic and nonacademic learning experiences** — Irrelevant Comparison (5% of students)
The argument already said "insofar as academic learning is concerned" — it is not trying to say anything about summer camp, life skills, or the educational value of catching fireflies. The flaw is not about comparing types of learning; it is about assuming one schedule causes forgetting and the other does not, without evidence for the second claim.

**B. draws on an arbitrary distinction between two groups** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
"Draws on an arbitrary distinction between two groups" — what two groups? There are no two groups of students here. There is one group of students in nine-month schools, and then there is a proposed schedule that nobody has tried yet. And the distinction between a nine-month schedule and a twelve-month schedule is not arbitrary — it is the whole point of the argument. The problem is not that the comparison is unfounded; it is that the comparison is one-sided.

**C. takes for granted, in comparing two situations, that a certain undesirable result is correlated with only one of them** — Correct (80% of students)
The argument found a problem with Schedule A (kids forget during summer break) and declared Schedule B the winner without checking whether Schedule B has the same problem. Three month-long breaks could be three separate forgetting festivals. The argument acts as if the forgetting problem has a personal vendetta against the nine-month schedule and would never dare show up in the twelve-month version. That assumption is completely unsupported, and this answer nails it: the argument treats forgetting as correlated with only one schedule when it could affect both.

**D. fails to show that the data on which the psychologists' conclusions were based was adequately representative of children in the population as a whole** — Bad Evidence Match / Sampling (12% of students)
This answer worries about whether the psychologists studied the right kids. But even if they studied every single child in every single nine-month school and got perfect data, the argument would still be flawed. The problem is not "we do not know enough about nine-month schools" — it is "we do not know anything about twelve-month schools." The answer is looking for a crack in the evidence when the real problem is a missing wall.

**E. claims to accept a view, but then rejects it in the course of argument** — Self-Contradiction (1% of students)
"Accepts a view, then rejects it" — that would require the argument to say "kids forget during breaks" and then later say "actually, no they do not." But the argument never contradicts itself like that. It takes the psychologists' finding at face value and runs with it. The problem is where it runs — straight past any consideration of whether the twelve-month schedule has the same issue. Self-contradiction is not the diagnosis. Selective blindness is.
