## S2 · Q10 — Weaken

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
The Industrial Revolution decreased the value that society conferred on physical labor because it enabled unskilled workers to quickly produce goods that formerly took skilled craftspeople long periods of time to produce. Clearly, our most important intellectual skills will similarly be devalued by electronic data-processing technology. Computations that once took skilled mathematicians a long time to perform can now be quickly performed by moderately well-trained high school students using computers.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The Industrial Revolution decreased the value that society conferred on physical labor because it enabled unskilled workers to quickly produce goods that formerly took skilled craftspeople long periods of time to produce. Computations that once took skilled mathematicians a long time to perform can now be quickly performed by moderately well-trained high school students using computers.
- **Conclusion:** Our most important intellectual skills will be devalued by electronic data-processing technology [similar to the way that the Industrial Revolution decreased the value that society conferred on physical labor].
- **Evaluate:** The argument makes a significant leap by discussing mathematical computations in the evidence and "our most important intellectual skills" in the conclusion. Aren't other types of intellectual skills important? Aren't reading and logical reasoning skills, such as those tested on the LSAT, also important? There might also be important differences between intellectual skills and physical skills, or differences between the Industrial Revolution and our current time, which would prevent us from devaluing intellectual skills.

**Answer choices**
**A. Much industrial machinery is now designed and built with the aid of computers.** — Opposite (1% of students)
This answer states that industrial machinery is now designed with the aid of computers. If anything, this supports the value of computers and data-processing technology, perhaps even strengthening the argument that such technology has significant capabilities. It certainly does not weaken the claim that intellectual skills will be devalued. The argument is about the devaluation of human skills, and showing that computers can also help design machinery only reinforces the trend the argument describes.

**B. Before electronic data-processing technology, improvements in mathematical techniques reduced the amount of time it took to perform computations.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
This answer says that improvements in mathematical techniques had already reduced computation time before computers arrived. The impact on the argument is unclear. Even with these improvements, the argument's evidence — that high school students with computers can now do what formerly required skilled mathematicians — might still hold. Pre-computer improvements in technique do not necessarily change the fact that computers represent a much larger leap in accessibility. This answer does not clearly undermine the argument's key assumption about which intellectual skills society values most.

**C. On average, skilled mathematicians tend to be much younger when they are in their most productive years than are skilled craftspeople.** — Unclear Impact (2% of students)
The age at which skilled mathematicians and craftspeople are most productive has no clear connection to whether intellectual skills will be devalued by technology. The argument is about value that society places on skills, not about when people peak in those skills. Even if mathematicians peak younger, this does not affect whether computer technology will devalue the intellectual skills society considers most important. The relevance of this age comparison to the argument's conclusion is completely unclear.

**D. The intellectual skills that society values most highly are not computational ones.** — Correct (73% of students)
This directly attacks the argument's key assumption — that computational skills are among society's most important intellectual skills. The evidence shows computers can perform computations that once required skilled mathematicians. The conclusion claims "our most important intellectual skills" will be devalued. But if the intellectual skills society values most are not computational ones, then the computer's ability to replace mathematical computation does not threaten those valued skills. The analogy between the Industrial Revolution and computers breaks down precisely at the point that matters: the Industrial Revolution devalued the physical skills that society most relied upon, but computers are devaluing computational skills, which may not be the intellectual skills society values most.

**E. Electronic data-processing technology has enabled people to perform some tasks that previously could not be performed at all.** — Opposite (14% of students)
This answer says that electronic data-processing technology has enabled people to perform tasks that previously could not be performed at all. Rather than weakening the argument, this reinforces the idea that technology is expanding capabilities and potentially replacing human intellectual work. It provides additional evidence for the power and reach of the technology, which, if anything, supports the prediction that intellectual skills will be devalued. A weakening answer needs to show why the prediction is wrong, not why the technology is impressive.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The Industrial Revolution devalued physical labor by enabling unskilled workers to produce goods that formerly required skilled craftspeople. Similarly, computers now let students perform computations that once required skilled mathematicians.
- **Conclusion:** Our most important intellectual skills will be devalued by electronic data-processing technology.
- **Evaluate:** The argument equates mathematical computation with "our most important intellectual skills." But computation is only one type of intellectual skill. Other intellectual skills — critical thinking, reading comprehension, logical reasoning — might not be replicable by computers.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that breaks the link between computational skills and "most important intellectual skills."

**Answer choices**
**A. Much industrial machinery is now designed and built with the aid of computers.** — Opposite (1% of students)
Computers helping design machinery reinforces the power of data-processing technology — strengthening the argument rather than weakening it.

**B. Before electronic data-processing technology, improvements in mathematical techniques reduced the amount of time it took to perform computations.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
Prior improvements in mathematical technique do not clearly undermine the argument. Computers may still represent a much larger accessibility leap, and this answer does not address which intellectual skills are most valued.

**C. On average, skilled mathematicians tend to be much younger when they are in their most productive years than are skilled craftspeople.** — Unclear Impact (2% of students)
The age at which people peak in their skills has no bearing on whether technology will devalue those skills. This comparison is irrelevant to the argument's conclusion.

**D. The intellectual skills that society values most highly are not computational ones.** — Correct (73% of students)
The argument assumes computational skills are our "most important" intellectual skills. If society values non-computational skills most, computers replacing math does not threaten what matters. This breaks the analogy at its crucial point.

**E. Electronic data-processing technology has enabled people to perform some tasks that previously could not be performed at all.** — Opposite (14% of students)
This reinforces the power of technology by showing it enables entirely new tasks. That supports the argument rather than weakening it.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The Industrial Revolution made skilled labor less special because machines let anyone do the job. By the same logic, computers are making math less special because anyone with a laptop can crunch numbers.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore, the most important intellectual skills are doomed to devaluation.
- **Evaluate:** Notice the bait and switch: the evidence is about math, but the conclusion is about "the most important intellectual skills." Since when is arithmetic society's most prized intellectual achievement? Reading, reasoning, critical analysis, creative problem-solving — none of these are threatened by the existence of calculators. The argument assumes that because computers can do math, they will devalue everything intellectual. That is quite the leap.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that says the intellectual skills society values most are not the computational kind.

**Answer choices**
**A. Much industrial machinery is now designed and built with the aid of computers.** — Opposite (1% of students)
Computers help design machinery now? That just makes the argument's point. The argument says technology is replacing human intellectual work, and this answer says "yes, and it is also designing machines." This is like trying to weaken the argument that smartphones are making watches obsolete by pointing out that smartphones now also tell you the weather.

**B. Before electronic data-processing technology, improvements in mathematical techniques reduced the amount of time it took to perform computations.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
Mathematicians got faster at computing before computers came along? Okay, but that does not change the fact that a high schooler with a laptop can now do what a skilled mathematician could not. This answer is like saying "people were already getting around faster before cars were invented, because they bred faster horses." True, but the car still changed everything. And more importantly, this does nothing to address whether math is actually society's most valued intellectual skill.

**C. On average, skilled mathematicians tend to be much younger when they are in their most productive years than are skilled craftspeople.** — Unclear Impact (2% of students)
Mathematicians peak young and craftspeople peak old. Interesting trivia, completely irrelevant argument. The question is whether computers will devalue intellectual skills, not when people are best at those skills. Knowing the average age of peak math productivity reveals nothing about whether society will value math less because computers can do it. This is a fun fact looking for an argument to attach itself to.

**D. The intellectual skills that society values most highly are not computational ones.** — Correct (73% of students)
The argument said "computers can do math, so our most important brainpower is doomed." This answer says "actually, our most important brainpower is not math." The ability to multiply large numbers quickly was never the pinnacle of human intellectual achievement. Reading, reasoning, creative thinking, ethical judgment, strategic analysis — those are the intellectual skills a civilized society tends to value most, and a calculator threatening none of them. The argument mistook one type of intellectual skill for all of them, and this answer calls out that mistake perfectly.

**E. Electronic data-processing technology has enabled people to perform some tasks that previously could not be performed at all.** — Opposite (14% of students)
Computers can now do things humans never could? Great — that makes the argument's case even stronger. The argument says technology will devalue intellectual skills, and this answer says technology is so powerful it is creating entirely new categories of work. That is like trying to argue against the unstoppable march of automation by pointing out that robots can also dance.

## S2 · Q11 — Paradox

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Wounds become infected because the break in the skin allows bacteria to enter. Infection slows healing. Often bacteria-killing ointment is applied to wounds after they have been cleaned, but a study at a Nigerian hospital found that cleaned wounds that were treated with honey—which contains significant quantities of bacteria—healed faster, on average, than both cleaned wounds treated with antibiotic ointment and wounds cleaned but not otherwise treated.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most helps to resolve the apparent discrepancy between the results of the study and the stated facts about wounds, bacteria, infection, and healing?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that...:** Wounds become infected because the break in the skin allows bacteria to enter. Infection slows healing. Honey contains significant quantities of bacteria.
- **How can it be that...:** In a study at a Nigerian hospital, cleaned wounds that were treated with honey healed faster, on average, than both cleaned wounds treated with antibiotic ointment and wounds cleaned but not otherwise treated.
- **Evaluate / Goal:** We need an answer to explain why the bacteria in the honey isn't causing the wounds to heal more slowly, and why the honey actually speeds healing. Maybe the bacteria in honey is different somehow from the bacteria that normally infects wounds. Or maybe something in honey speeds the healing of wounds to a much greater extent than the bacteria slows healing.

**Answer choices**
**A. Wounds that have simply been cleaned with soap and water and not otherwise treated heal faster than wounds that have been cleaned and then treated with antibiotic ointment.** — Irrelevant Comparison (0% of students)
This answer tells us that simply-cleaned wounds heal faster than ointment-treated wounds. While this is an interesting finding, it does not address the core paradox — why honey, despite containing bacteria, helps wounds heal faster than either alternative. If anything, this deepens one mystery (why ointment seems to slow healing compared to no treatment) while completely ignoring the honey question. We need to know why honey works, not just that ointment does not.

**B. The bacteria found in honey are present in much lower concentrations than the concentrations of bacteria typically present in infected wounds, and applying antibiotic ointment to a wound rarely if ever kills all of the bacteria infecting the wound.** — No Impact (4% of students)
This answer offers two facts: honey's bacteria are in lower concentrations than those in typical infections, and ointment rarely kills all bacteria. While these seem helpful at first, neither resolves the paradox. Lower concentrations of bacteria still means more bacteria being added to a wound — any additional bacteria should slow healing according to the stated facts. And ointment not killing all bacteria does not explain why honey (which adds bacteria) heals wounds faster. We need an explanation for honey's positive effect, not just a reduction in its expected negative effect.

**C. Honey has properties that inhibit the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria the honey contains, and antibiotic ointments damage sensitive wound tissue, which slows healing.** — Correct (77% of students)
This answer resolves both halves of the paradox beautifully. First, honey has properties that inhibit bacterial growth in wounds, including its own bacteria. This explains why honey does not cause infection despite containing bacteria — the honey itself neutralizes the threat. Second, antibiotic ointments damage sensitive wound tissue, which slows healing. This explains why honey outperforms ointment — while ointment kills bacteria, it also damages the wound. Honey gets the best of both worlds: it inhibits bacteria (like ointment) without damaging tissue (unlike ointment). Together, these two mechanisms explain why honey-treated wounds heal faster than both ointment-treated and untreated wounds.

**D. The high concentration of sugar in honey inhibits the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria contained in the honey itself.** — Too Weak (11% of students)
This answer explains why honey's bacteria might not cause infection — the high sugar concentration inhibits bacterial growth, including honey's own bacteria. This resolves one half of the paradox: why honey does not make things worse. But it does not explain why honey heals wounds faster than ointment or faster than no treatment at all. Inhibiting bacteria puts honey on roughly equal footing with ointment (which kills bacteria) but does not explain honey's superior healing. We still need to know why the ointment is doing worse, and this answer does not address that.

**E. The antibiotic ointment used in the study damages sensitive tissue in wounds, which slows healing, but honey does not have this effect if the wound has been cleaned.** — Too Weak (8% of students)
This answer tells us that ointment damages wound tissue, explaining why ointment underperforms — but it does not explain why honey works. Even if ointment is harmful to tissue, that only explains the ointment-vs-cleaning comparison, not the honey-vs-cleaning comparison. Honey still contains bacteria, and we still need an explanation for why honey-treated wounds heal faster than wounds that were only cleaned. Without accounting for why honey's bacteria do not slow healing, this answer leaves the core paradox unresolved.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that...:** Bacteria in wounds cause infection, which slows healing. Honey contains significant bacteria.
- **How can it be that...:** Cleaned wounds treated with honey healed faster than wounds treated with antibiotic ointment and wounds only cleaned.
- **Evaluate / Goal:** We need something that explains both why honey's bacteria does not harm healing and why honey actively speeds healing beyond what ointment achieves.

**Answer choices**
**A. Wounds that have simply been cleaned with soap and water and not otherwise treated heal faster than wounds that have been cleaned and then treated with antibiotic ointment.** — Irrelevant Comparison (0% of students)
This compares cleaning alone to ointment but never addresses honey. We need to explain why honey, with its bacteria, heals wounds faster. This answer ignores the central paradox.

**B. The bacteria found in honey are present in much lower concentrations than the concentrations of bacteria typically present in infected wounds, and applying antibiotic ointment to a wound rarely if ever kills all of the bacteria infecting the wound.** — No Impact (4% of students)
Lower bacterial concentration still means adding bacteria to a wound, and ointment not killing all bacteria does not explain honey's superior healing. Neither fact resolves why honey actively helps.

**C. Honey has properties that inhibit the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria the honey contains, and antibiotic ointments damage sensitive wound tissue, which slows healing.** — Correct (77% of students)
Honey inhibits bacterial growth (solving the bacteria paradox) and ointment damages wound tissue (explaining why ointment loses). This two-part explanation resolves the full paradox.

**D. The high concentration of sugar in honey inhibits the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria contained in the honey itself.** — Too Weak (11% of students)
This explains why honey's bacteria do not harm the wound but not why honey heals faster than ointment. Bacterial inhibition alone does not give honey the edge over antibiotics.

**E. The antibiotic ointment used in the study damages sensitive tissue in wounds, which slows healing, but honey does not have this effect if the wound has been cleaned.** — Too Weak (8% of students)
Ointment damaging tissue explains why ointment loses, but not why honey (with its bacteria) beats even untreated wounds. The core paradox remains unresolved.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that...:** Bacteria in wounds = bad. Infection = slow healing. Honey = full of bacteria. So honey on wounds should equal disaster, right?
- **How can it be that...:** Honey-treated wounds healed faster than ointment-treated wounds AND faster than just-cleaned wounds. Honey, the thing full of bacteria, beat the stuff specifically designed to kill bacteria.
- **Evaluate / Goal:** This is a two-part puzzle: (1) why does honey's bacteria not make things worse? and (2) why does honey make things better than antibiotics? The correct answer needs to handle both. An answer that only explains why honey is not harmful, without explaining why it is better than ointment, only gets halfway there.

**Answer choices**
**A. Wounds that have simply been cleaned with soap and water and not otherwise treated heal faster than wounds that have been cleaned and then treated with antibiotic ointment.** — Irrelevant Comparison (0% of students)
So ointment is worse than just cleaning? That is mildly interesting but completely beside the point. The mystery is about honey — the thing loaded with bacteria that somehow heals wounds better than everything else. This answer talks about the race for second and third place while ignoring the surprise winner.

**B. The bacteria found in honey are present in much lower concentrations than the concentrations of bacteria typically present in infected wounds, and applying antibiotic ointment to a wound rarely if ever kills all of the bacteria infecting the wound.** — No Impact (4% of students)
"There is less bacteria in honey than in a typical infection" — okay, but there is still more bacteria than in a wound with no honey. Adding a small amount of bacteria should still be worse than adding zero bacteria. And "ointment does not kill ALL bacteria" — sure, but it kills some, which should be better than adding more. Neither of these facts explains the actual result: honey heals wounds better than everything else. This answer softens the paradox without resolving it.

**C. Honey has properties that inhibit the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria the honey contains, and antibiotic ointments damage sensitive wound tissue, which slows healing.** — Correct (77% of students)
Honey is secretly a double agent: it shows up carrying bacteria, but it also has properties that shut those bacteria down once they arrive at the wound. Problem one solved — the bacteria are neutralized. Meanwhile, the antibiotic ointment is doing its job killing bacteria but also doing damage to the wound tissue, basically healing with one hand and hurting with the other. So honey gets the antibacterial effect without the collateral damage, while ointment gets the antibacterial effect with a tissue-destruction side effect. No wonder honey wins — it is the only option playing offense without also playing against itself.

**D. The high concentration of sugar in honey inhibits the growth of bacteria in wounds, including the bacteria contained in the honey itself.** — Too Weak (11% of students)
Sugar in honey stops bacterial growth — great, that explains why honey does not make things worse. But the study says honey makes things better than antibiotics. If honey just neutralizes bacteria, it should tie with ointment (which also fights bacteria), not beat it. This answer explains why honey is not harmful but not why it is the champion. Solving half the mystery is not enough when the other half is staring you in the face.

**E. The antibiotic ointment used in the study damages sensitive tissue in wounds, which slows healing, but honey does not have this effect if the wound has been cleaned.** — Too Weak (8% of students)
Okay, so ointment damages wound tissue. That explains why ointment lost the race — it was running with weights on its ankles. But what about honey vs. no treatment? Cleaned wounds without any ointment should heal at their natural rate. Honey adds bacteria to those wounds and somehow heals them faster. This answer explains why one competitor lost but not why the surprise winner won. It is like explaining a boxing upset by saying the favorite had a bad corner team, without mentioning that the underdog threw better punches.

---

## S2 · Q12 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Art student: Great works of art evoke passionate responses in those who view them. Thus, since it is well known in art circles that the provocative work of abstract painter Ezekiel Reilly elicits intensely emotional responses in those who view them, his art is great.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument’s reasoning?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Great works of art evoke passionate responses in those who view them. (GW → PR) The work of abstract painter Ezekiel Reilly elicits intensely emotional responses in those who view them. (PR)
- **Conclusion:** His art is great. (GW)
- **Evaluate:** In order to draw this conclusion, we must assume that any work of art which elicits a passionate response is a great work. This is an illegal reversal of the first premise. (PR → GW)

**Answer choices**
**A. One of the premises used to support the argument’s conclusion assumes the truth of that conclusion.** — Circular Reasoning (9% of students)
Circular reasoning occurs when a premise is essentially a restatement of the conclusion — the argument assumes what it is trying to prove. That is not happening here. The premise that great art evokes passionate responses is not the same as the conclusion that Reilly's art is great. The premise about Reilly's work evoking emotional responses is also not a restatement of the conclusion. These are distinct claims. The argument's problem is that it reverses the conditional, not that it assumes its own conclusion.

**B. The argument treats a condition that is necessary for having a certain quality as if it must also be sufficient for having that quality.** — Correct (83% of students)
This precisely describes the argument's flaw. The first premise establishes that evoking passionate responses is a necessary condition for being great art — all great art has this quality. The argument then treats this necessary condition as if it were also sufficient — as if having this quality guarantees greatness. But many things that are not great art could evoke passionate responses. Provocative, disturbing, or even terrible art can elicit intense emotions. The argument assumes that because Reilly's art satisfies a necessary condition for greatness, it must therefore be great. This is the formal fallacy of affirming the consequent, equivalently described as confusing necessary and sufficient conditions.

**C. The argument misapplies a general claim about the members of a class to an atypical instance of that class.** — Sampling (3% of students)
This answer suggests the argument misapplies a general claim to an atypical instance. But there is no reason to believe Reilly's work is atypical of the class being discussed. The argument applies the claim "great art evokes passion" to Reilly's work, which does evoke passion. The problem is not that Reilly's work is an unusual case that does not fit the general claim — it is that the argument reverses the direction of the conditional. Even if Reilly's work were perfectly typical, the argument would still be flawed.

**D. The argument contains a generalization derived from an insufficient number of instances.** — Sampling (3% of students)
This answer describes a hasty generalization — drawing a broad conclusion from too few instances. The argument is not making a generalization at all. It is making a specific claim about one artist's work (Reilly's art is great). There is no indication that the argument uses an insufficient number of Reilly's works or an insufficient number of viewer reactions. The flaw is structural — reversing a conditional — not statistical.

**E. The argument draws a conclusion expressing a value judgment from premises that do not involve statements about values.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
This answer claims the argument draws a value judgment from non-value premises. But the first premise is itself a value statement: "great works of art evoke passionate responses" contains the value-laden term "great." The conclusion ("his art is great") and the premise both involve the concept of artistic quality. So the argument does not lack value-laden premises — it has one. The flaw is not about moving from factual premises to value conclusions; it is about reversing the direction of a conditional relationship within those value claims.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Great works of art evoke passionate responses (Great -> Passionate). Reilly's work elicits intensely emotional responses (Passionate).
- **Conclusion:** Reilly's art is great (Great).
- **Evaluate:** The argument reverses the conditional. From "great art evokes passion" it concludes "art that evokes passion is great." That is an illegal reversal — treating a necessary condition as sufficient.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that describes confusing a necessary condition with a sufficient condition.

**Answer choices**
**A. One of the premises used to support the argument’s conclusion assumes the truth of that conclusion.** — Circular Reasoning (9% of students)
No premise restates the conclusion. "Great art evokes passion" and "Reilly's work evokes emotion" are different from "Reilly's art is great." The flaw is a conditional reversal, not circular reasoning.

**B. The argument treats a condition that is necessary for having a certain quality as if it must also be sufficient for having that quality.** — Correct (83% of students)
The argument treats "evokes passion" as sufficient for greatness when it is only established as necessary. Great art must evoke passion, but not everything that evokes passion is great. This is the necessary-sufficient confusion.

**C. The argument misapplies a general claim about the members of a class to an atypical instance of that class.** — Sampling (3% of students)
Nothing indicates Reilly's work is atypical. The flaw is reversing the conditional direction, not misapplying a generalization to an outlier case.

**D. The argument contains a generalization derived from an insufficient number of instances.** — Sampling (3% of students)
The argument makes a specific claim about one artist, not a broad generalization from limited data. The flaw is a conditional reversal, not insufficient sampling.

**E. The argument draws a conclusion expressing a value judgment from premises that do not involve statements about values.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
The first premise involves "great" art — a value judgment. The argument does use value-laden premises, so this description is inaccurate. The flaw is a conditional reversal, not a fact-to-value transition.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Great art evokes passion. Reilly's art evokes passion.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore, Reilly's art is great.
- **Evaluate:** This is the classic "all dogs are mammals, this animal is a mammal, therefore it is a dog" pattern. Just because great art makes people emotional does not mean everything that makes people emotional is great art. A car alarm evokes intense emotional responses too, but nobody is hanging one in the Louvre. The argument flips the conditional and pretends the necessary condition is sufficient.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that calls out the necessary-sufficient confusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. One of the premises used to support the argument’s conclusion assumes the truth of that conclusion.** — Circular Reasoning (9% of students)
Circular reasoning is when the argument says "X because X" with different words. But this argument does not assume Reilly's art is great anywhere in its premises. It says great art evokes passion (not about Reilly) and Reilly's art evokes passion (not a claim about greatness). Those are real premises, not the conclusion in disguise. The problem is that the argument draws the wrong conclusion from those premises, not that it smuggles the conclusion into the premises.

**B. The argument treats a condition that is necessary for having a certain quality as if it must also be sufficient for having that quality.** — Correct (83% of students)
The argument says "great art evokes passion" and hears "anything that evokes passion is great art." Those are very different statements. All great restaurants serve food, but not everything that serves food is a great restaurant. All mammals are warm-blooded, but not everything warm-blooded is a mammal. Reilly's art makes people emotional? Sure. But so might a painting of a spider or an unpleasant noise sculpture. The argument flipped the conditional and pretended the necessary condition was the whole story.

**C. The argument misapplies a general claim about the members of a class to an atypical instance of that class.** — Sampling (3% of students)
This answer says the argument misapplied a general rule to a weird outlier case. But Reilly's art is not some bizarre exception — it evokes emotion, just like the general rule says great art does. The problem is that the argument concluded greatness from emotion rather than emotion from greatness. Reilly's work fits the pattern perfectly — the argument just read the pattern backward.

**D. The argument contains a generalization derived from an insufficient number of instances.** — Sampling (3% of students)
This answer says the argument jumped to a conclusion from too few examples. But the argument is not trying to generalize about all art or all emotional responses. It is making one specific claim: Reilly's art is great. And the problem with that claim is not "not enough data" — it is "wrong logical move." The argument could have surveyed every single person who ever saw Reilly's work and confirmed they all cried, and it would still be flawed, because making people cry does not prove artistic greatness.

**E. The argument draws a conclusion expressing a value judgment from premises that do not involve statements about values.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
This answer says the argument goes from facts to values. But "great works of art" in the first premise is already a value claim — "great" is not a measurement, it is a judgment. Both the premise and the conclusion are in the value neighborhood. The flaw is not that the argument jumped from facts to opinions; it is that it read its own conditional backward. The value terms were there from the start.

---

## S2 · Q13 — Strengthen

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
In northern Europe, archaeologists have discovered 400,000-year-old sharpened wooden poles alongside flint cutting implements and the remains of horses. Since it is normally assumed that Homo sapiens did not inhabit Europe prior to 200,000 years ago, this discovery effectively disproves the widespread belief that the humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens were entirely gatherers and scavengers and did not hunt.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, would add the most support to the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** In northern Europe, archaeologists have discovered 400,000-year-old sharpened wooden poles alongside flint cutting implements and the remains of horses. It is normally assumed that Homo sapiens did not inhabit Europe prior to 200,000 years ago.
- **Conclusion:** This discovery [the sharpened poles, cutting implements, and remains] effectively disproves the widespread belief that the humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens were entirely gatherers and scavengers and did not hunt.
- **Evaluate:** If we accept the assumption about Homo sapiens (humans) in the second premise, then the sharpened wooden poles and flint cutting implements were probably not made by humans and were likely made by our humanlike ancestors. However, we don't know what the poles and cutting implements were used for. We can't be certain that they were used for hunting, so their existence doesn't necessarily prove or disprove anything.
- **Goal:** A correct answer might support the idea that the sharpened poles or cutting implements were used for hunting. It could also refute the alternative possibility that they were used for something other than hunting.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sharpened wooden poles were not used by humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens for self-defense or to cut or transport scavenged carcasses.** — Correct (70% of students)
This answer eliminates two major alternative explanations for the sharpened poles. If the poles were not used for self-defense or for cutting and transporting scavenged carcasses, then the remaining plausible use is hunting. By ruling out non-hunting purposes, this answer strengthens the inference that the poles were hunting weapons. Combined with the horse remains found alongside, the hunting interpretation becomes much more compelling. The argument needed help closing the gap between "found tools near dead animals" and "used tools to kill animals," and this answer does exactly that.

**B. Humanlike inhabitants of northern Europe are known to have used stones and wooden sticks as tools more than 400,000 years ago.** — Premise Support (10% of students)
This answer establishes that humanlike inhabitants used stone and wooden tools more than 400,000 years ago. This makes it more plausible that the discovered artifacts were made by humanlike precursors rather than some other agent, which reinforces a premise. But the argument's weakness is not about who made the tools — it is about what the tools were used for. Knowing that precursors were capable of making tools does not tell us whether those tools were used for hunting, self-defense, or processing scavenged food. This answer supports the premise but does not strengthen the conclusion.

**C. Homo sapiens evolved from humanlike precursors at least 200,000 years earlier than is normally assumed.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
This answer says Homo sapiens evolved from precursors at least 200,000 years earlier than normally assumed. Even if modern humans evolved earlier, the argument's second premise states they "did not inhabit Europe" before 200,000 years ago — and evolving earlier does not necessarily mean inhabiting Europe earlier. This answer challenges the timeline of evolution, not the timeline of European habitation, which is the relevant premise. And even if it did affect the timeline, it would not help determine whether the tools were used for hunting.

**D. The humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens developed widely divergent patterns of behavior in the very different ecosystems they inhabited.** — Unclear Impact (5% of students)
This answer says that humanlike precursors developed "widely divergent patterns of behavior" in different ecosystems. This is too vague to strengthen the argument. "Widely divergent behavior" could include hunting, but it could also include many other activities — tool-making for non-hunting purposes, complex social behaviors, shelter construction, or entirely different survival strategies. Without specifying that hunting was among these behaviors, this answer has no clear bearing on the conclusion. It broadens the range of possible behaviors without narrowing down to the one the argument needs to establish.

**E. Prehistoric Homo sapiens who adopted hunting as a means of food acquisition did not abandon scavenging and gathering.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
This answer says that prehistoric Homo sapiens who hunted did not abandon scavenging and gathering. This is about Homo sapiens, not about the humanlike precursors the argument discusses. Even applying it to precursors, the fact that hunting and scavenging coexisted does not help establish that the precursors hunted in the first place. If anything, the coexistence of multiple food-acquisition strategies makes it harder to determine from artifacts alone whether a particular set of tools was used for hunting, scavenging, or gathering. This answer does not help resolve the ambiguity about tool use that weakens the argument.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** 400,000-year-old sharpened wooden poles, flint cutting implements, and horse remains found in northern Europe. Homo sapiens did not inhabit Europe before 200,000 years ago.
- **Conclusion:** This disproves the belief that humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens were entirely gatherers and scavengers who did not hunt.
- **Evaluate:** The argument assumes the poles and implements were used for hunting. But they could have been used for other purposes — self-defense, cutting scavenged meat, or transporting carcasses found dead.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that supports the hunting interpretation by eliminating alternative uses for the tools.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sharpened wooden poles were not used by humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens for self-defense or to cut or transport scavenged carcasses.** — Correct (70% of students)
Eliminating self-defense and scavenging as purposes for the poles leaves hunting as the most likely use. This closes the gap between finding tools near animal remains and concluding the tools were for hunting.

**B. Humanlike inhabitants of northern Europe are known to have used stones and wooden sticks as tools more than 400,000 years ago.** — Premise Support (10% of students)
This confirms precursors made tools, which supports a premise. But the argument's gap is about tool purpose (hunting vs. other uses), and this answer does not address that.

**C. Homo sapiens evolved from humanlike precursors at least 200,000 years earlier than is normally assumed.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
Earlier evolution of Homo sapiens does not mean earlier European habitation. And regardless, this does not help determine whether the tools were used for hunting.

**D. The humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens developed widely divergent patterns of behavior in the very different ecosystems they inhabited.** — Unclear Impact (5% of students)
"Widely divergent behavior" is too vague — it could include hunting or many other activities. Without specifying hunting, this has no clear impact on the argument.

**E. Prehistoric Homo sapiens who adopted hunting as a means of food acquisition did not abandon scavenging and gathering.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
This is about Homo sapiens, not precursors. And the coexistence of hunting and scavenging does not help establish that the precursors hunted — it makes tool interpretation harder, not easier.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Really old sharpened sticks, flint knives, and dead horses found together in northern Europe — 400,000 years old. Humans were not around yet, so the predecessors made these tools.
- **Conclusion:** Proof that the precursors hunted, which contradicts the belief they only gathered and scavenged.
- **Evaluate:** Sharp sticks plus dead horses looks like hunting, but there are other explanations. Maybe the sticks were for poking predators away from dinner. Maybe the cutting tools were for carving up roadkill. Maybe the horses died of natural causes and the precursors just found them. The argument needs help eliminating these alternatives.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that rules out non-hunting explanations for the tools.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sharpened wooden poles were not used by humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens for self-defense or to cut or transport scavenged carcasses.** — Correct (70% of students)
Sharp sticks were not for self-defense or for butchering roadkill? Then what were they for? With dead horses lying right next to them, the answer becomes pretty obvious: hunting. This answer closes the back doors one by one. Not for defense — check. Not for scavenging — check. The only door left open leads to the hunting conclusion. Exactly what the argument needed.

**B. Humanlike inhabitants of northern Europe are known to have used stones and wooden sticks as tools more than 400,000 years ago.** — Premise Support (10% of students)
Knowing that precursors used tools 400,000 years ago confirms they could have made these particular tools. Okay, but nobody was seriously doubting that. The real question is not "who made the sharp sticks?" but "what were the sharp sticks for?" This answer verifies the easy part of the argument while leaving the hard part untouched.

**C. Homo sapiens evolved from humanlike precursors at least 200,000 years earlier than is normally assumed.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
So Homo sapiens evolved earlier than thought? Even if true, evolving in Africa 400,000 years ago does not mean showing up in northern Europe 400,000 years ago. The premise says they did not inhabit Europe until 200,000 years ago — a claim about geography, not evolution. And even if the timeline were reshuffled entirely, the argument would still need to explain what those sharp sticks were for. This answer is fixing the wrong part of the puzzle.

**D. The humanlike precursors of Homo sapiens developed widely divergent patterns of behavior in the very different ecosystems they inhabited.** — Unclear Impact (5% of students)
"Widely divergent patterns of behavior" — so precursors in different places did different things. That is interesting anthropology but reveals nothing about whether the specific precursors in northern Europe were hunting horses with sharp sticks. "They had diverse behaviors" does not help identify which behaviors. It is like saying "people at the party did all sorts of things" when the question is whether anyone ate the cake.

**E. Prehistoric Homo sapiens who adopted hunting as a means of food acquisition did not abandon scavenging and gathering.** — Unclear Impact (10% of students)
Prehistoric humans who hunted also scavenged? So they were multitaskers. This does not help determine whether the precursors in northern Europe were hunting horses or just processing horse carcasses they found. If hunting and scavenging happen simultaneously, then sharp sticks next to dead horses could mean either thing. The argument needs the possibilities narrowed down, and this answer keeps them wide open.

---

## S2 · Q14 — Principle-Strengthen

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Politician: Our public libraries are open only on weekdays and generally at times when most children are at school and most adults at work. Hence, most taxpayers and their families have few opportunities to use public libraries. Therefore, no new taxes supporting the library system should be approved unless the library hours are changed to better suit taxpayers and their families.

### Prompt
Which one of the following principles, if valid, would most help to justify the politician’s reasoning?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Most taxpayers and their families have few opportunities to use public libraries because they are only open at times when most children are at school and most adults are at work.
- **Conclusion:** If new taxes supporting the library system are approved, the library hours should be changed to better suit taxpayers and their families.
- **Evaluate:** This sounds like a reasonable argument, but it's not airtight. There might be ways to make libraries more useful to taxpayers without changing the hours. Additional tax money could be used to improve libraries' web sites and online services, allowing taxpayers to benefit from libraries even when the actual library facilities aren't open. We could pass laws requiring employers and schools to give people time off so they can use the library when it's open. If any of these alternatives occur, the conclusion doesn't necessarily hold up.
- **Goal:** An ideal correct answer might state that creating any new taxes to support the library system requires giving taxpayers more opportunities to use libraries. (New taxes → more opportunities for taxpayers) Or, it might state that giving taxpayers these opportunities requires changing the library hours. (More opportunities for taxpayers → change hours)

**Answer choices**
**A. Libraries and other public facilities that serve an educational purpose should be made as convenient as possible for taxpayers and their families to use.** — Out of Scope (7% of students)
This answer says libraries and educational facilities "should be made as convenient as possible." While this sounds related, it does not connect convenience to tax approval. The argument's conclusion is specifically about whether new taxes should be approved, and this principle says nothing about taxation. Making libraries convenient could be achieved in ways unrelated to taxes or library hours — for example, through online services or mobile libraries. This principle is too detached from the argument's specific conclusion about tax approval to justify it.

**B. If use of a public facility is made more convenient for taxpayers and their families, then new taxes supporting that facility should be approved.** — Reversal (17% of students)
This answer says: if use of a facility is made more convenient, then new taxes should be approved. That gives us: more convenient -> approve taxes. But the argument needs: approve taxes -> more convenient (or more specifically, approve taxes -> adequate access). This answer provides the reverse conditional. Under this principle, we could conclude taxes should be approved if hours are changed, but we could not conclude that taxes should not be approved unless hours are changed. The direction is backward.

**C. Taxpayers who have plenty of opportunities to use a public facility should have to pay taxes to support that facility.** — Reversal (1% of students)
This answer says taxpayers with plenty of opportunities to use a facility "should have to pay taxes" for it. This gives us: plenty of opportunities -> should pay taxes. Like answer B, this is the reverse of what we need. The argument needs: should pay taxes -> adequate opportunities. This principle tells us when people should pay taxes (when they have access), not when taxes should be denied (when they lack access). Furthermore, the argument is about approving new taxes, not about whether existing taxpayers "should have to pay."

**D. The best way to increase usage of public libraries is to change the library hours for the convenience of most people.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
This answer says the best way to increase usage is to change library hours. But the argument's conclusion is about whether new taxes should be approved, not about the best way to increase library usage. Usage and opportunity are different concepts — the argument is about giving taxpayers opportunities to use libraries, not about maximizing actual usage. Even if changing hours is the best way to increase usage, this principle does not connect that fact to the question of tax approval. The argument needs a principle about taxation, not a principle about usage optimization.

**E. A new tax supporting a public facility should be approved only when most taxpayers have ample opportunities to use that facility.** — Correct (71% of students)
This principle states that new taxes supporting a public facility should be approved only when most taxpayers have ample opportunities to use that facility. Applied to the argument: new library taxes should be approved only when most taxpayers can actually use libraries. Since libraries are currently open only when most taxpayers cannot attend, most taxpayers lack ample opportunities. Therefore, new library taxes should not be approved unless hours change to provide those opportunities. The principle bridges the gap between "taxpayers lack access" and "taxes should be conditional on improved access." The use of "public facility" rather than "library" is acceptably broader — the argument's specific case fits within this general principle.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Most taxpayers and their families have few opportunities to use public libraries because libraries are only open when children are at school and adults are at work.
- **Conclusion:** No new taxes supporting the library system should be approved unless library hours are changed to better suit taxpayers.
- **Evaluate:** The gap: lacking opportunities to use libraries does not automatically mean new taxes should be conditional on changing hours. We need a principle linking taxpayer access to tax approval.
- **Goal:** Find a principle stating that new taxes for a public facility require that taxpayers have adequate opportunities to use it.

**Answer choices**
**A. Libraries and other public facilities that serve an educational purpose should be made as convenient as possible for taxpayers and their families to use.** — Out of Scope (7% of students)
This addresses making libraries convenient but says nothing about tax approval. The argument's conclusion is about when new taxes should be approved, which this principle does not address.

**B. If use of a public facility is made more convenient for taxpayers and their families, then new taxes supporting that facility should be approved.** — Reversal (17% of students)
This says "more convenient -> approve taxes." The argument needs "approve taxes -> adequate access." The conditional direction is reversed from what the conclusion requires.

**C. Taxpayers who have plenty of opportunities to use a public facility should have to pay taxes to support that facility.** — Reversal (1% of students)
This says "adequate access -> should pay taxes." The argument needs "approve taxes -> adequate access." Same reversal problem as answer B.

**D. The best way to increase usage of public libraries is to change the library hours for the convenience of most people.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
This is about the best way to increase usage, not about when taxes should be approved. The argument needs a taxation principle, and this is a usage-optimization principle.

**E. A new tax supporting a public facility should be approved only when most taxpayers have ample opportunities to use that facility.** — Correct (71% of students)
New taxes for a public facility require that most taxpayers have ample access. Most taxpayers currently lack access to libraries. Therefore, no new library taxes unless access improves — exactly the argument's conclusion.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Libraries are open when nobody can go — kids are at school, adults are at work. Most taxpayers have almost no chance to actually use the library.
- **Conclusion:** Do not approve new library taxes unless the hours change.
- **Evaluate:** The argument goes from "taxpayers cannot use this thing" to "taxpayers should not pay more for it unless they can." That seems reasonable, but it relies on an unstated principle: if you are taxing people for a service, they should actually be able to use it.
- **Goal:** Find the principle that makes tax approval conditional on taxpayer access. Not the other way around.

**Answer choices**
**A. Libraries and other public facilities that serve an educational purpose should be made as convenient as possible for taxpayers and their families to use.** — Out of Scope (7% of students)
"Libraries should be as convenient as possible" — a lovely sentiment, but it does not mention taxes anywhere. The argument is not just saying libraries should be convenient. It is saying do not approve new taxes unless the situation improves. This principle might justify a city council resolution about library hours but it does not justify making tax approval conditional on those hours. Missing the key ingredient.

**B. If use of a public facility is made more convenient for taxpayers and their families, then new taxes supporting that facility should be approved.** — Reversal (17% of students)
This says "if they make it convenient, approve the taxes." The argument says "do not approve taxes unless they make it convenient." Those sound similar but the logic goes in opposite directions. This principle would justify saying "hours changed? Great, pass the tax." But the argument says "no change, no tax." This answer reads the conditional backward — a classic LSAT trap on Principle questions.

**C. Taxpayers who have plenty of opportunities to use a public facility should have to pay taxes to support that facility.** — Reversal (1% of students)
Same problem as B, different wording. "People who can use the library should pay for it." Okay, but the argument is saying "people who cannot use the library should not be asked to pay more for it." Those are opposite directions. This principle justifies taxing people who have access, not blocking taxes when people lack access.

**D. The best way to increase usage of public libraries is to change the library hours for the convenience of most people.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
This is practical advice about how to get more people into libraries. But the argument is making a claim about tax policy. "Here is the best way to boost library traffic" does not connect to "do not approve new taxes unless this happens." These are different conversations — one is operational, the other is about the conditions for fiscal support. The argument needs a tax principle, and this is a library management tip.

**E. A new tax supporting a public facility should be approved only when most taxpayers have ample opportunities to use that facility.** — Correct (71% of students)
This is the missing rule: do not make taxpayers pay for something they cannot use. New taxes for a public facility should only be approved when taxpayers can actually use it. Taxpayers cannot currently use the library. Therefore, no new taxes until they can. The argument adds "unless hours change" because that is what would give taxpayers access. The principle is stated for "public facility" rather than "library" specifically, but that is fine — the library is a public facility, and a broader principle can justify a narrower conclusion.

---

## S2 · Q15 — Most Supported

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
The introduction of mass production techniques in modern industrial economies allowed the owners of industries to lower prices because they could employ fewer workers, many of whom required little training. The lower prices allowed workers to buy goods that they previously would not have been able to afford. But since jobs for workers with little training are more vulnerable to elimination than those for more highly trained workers, _______.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most logically completes the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** The introduction of mass production techniques in modern industrial economies allowed for lower prices because industries could employ fewer workers, many of whom required little training. The lower prices allowed workers to buy goods that they previously would not have been able to afford. But jobs for workers with little training are more vulnerable to elimination than those for more highly trained workers.
- **Evaluate:** The statements that we're given are the evidence in the argument. The correct answer will be a conclusion that is supported by this evidence. The premises describe a benefit for workers—the ability to buy goods that they previously couldn't afford. But the premises also describe a disadvantage. The workers with little training are at a higher risk for losing their jobs.
- **Goal:** The correct answer is likely to focus on the disadvantage, or at least state that workers with little training in a modern industrial economy experience a mixed situation with both advantages and disadvantages.

**Answer choices**
**A. highly trained workers have more purchasing power in modern industrial economies than workers who are less trained** — Unsupported Comparison (10% of students)
The stimulus never compares the purchasing power of highly trained workers to that of less-trained workers. The evidence says lower prices allowed workers generally to buy previously unaffordable goods — it does not distinguish between how much different training levels benefit from these lower prices. This comparison requires information that is not provided in the premises.

**B. the introduction of mass production techniques has decreased benefits for workers as it has increased the profits for owners of industries** — Contradicted (6% of students)
The stimulus does not say mass production decreased benefits for workers — it says lower prices allowed workers to buy goods they previously could not afford, which is an increase in benefits. The third premise introduces a vulnerability (job insecurity for less-trained workers) but does not establish that overall benefits decreased. Additionally, the stimulus never mentions profits for owners of industries, so the claim about increased profits is unsupported. This answer overstates the negative while introducing an unsupported element about profits.

**C. even the highest paid employees in modern industrial economies are never able to achieve job security** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The stimulus says highly trained workers have more secure jobs than less-trained ones. This answer says "even the highest paid employees" can never achieve job security. The third premise contradicts this by establishing that more highly trained workers face less vulnerability than less-trained workers — implying greater (though perhaps not absolute) job security. The word "never" makes this answer especially extreme and unsupportable. If anything, the premises suggest more highly trained workers can achieve meaningful job security.

**D. a source of increased purchasing power for workers in modern industrial economies also undermines their job security** — Correct (77% of students)
This answer precisely captures the paradox described in the premises. The "source of increased purchasing power" is mass production's effect of lowering prices through fewer, less-trained workers. This same dynamic — reliance on less-trained workers — also "undermines their job security" because such jobs are more vulnerable to elimination. The answer correctly identifies that a single cause produces both a benefit (purchasing power) and a detriment (job insecurity). It stays within the scope of the evidence, avoids unsupported comparisons, and accurately reflects the tension the stimulus describes.

**E. the percentage of workers who can afford to purchase goods produced by modern industrial techniques is shrinking** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
The stimulus describes workers' jobs being vulnerable to elimination but does not say workers have actually lost their jobs in significant numbers. "Vulnerable to elimination" is a risk, not an accomplished fact. We cannot conclude that the percentage of workers who can afford goods is shrinking, because we do not know how many workers have actually been laid off or whether new jobs have replaced old ones. The evidence describes a potential threat, not an ongoing trend of actual job losses that would reduce the purchasing workforce.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Mass production allowed lower prices by employing fewer, less-trained workers. Workers could buy previously unaffordable goods. But jobs for less-trained workers are more vulnerable to elimination.
- **Evaluate:** The same thing that gave workers more purchasing power (fewer jobs requiring less training) also made their employment less secure. A single cause with both a benefit and a drawback.
- **Goal:** The conclusion should capture this dual nature: a source of purchasing power that also undermines job security.

**Answer choices**
**A. highly trained workers have more purchasing power in modern industrial economies than workers who are less trained** — Unsupported Comparison (10% of students)
The stimulus does not compare purchasing power across training levels. It says lower prices benefited workers generally, without distinguishing between more and less trained workers.

**B. the introduction of mass production techniques has decreased benefits for workers as it has increased the profits for owners of industries** — Contradicted (6% of students)
Mass production increased purchasing power (a benefit), and the evidence never says overall benefits decreased. Profits for owners are also not discussed.

**C. even the highest paid employees in modern industrial economies are never able to achieve job security** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The stimulus says highly trained workers have more secure jobs. "Never able to achieve job security" contradicts this directly, and the absolute "never" makes it especially unsupported.

**D. a source of increased purchasing power for workers in modern industrial economies also undermines their job security** — Correct (77% of students)
Mass production is both the source of purchasing power (lower prices) and the source of job vulnerability (less-trained positions). This answer captures the single cause with dual effects.

**E. the percentage of workers who can afford to purchase goods produced by modern industrial techniques is shrinking** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
"Vulnerable to elimination" describes a risk, not actual job losses. We cannot conclude the purchasing workforce is shrinking without knowing how many workers have actually lost jobs.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Mass production: fewer workers needed, less training required, prices drop. Workers can buy more stuff. But — and here is the catch — jobs for people with less training are easier to eliminate.
- **Evaluate:** Same cause, opposite effects. Mass production gave workers cheaper goods with one hand and a pink slip with the other. The very thing that put more products on their shelves also put their jobs at risk.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that captures this bittersweet irony without going overboard in either direction.

**Answer choices**
**A. highly trained workers have more purchasing power in modern industrial economies than workers who are less trained** — Unsupported Comparison (10% of students)
The stimulus never puts highly trained workers and less-trained workers in a purchasing-power competition. It just says lower prices let workers buy more stuff. That is about workers as a group, not a breakdown by training level. This answer invented a comparison the evidence never made.

**B. the introduction of mass production techniques has decreased benefits for workers as it has increased the profits for owners of industries** — Contradicted (6% of students)
Decreased benefits? The stimulus just established that workers could now buy things they could not afford before. That is an increase, not a decrease. Yes, there is a downside (job vulnerability), but the evidence never says the downside outweighs the upside. And "increased profits for owners" comes from nowhere — the stimulus mentions owners lowering prices, not fattening their wallets. Two claims, zero support.

**C. even the highest paid employees in modern industrial economies are never able to achieve job security** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The stimulus literally says highly trained workers' jobs are less vulnerable than less-trained workers' jobs. That means more training equals more security. This answer says nobody — not even the highest paid — can ever be secure. That is not what the evidence says. "Never" is a very strong word, and the stimulus is working in the opposite direction.

**D. a source of increased purchasing power for workers in modern industrial economies also undermines their job security** — Correct (77% of students)
Mass production: the gift that keeps on giving and taking away. It gave workers cheaper goods (source of purchasing power) by creating a system that relies on less-trained workers — the very workers whose jobs are easiest to eliminate (undermines job security). Same coin, two sides. This answer captures that irony without overdramatizing it into "mass production is bad" or underplaying it into "mass production is great." It is the Goldilocks answer for a stimulus that presents both a silver lining and a storm cloud.

**E. the percentage of workers who can afford to purchase goods produced by modern industrial techniques is shrinking** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
"Vulnerable to elimination" is not the same as "eliminated." The stimulus says these jobs could be cut, not that they have been. Concluding that the percentage of workers who can afford things is shrinking requires actual layoff data, not just a theoretical risk. It is like saying "this bridge could collapse" and concluding "fewer people are crossing the river." Maybe, but you need evidence of actual collapse first.

---

## S2 · Q16 — Sufficient Assumption

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Child psychologist: Psychologists have found that most children under the age of six are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes toward animals. Ordinarily, it is only between the ages of six and nine that children begin to understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs. Hence, most children should not have pets until they are at least six years old.

### Prompt
The child psychologist’s conclusion follows logically if which one of the following is assumed?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Ordinarily, it is only between the ages of six and nine that children begin to understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.
- **Conclusion:** Most children should not have pets until they are at least six years old.
- **Evaluate:** Conditional statements are generally about things that must always be true. We don't usually consider something a conditional statement when it merely describes something that "ordinarily" happens or something that happens with "most" people in a group. However, in this case, as long as we remember the words "ordinarily" and "most" in the original argument, we can use conditional statements to better understand the argument. The premise states that most children under age six do not understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs. (under 6 → not understand) The conclusion states that most children under six should not have pets. (under 6 → should not have pets)
- **Goal:** We're looking for an answer which states that anyone who does not understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs should not have pets. (not understand → should not have pets) The contrapositive of this would also be correct. (should have pets → understand)

**Answer choices**
**A. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals rely on others to take care of a pet.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
This answer says most egocentric and selfish children rely on others to care for pets. The argument does not discuss who takes care of pets or whether children rely on others. The argument's gap is about whether not understanding animals' independence means children should not have pets. Relying on others for pet care is an entirely different issue from whether a child should own a pet in the first place.

**B. Children who are old enough to understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs should be allowed to have pets.** — Negation (24% of students)
This answer says children who understand animals' independence should be allowed to have pets. Diagrammed: Understand -> Should have pets. But we need the reverse: Should have pets -> Understand. This answer provides an illegal negation of the needed assumption. The argument needs to establish that understanding is a requirement for having pets, not that understanding guarantees permission to have pets. A child who understands animals should perhaps be allowed a pet, but that does not help us conclude that a child who does not understand animals should not have one.

**C. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals do not have pets.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
This answer says most egocentric and selfish children do not have pets. This describes what is actually the case, not what should be the case. The argument's conclusion is prescriptive — most children should not have pets until age six. Knowing that most egocentric children currently do not have pets does not establish whether they should or should not. Descriptive facts about pet ownership patterns cannot bridge the gap to a normative conclusion about what ought to happen.

**D. Most children are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards their pets and do not understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Unrelated to Goal (9% of students)
This answer says most children are egocentric and do not understand their pets' feelings and needs. This essentially restates and combines aspects of the two premises but does not connect them to the conclusion's prescription that children should not have pets. The argument already tells us children under six are egocentric and do not understand animals. Restating this fact does not explain why this means they should not have pets. The answer fails to address the should/should-not component that is essential to bridging evidence and conclusion.

**E. The only children who should have pets are those who understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Correct (63% of students)
This answer states that the only children who should have pets are those who understand their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs. Diagrammed: Should have pets -> Understand. Contrapositive: Not understand -> Should not have pets. The evidence establishes that most children under six do not understand animals' independence (Under 6 -> Not understand). Combining: Under 6 -> Not understand -> Should not have pets. This produces the conclusion: most children under six should not have pets. The answer perfectly bridges the gap between the evidence about understanding and the conclusion about pet ownership.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Most children under six do not understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.
- **Conclusion:** Most children should not have pets until they are at least six.
- **Evaluate:** We need: not understanding animals' independence -> should not have pets. This bridges the evidence to the conclusion.
- **Goal:** Find: should have pets -> understands animals are independent (the contrapositive also works).

**Answer choices**
**A. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals rely on others to take care of a pet.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
Relying on others for pet care is not the issue. The argument needs a link between not understanding animals and not having pets, not information about who feeds the goldfish.

**B. Children who are old enough to understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs should be allowed to have pets.** — Negation (24% of students)
This says "understand -> should have pets." We need "should have pets -> understand." This is the reverse — an illegal negation of the required assumption.

**C. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals do not have pets.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
This describes current reality (most egocentric kids do not have pets), not a normative principle (they should not have pets). The argument needs a prescriptive assumption, not a descriptive fact.

**D. Most children are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards their pets and do not understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Unrelated to Goal (9% of students)
This restates the premises without connecting them to the conclusion's "should not have pets" prescription. We already know children are egocentric — we need to know why that means no pets.

**E. The only children who should have pets are those who understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Correct (63% of students)
"Only" children who understand animals should have pets means: should have pets -> understand. Contrapositive: not understand -> should not have pets. Combined with "under 6 -> not understand," this yields the conclusion perfectly.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Kids under six generally do not understand that animals have their own feelings and needs. This awareness usually kicks in between ages six and nine.
- **Conclusion:** Most kids should not have pets until six.
- **Evaluate:** The argument goes: kids under six do not understand animals -> kids under six should not have pets. The missing link is obvious: not understanding animals means you should not have pets. Or flipped: having pets requires understanding animals.
- **Goal:** Find the assumption that says "no understanding of animal independence = no pets for you."

**Answer choices**
**A. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals rely on others to take care of a pet.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
So egocentric kids make other people feed their hamster? Interesting parenting detail, irrelevant logical connection. The argument is not about who does the chores — it is about whether kids who do not understand animals should have pets at all. Whether little Timmy feeds the dog himself or makes Mom do it has nothing to do with why he should or should not have a dog in the first place.

**B. Children who are old enough to understand that animals are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs should be allowed to have pets.** — Negation (24% of students)
"Old enough to understand animals? Then you can have a pet." That sounds reasonable, but it goes the wrong direction. The argument needs: "If you do not understand animals, no pets for you." This answer says: "If you do understand animals, pets for you." Those are different claims. Knowing that understanding animals gets you a pet does not prove that lacking understanding bars you from one. This is the classic LSAT negation trap — right concept, wrong direction.

**C. Most children who are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards animals do not have pets.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
Most egocentric kids do not have pets? Maybe their parents already figured this out. But "they do not have pets" is not the same as "they should not have pets." The argument needs a rule about what ought to be, not a report about what already is. This is the difference between "most people do not jaywalk" and "most people should not jaywalk." The argument needs the should, and this answer provides the do.

**D. Most children are egocentric and selfish in their attitudes towards their pets and do not understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Unrelated to Goal (9% of students)
This is the argument's evidence wearing a slightly different outfit. "Most children are egocentric and do not understand animals" — yes, the argument already said that. But the conclusion is not "children are egocentric." The conclusion is "children should not have pets." This answer confirms the known facts without providing what is actually needed: why being egocentric means you should not have a pet.
