**E. The only children who should have pets are those who understand that their pets are independent creatures with their own feelings and needs.** — Correct (63% of students)
"The only children who should have pets are the ones who get it." "Only" is the magic word — it makes understanding animals a requirement for pet ownership, not just a nice bonus. No understanding? No pets. And since kids under six generally do not understand animals' feelings and needs, kids under six generally should not have pets. The chain links perfectly: under six -> do not understand -> should not have pets. This is the assumption that makes the whole argument work.

---

## S2 · Q17 — Parallel Flaw

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
The average tax refund received by taxpayers who use tax preparation services is about 50 percent higher than the average refund received by those who do not. So if you want a large refund, you should use a tax preparation service.

### Prompt
Which one of the following arguments exhibits flawed reasoning most similar to the flawed reasoning exhibited by the argument above?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The average tax refund received by taxpayers who use tax preparation services is about 50 percent higher than the average refund received by those who do not.
- **Conclusion:** If you want a large refund, you should use a tax preparation service.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence describes a correlation between using a tax preparation service and getting a larger tax refund. The conclusion states that, for those who want a large refund, the preferred option is to use a tax preparation service. There's an underlying assumption that the tax preparation service causes taxpayers to receive a larger refund, but that isn't necessarily the cause. This argument exhibits a Causal Flaw.

**Answer choices**
**A. People who invest heavily in the stock market generally have higher incomes than people who do not; so if you want to have a high income, you should invest heavily in the stock market.** — Correct (89% of students)
This argument mirrors the original's flawed structure exactly. The evidence presents a correlation: people who invest heavily in the stock market generally have higher incomes. The conclusion draws a causal recommendation: if you want a high income, you should invest heavily in the stock market. Just as with the tax preparation argument, the correlation can be explained by a confounding variable — people with high incomes have more disposable money to invest, rather than investing causing the high income. The causal direction is ambiguous, and the argument ignores the possibility that a third factor (existing wealth) drives both the investing and the high income. The structure is a perfect match: correlation in the evidence, causal prescription in the conclusion, confounding variable unaddressed.

**B. Establishing an in-house print shop usually enables companies to produce their publications more efficiently. So if your company produces publications, then it should establish an in-house print shop.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
This argument's evidence uses the phrase "usually enables," which describes a causal relationship, not a mere correlation. The original argument's evidence described a statistical correlation between two groups without establishing any causal mechanism. When evidence already states that one thing "enables" another, recommending that action is not a correlation-to-causation error — it is a reasonable inference from the stated causal relationship. The flaw structure does not match because the evidence type is fundamentally different.

**C. Quitting smoking will lower your life insurance premium and will also decrease your risk of lung cancer. So if you want to lower your life insurance premium, you should try to reduce your risk of lung cancer.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
This argument has two structural problems that distinguish it from the original. First, the evidence uses "will lower" and "will also decrease," which assert direct causal relationships rather than mere correlations. The original argument's evidence only described a statistical difference between groups. Second, the conclusion draws a causal link between two effects (lowering insurance premiums and reducing cancer risk), which is an entirely different reasoning error from the original. The original concluded that one variable causes another; this argument concludes that two effects of the same cause are causally linked to each other. Neither the evidence type nor the conclusion structure matches.

**D. People with larger-than-average estates to manage usually find it helpful to have their own lawyers; so if you have a larger-than-average estate to manage, you should get your own lawyer.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
This argument's evidence states that people with large estates "usually find it helpful" to have their own lawyers. This describes a preference or subjective assessment of helpfulness, not a statistical correlation between two independently measurable variables. The original argument compared average refund amounts between two groups — a quantitative correlation. This answer describes a group's attitude toward a service, which is closer to a causal claim (having a lawyer helps) than to a bare correlation. The evidence structure does not match, so the flaw pattern differs.

**E. In deciding who they will call, telemarketers target people who have purchased from telemarketers in the past. So if you do not want to receive calls from telemarketers, you should not purchase anything from them.** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
This argument's evidence describes a deliberate action by telemarketers: they target people who have purchased from telemarketers before. This is a description of a behavioral strategy, not a statistical correlation between two independently measured variables. The original argument's evidence compared average outcomes across two groups. Additionally, the conclusion's reasoning (do not buy from telemarketers to avoid calls) follows logically from the evidence — if telemarketers target past buyers, then not buying should reduce calls. There is no correlation-to-causation error here because the evidence establishes an actual decision-making process, not a mere statistical association.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Taxpayers who use tax preparation services receive average refunds about 50 percent higher than those who do not use such services.
- **Conclusion:** If you want a large refund, you should use a tax preparation service.
- **Evaluate:** The argument treats a correlation (using services correlates with bigger refunds) as causation (using services causes bigger refunds). People with complex finances may both seek professional help and have more deductions — the service does not necessarily create the larger refund.
- **Goal:** Find the answer choice whose argument has the same structure: a correlation is observed, and from that correlation a causal recommendation is drawn.

**Answer choices**
**A. People who invest heavily in the stock market generally have higher incomes than people who do not; so if you want to have a high income, you should invest heavily in the stock market.** — Correct (89% of students)
Correlation: high-income people invest more. Conclusion: investing causes high income. Same flaw — people with high incomes have more money to invest, not the other way around. The confounding variable (existing wealth) is ignored, just as in the original.

**B. Establishing an in-house print shop usually enables companies to produce their publications more efficiently. So if your company produces publications, then it should establish an in-house print shop.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
"Usually enables" describes a causal relationship, not a correlation. The original argument's flaw was treating correlation as causation, but this evidence already establishes causation.

**C. Quitting smoking will lower your life insurance premium and will also decrease your risk of lung cancer. So if you want to lower your life insurance premium, you should try to reduce your risk of lung cancer.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
The evidence states causal facts ("will lower," "will also decrease"), not correlations. And the conclusion oddly links two effects of quitting smoking to each other, rather than recommending the cause to achieve an effect. Neither component matches the original.

**D. People with larger-than-average estates to manage usually find it helpful to have their own lawyers; so if you have a larger-than-average estate to manage, you should get your own lawyer.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
"Usually find it helpful" describes a subjective assessment of usefulness, not a statistical correlation. The original's evidence compared measurable outcomes between two groups. The evidence types do not match.

**E. In deciding who they will call, telemarketers target people who have purchased from telemarketers in the past. So if you do not want to receive calls from telemarketers, you should not purchase anything from them.** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
The evidence describes a deliberate targeting strategy, not a correlation. And the conclusion logically follows — if telemarketers target past buyers, not buying should reduce calls. No correlation-causation flaw exists here.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** People who use tax prep services get bigger refunds on average. A 50 percent bump, no less.
- **Conclusion:** Want a big refund? Use a tax prep service.
- **Evaluate:** This is the classic "people who carry umbrellas get rained on more, so carrying an umbrella must cause rain" pattern. People with complicated finances — lots of deductions, investments, side income — are exactly the ones who hire professionals. They would get big refunds anyway because of their tax situation, not because the preparer sprinkled magic dust on the return. The argument sees two things happening together and assumes one causes the other.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that copies this structure: correlation in the evidence, causal recommendation in the conclusion, confounding variable ignored.

**Answer choices**
**A. People who invest heavily in the stock market generally have higher incomes than people who do not; so if you want to have a high income, you should invest heavily in the stock market.** — Correct (89% of students)
"Rich people invest in stocks, so investing in stocks will make you rich." This is the tax prep argument wearing a Wall Street suit. High-income people have extra money lying around, so of course they invest more. The investing did not make them rich — being rich made them investors. Same flaw, same structure: correlation mistaken for causation, confounding variable (existing wealth) completely ignored, and a confident recommendation that gets the causal arrow backward.

**B. Establishing an in-house print shop usually enables companies to produce their publications more efficiently. So if your company produces publications, then it should establish an in-house print shop.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
"Usually enables" is doing something the original argument's evidence never did — actually claiming one thing causes another. The tax prep argument just said "these two things go together" and jumped to "one causes the other." This argument's evidence already says the print shop enables efficiency. So the recommendation to set up a print shop follows from a stated causal claim, not from a bare correlation. Different starting point, different flaw (if any).

**C. Quitting smoking will lower your life insurance premium and will also decrease your risk of lung cancer. So if you want to lower your life insurance premium, you should try to reduce your risk of lung cancer.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
This argument is a mess, but not the same kind of mess as the original. The evidence flat-out says quitting smoking will lower premiums and will reduce cancer risk — those are causal claims, not correlations. Then the conclusion gets creative and says reducing cancer risk will lower premiums, linking two effects of the same cause to each other. That is a different species of bad reasoning. The original saw a correlation and called it causation; this one sees two effects and calls one the cause of the other.

**D. People with larger-than-average estates to manage usually find it helpful to have their own lawyers; so if you have a larger-than-average estate to manage, you should get your own lawyer.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
People with big estates "find it helpful" to have lawyers. That is not a correlation — that is basically saying "lawyers help with big estates." The tax prep argument never said people "found it helpful" to use tax services. It just said service users got bigger refunds and let you assume the service was the reason. When the evidence already says something is helpful, recommending it is not a correlation-causation error. It is just following the evidence.

**E. In deciding who they will call, telemarketers target people who have purchased from telemarketers in the past. So if you do not want to receive calls from telemarketers, you should not purchase anything from them.** — Bad Evidence Match (2% of students)
Telemarketers call people who bought from them before. That is not a mysterious correlation — that is a business strategy with a clear causal mechanism. And the advice follows perfectly: stop buying, stop getting targeted. The tax prep argument saw two things happening together and guessed at causation. This argument sees a deliberate cause-and-effect chain and draws a sensible conclusion from it. No flaw to parallel here.

---

## S2 · Q18 — Sufficient Assumption

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
In an experiment, each of 200 randomly selected people was videotaped while describing action-packed excerpts from previously unfamiliar cartoons. Half the subjects were allowed to gesture while speaking, and the other half were not. Those who gestured spoke more quickly and repeated themselves less. This indicates that gesturing helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if assumed, enables the conclusion in the argument to be properly drawn?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The subjects in an experiment who gestured spoke more quickly and repeated themselves less than those who didn't gesture. (G → SQ and ↓R)
- **Conclusion:** gesturing helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want. (G → QFP) It's worth noting that the conclusion isn't truly a conditional statement. It's similar to one, but it only claims that gesturing helps speakers to find phrases. It doesn't state that gesturing guarantees anything. However, as long as we keep the actual language of the conclusion in mind, diagramming the conclusion as a conditional statement can help us understand the argument.
- **Goal:** We're looking for an answer which states that when someone speaks quickly or repeats themselves less, this helps them to quickly find the phrases they want. (SQ or ↓R → QFP)

**Answer choices**
**A. Ordinarily almost everyone regularly gestures when speaking quickly and with little repetition, regardless of the topic being discussed.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
This answer says that almost everyone ordinarily gestures when speaking quickly with little repetition. This tells us gesturing is common when people already speak quickly and repeat little, but it does not establish that gesturing causes or helps with finding phrases. The conclusion is about gesturing helping find phrases, and this answer says nothing about phrase retrieval. It also reverses the relationship: instead of gesturing leading to quicker speech, it says quicker speech is accompanied by gesturing — which does not bridge the gap to the conclusion about finding phrases.

**B. The cartoons were chosen by those who conducted the experiment and were selected from a variety of sources.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
This answer describes how the cartoons were selected — chosen by experimenters from a variety of sources. This is a methodological detail about the experiment's design that has no bearing on whether gesturing helps speakers find phrases. The argument's gap is between speech outcomes (speed, repetition) and cognitive function (phrase retrieval). How the cartoon stimuli were chosen is completely irrelevant to bridging that gap. This answer does not address phrase-finding at all.

**C. Any form of behavior correlated with quicker speech and less repetition in speech helps speakers find the phrases they want quickly.** — Correct (59% of students)
This answer states that any behavior correlated with quicker speech and less repetition helps speakers find the phrases they want quickly. The evidence established that gesturing correlates with quicker speech and less repetition. This answer provides the bridge principle: if a behavior correlates with those speech outcomes, it helps with phrase-finding. Applying this principle to gesturing: gesturing correlates with quicker speech and less repetition (from the evidence), therefore gesturing helps speakers find phrases quickly (the conclusion). The assumption perfectly fills the gap between the measured outcomes and the cognitive claim, making the conclusion follow logically from the evidence.

**D. Any form of behavior that helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want also enables them to speak more quickly and repeat themselves less than they would if they were not engaged in that behavior.** — Reversal (22% of students)
This answer says that behavior helping speakers find phrases quickly also enables quicker speech with less repetition. Diagrammed: Helps find phrases -> Quicker speech and less repetition. The argument needs the opposite direction: Quicker speech and less repetition -> Helps find phrases. This answer provides the reverse conditional. Knowing that phrase-finding leads to better speech does not establish that better speech indicates phrase-finding. The argument observed the speech outcomes and needs to infer the cognitive cause, but this answer only confirms that the cognitive cause would produce those outcomes — which does not validate the inference in the needed direction.

**E. Of the subjects who were allowed to gesture, those who spoke the most quickly and repeated themselves least were among those who gestured the most.** — Unrelated to Goal (14% of students)
This answer says that among the gesturing subjects, those who spoke most quickly and repeated least were among those who gestured most. This describes a dose-response relationship within the gesturing group (more gesturing correlates with more fluent speech), but it says nothing about finding phrases. The conclusion is specifically about phrase retrieval, and this answer never addresses that concept. Even if more gesturing produces more fluent speech, that does not establish why fluent speech indicates finding phrases quickly. The phrase-retrieval gap remains unbridged.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** In an experiment, subjects who gestured while describing cartoons spoke more quickly and repeated themselves less than those who did not gesture.
- **Conclusion:** Gesturing helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence establishes that gesturing correlates with quicker speech and less repetition. The conclusion claims gesturing helps find phrases quickly. The gap: we need to know that speaking more quickly and repeating less indicates finding phrases quickly.
- **Goal:** Find an assumption that links "speaking quickly and repeating less" to "finding phrases quickly." Something like: behavior correlated with quicker speech and less repetition helps speakers find phrases.

**Answer choices**
**A. Ordinarily almost everyone regularly gestures when speaking quickly and with little repetition, regardless of the topic being discussed.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
This says people naturally gesture when speaking quickly, but says nothing about finding phrases. The conclusion is about phrase retrieval, and this answer does not address that concept at all.

**B. The cartoons were chosen by those who conducted the experiment and were selected from a variety of sources.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
How the cartoons were selected is a methodological detail irrelevant to whether gesturing helps find phrases. This answer does not address phrase retrieval at all.

**C. Any form of behavior correlated with quicker speech and less repetition in speech helps speakers find the phrases they want quickly.** — Correct (59% of students)
Gesturing correlates with quicker speech and less repetition (evidence). Any behavior with those correlations helps find phrases (this assumption). Therefore gesturing helps find phrases (conclusion). The bridge is complete.

**D. Any form of behavior that helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want also enables them to speak more quickly and repeat themselves less than they would if they were not engaged in that behavior.** — Reversal (22% of students)
This says: helps find phrases -> quicker speech and less repetition. We need the reverse: quicker speech and less repetition -> helps find phrases. Wrong conditional direction.

**E. Of the subjects who were allowed to gesture, those who spoke the most quickly and repeated themselves least were among those who gestured the most.** — Unrelated to Goal (14% of students)
This describes a dose-response within the gesturing group but says nothing about finding phrases. The conclusion's core claim about phrase retrieval is left unaddressed.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Scientists told 200 people to describe cartoon action scenes. Half could wave their hands around; half had to keep still. The hand-wavers talked faster and repeated themselves less.
- **Conclusion:** Gesturing helps you find the words you are looking for.
- **Evaluate:** The experiment measured talking speed and repetition. The conclusion is about finding phrases. Those are not obviously the same thing. Maybe gesturers just talk faster without actually finding better words. We need something that says "if you talk faster and repeat less, that means you are finding phrases more easily."
- **Goal:** Find the bridge that connects the measured results (speed, less repetition) to the claimed explanation (finding phrases quickly).

**Answer choices**
**A. Ordinarily almost everyone regularly gestures when speaking quickly and with little repetition, regardless of the topic being discussed.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
So when people talk fast and do not repeat themselves, they gesture? Okay, but the argument is trying to prove the opposite direction: that gesturing helps you find words. This answer describes what happens when people already speak fluently, not what gesturing does for you. And it never mentions finding phrases at all — which is the entire point of the conclusion.

**B. The cartoons were chosen by those who conducted the experiment and were selected from a variety of sources.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
The experimenters picked the cartoons from a variety of sources. Great. How does that help establish that gesturing aids phrase retrieval? It does not. This is a detail about how the study was set up, not about what the study's results mean. Knowing who chose the cartoons says exactly nothing about whether waving your hands helps you find words.

**C. Any form of behavior correlated with quicker speech and less repetition in speech helps speakers find the phrases they want quickly.** — Correct (59% of students)
Here is the missing puzzle piece: if a behavior goes hand in hand with faster, less repetitive speech, then that behavior helps you find words. Gesturing goes with faster, less repetitive speech. Therefore, gesturing helps you find words. Done. The assumption is broad — it covers any behavior with those speech correlations, not just gesturing — but broad is fine for a sufficient assumption. It just needs to be strong enough to guarantee the conclusion, and it is.

**D. Any form of behavior that helps speakers quickly find the phrases they want also enables them to speak more quickly and repeat themselves less than they would if they were not engaged in that behavior.** — Reversal (22% of students)
This says finding phrases leads to faster speech. The argument needs faster speech to indicate finding phrases. Those are different claims. It is like knowing that being hungry makes you eat, and then concluding that everyone who eats must be hungry. Wrong direction. The argument saw the speech outcomes and needs to work backward to the cause. This answer only works forward from the cause to the outcomes.

**E. Of the subjects who were allowed to gesture, those who spoke the most quickly and repeated themselves least were among those who gestured the most.** — Unrelated to Goal (14% of students)
The biggest gesturers talked the fastest and repeated the least. Cool — that strengthens the gesturing-fluency connection. But the argument is not just claiming gesturing makes you talk faster. It is claiming gesturing helps you find the words you are looking for. This answer doubles down on the speed-and-repetition data without ever touching the phrase-finding claim. More of the same evidence does not fill a different kind of gap.

---

## S2 · Q19 — Must be True

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Committee chairperson: No new course will be approved for next year’s schedule unless a proposal for it has already been received either by this committee or by Dean Wilson. Dean Wilson has received only one new course proposal, and all of the new course proposals that this committee has received are for upper-level courses. It has been decided that, beginning next year, all upper-level courses will have prerequisites.

### Prompt
Which one of the following can be properly inferred if the chairperson’s statements are true?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** No new course will be approved for next year's schedule unless a proposal for it has already been received either by this committee or by Dean Wilson. (NCA → PRC or PRDW) Dean Wilson has received only one new course proposal. (PRDW = 1) All of the new course proposals that this committee has received are for upper-level courses. (PRC → UL) Beginning next year, all upper-level courses will have prerequisites. (UL → P)
- **Evaluate:** We can combine the final two conditional statements to conclude that all of the new course proposals that this committee has received will have prerequisites. (PRC → P)

**Answer choices**
**A. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then there will be no new courses next year that have prerequisites.** — Negation (9% of students)
This answer says if there are no new upper-level courses, then no new courses will have prerequisites. We know upper-level courses have prerequisites (UL -> P), but this answer assumes the reverse: non-upper-level courses do not have prerequisites (~UL -> ~P). That is an illegal negation of the conditional. A lower-level course could also have prerequisites for reasons not discussed in the stimulus. The premises only tell us upper-level courses will have prerequisites, not that they are the only courses with prerequisites.

**B. If all of the new courses next year are upper-level courses, then all of the new course proposals submitted to the committee will have been approved.** — Reversal (8% of students)
This answer says if all new courses are upper-level, then all committee proposals were approved. We know the committee's proposals are for upper-level courses (PRC -> UL), but this answer assumes the reverse: upper-level courses came from the committee (UL -> PRC). That is an illegal reversal. Dean Wilson's proposal could also be for an upper-level course. Additionally, the committee could have received proposals without approving them. Nothing guarantees that receiving a proposal leads to approval.

**C. If there is more than one new course next year, at least one of them will be a course that has a prerequisite.** — Correct (54% of students)
If more than one new course is approved, at least two proposals were submitted. Dean Wilson received only one proposal, so he can account for at most one approved course. Any additional approved courses must have had proposals submitted to the committee. All committee proposals are for upper-level courses, and all upper-level courses have prerequisites. Therefore, at least one of the approved courses (the one that came through the committee) will have prerequisites. This follows directly from combining the premises without any unsupported inferences.

**D. If the new course proposal that Dean Wilson received is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites.** — Too Strong (26% of students)
This answer says if Dean Wilson's proposal is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites. "All courses" goes far beyond what the premises support. The premises only tell us that new upper-level courses will have prerequisites — not that existing courses will have prerequisites. There could be many continuing lower-level courses on the schedule without prerequisites. This answer massively overgeneralizes from a rule about new upper-level courses to a claim about every course offered.

**E. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then the new course proposal that was submitted to Dean Wilson will have been approved.** — Unsupported (3% of students)
This answer says if no new upper-level courses are approved, then Dean Wilson's proposal will have been approved. There is no logical connection between the absence of upper-level courses and Dean Wilson's proposal being approved. If no upper-level courses are approved, the committee proposals were rejected. But that tells us nothing about whether Dean Wilson's proposal was accepted or rejected — those are independent decisions. Dean Wilson's proposal could be rejected regardless of what happens with the committee's proposals.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** No new course is approved unless a proposal was received by the committee or by Dean Wilson. Dean Wilson received only one proposal. All proposals the committee received are for upper-level courses. All upper-level courses will have prerequisites.
- **Evaluate:** Chain: committee proposals are upper-level, and upper-level courses have prerequisites. So all committee-approved courses will have prerequisites. Dean Wilson has exactly one proposal, which may or may not be upper-level. If more than one new course is approved, at least one must have come through the committee and therefore must have prerequisites.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that must be true from combining these conditional statements. The key insight is that Dean Wilson can account for at most one non-prerequisite course.

**Answer choices**
**A. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then there will be no new courses next year that have prerequisites.** — Negation (9% of students)
This assumes ~UL -> ~P, which is an illegal negation of UL -> P. Lower-level courses could also have prerequisites. The premises do not establish that only upper-level courses have prerequisites.

**B. If all of the new courses next year are upper-level courses, then all of the new course proposals submitted to the committee will have been approved.** — Reversal (8% of students)
This reverses PRC -> UL into UL -> PRC. Upper-level courses could come through Dean Wilson too, not just the committee. And receiving a proposal does not guarantee approval.

**C. If there is more than one new course next year, at least one of them will be a course that has a prerequisite.** — Correct (54% of students)
Dean Wilson has only one proposal. If there are two or more new courses, at least one must come through the committee, making it upper-level with prerequisites. This follows directly from combining the premises.

**D. If the new course proposal that Dean Wilson received is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites.** — Too Strong (26% of students)
"All courses offered next year" is far too broad. The prerequisite rule applies to upper-level courses, not to every course on the entire schedule. Existing lower-level courses could have no prerequisites.

**E. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then the new course proposal that was submitted to Dean Wilson will have been approved.** — Unsupported (3% of students)
There is no logical link between the committee's proposals being rejected and Dean Wilson's proposal being approved. These are independent decisions with no stated connection.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Four rules: (1) new courses need a proposal from the committee or Dean Wilson, (2) the Dean got exactly one proposal, (3) everything the committee got is upper-level, and (4) upper-level means prerequisites. Chain those last two together: committee courses are upper-level, upper-level means prerequisites, so committee courses have prerequisites.
- **Evaluate:** Dean Wilson is the wild card — one proposal that could be anything. But the committee is locked down: every course from there gets prerequisites. So if more than one new course gets approved, the extras must go through the committee (the Dean only has one), and committee courses get prerequisites. At least one prerequisite course is guaranteed the moment you have two or more new courses.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that captures this: more than one new course means at least one has prerequisites.

**Answer choices**
**A. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then there will be no new courses next year that have prerequisites.** — Negation (9% of students)
All upper-level courses have prerequisites. Does that mean non-upper-level courses do not? Not at all. "All dogs are animals" does not mean "all non-dogs are not animals." A lower-level course could easily have prerequisites too — the premises just do not discuss that possibility. This answer flips the conditional and pretends it works both ways.

**B. If all of the new courses next year are upper-level courses, then all of the new course proposals submitted to the committee will have been approved.** — Reversal (8% of students)
Every committee proposal is upper-level, so every upper-level course came from the committee? That is like saying "every dog is an animal, so every animal is a dog." Dean Wilson could also have an upper-level course proposal. And even if the committee received proposals, who says they approved them all? This answer reverses the conditional and throws in an extra unsupported leap for good measure.

**C. If there is more than one new course next year, at least one of them will be a course that has a prerequisite.** — Correct (54% of students)
The Dean has one proposal in the drawer. If two new courses show up on the schedule, at least one of them did not come from the Dean — it had to go through the committee. And the committee's courses are all upper-level, and upper-level means prerequisites. So the second course (or third, or fourth) is guaranteed to have prerequisites. The Dean's one proposal might dodge the prerequisite requirement, but the committee's courses cannot. Two or more new courses means at least one gets prerequisites. The domino chain works perfectly.

**D. If the new course proposal that Dean Wilson received is for an upper-level course, then all courses offered next year will have prerequisites.** — Too Strong (26% of students)
"All courses offered next year will have prerequisites" — every single one? Intro to Basket Weaving now requires Calculus II? The premises say upper-level courses get prerequisites. Not all courses. Not lower-level courses. Not the ones that have been on the schedule for a decade. This answer takes a rule about a subset and applies it to the entire university catalog.

**E. If there are no new upper-level courses next year, then the new course proposal that was submitted to Dean Wilson will have been approved.** — Unsupported (3% of students)
So if the committee's upper-level courses do not make the cut, the Dean's proposal must get approved? Why? There is no rule that says somebody has to approve something. Maybe no new courses get approved at all. Maybe the Dean's proposal is terrible. There is zero logical connection between "committee proposals rejected" and "Dean's proposal accepted." This answer invented a relationship between two independent decisions.

---

## S2 · Q20 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
The olecranon process is a bony part of the mammalian elbow. The shorter the olecranon process, the faster the forelimbs can typically be moved. Predatory mammals must move their forelimbs very quickly when attacking prey, and thus generally have short olecranon processes. It has recently been found that the extinct mammal Megatherium had a short olecranon process. Hence, in all probability, Megatherium was a predator.

### Prompt
The argument is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Predatory mammals generally have short olecranon processes. (predator → short OP (generally)) Megatherium had a short olecranon process. (Meg → short OP)
- **Conclusion:** In all probability, Megatherium was a predator. (Meg → predator (probably))
- **Evaluate:** Let's just call Megatherium "Meg" and call the olecranon process "OP." The argument takes evidence about Meg having a short OP and jumps to a conclusion about Meg being a predator. We can reach that conclusion if we know that any extinct mammal with a short OP was probably a predator: (short OP → predator (probably)) But that's a reversal of the first premise: (predator → short OP (generally)) Here's another way of saying this: the evidence doesn't establish that most mammals with a short OP are predators. It's possible that most extinct mammals with short OPs were not predators.

**Answer choices**
**A. It fails to address the possibility that most mammals with short olecranon processes have not been predators.** — Correct (60% of students)
This answer identifies the argument's central flaw: it fails to consider that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not have been predators. The argument establishes that predators generally have short olecranon processes (predator -> short OP), but this does not mean that most mammals with short olecranon processes are predators (short OP -> predator). Many non-predatory mammals could have short olecranon processes for other reasons — digging, climbing, swimming, or defense. Without establishing that predation is the primary or dominant reason for this trait, the argument cannot conclude that Megatherium was probably a predator just because it had the trait. The answer correctly identifies the reversal of the conditional.

**B. It takes for granted that the only reason predators need to move their forelimbs quickly is to attack prey.** — Bad Assumption (25% of students)
This answer says the argument assumes the only reason predators need to move forelimbs quickly is to attack prey. But the argument does not depend on this being the only reason. The argument's conclusion is based on the link between short olecranon processes and predation — not on whether predators also move fast for other reasons. Even if predators also use fast forelimbs for chasing, defending territory, or other activities, the argument's reasoning would be equally flawed (or not) because those additional uses do not affect the reversal problem. The flaw lies in the direction of the inference, not in whether predators have multiple uses for fast limbs.

**C. It overlooks the possibility that Megatherium could have been a successful predator even if it did not have a short olecranon process.** — Irrelevant (4% of students)
This answer says the argument overlooks the possibility that Megatherium could have been a successful predator without a short olecranon process. But the argument is trying to prove that Megatherium was a predator because it had a short olecranon process. Whether Megatherium could have been a predator without this trait is irrelevant to whether having the trait indicates predation. To weaken the argument, we would need to show that having a short OP does not indicate predation — not that predation is possible without it. This answer addresses an alternative scenario that has no bearing on the argument's actual reasoning.

**D. It takes for granted that, on average, the olecranon processes of extinct mammals were no shorter than those of living mammals are.** — Irrelevant Comparison (6% of students)
This answer suggests the argument assumes extinct mammals' olecranon processes were no shorter on average than those of living mammals. But the argument's premises speak about mammals generally, not specifically about living mammals. The claim that predatory mammals have short olecranon processes is presented as a general biological principle. There is no indication that this principle applies only to living mammals. Whether extinct mammals had shorter olecranon processes on average than living ones does not affect the argument's logic — the flaw is in reversing the conditional, not in making improper comparisons between extinct and living species.

**E. It fails to address the possibility that a longer olecranon process may confer some other advantage, such as greater strength.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
This answer says the argument fails to consider that a longer olecranon process might confer advantages like greater strength. While this is biologically interesting, it is irrelevant to the argument's reasoning. The argument is about what a short olecranon process indicates, not about what a long olecranon process offers. The benefits of a long olecranon process do not affect whether a short one indicates predation. The argument's flaw is that it reverses the conditional between predation and short olecranon processes, and information about the advantages of long olecranon processes does not address this reversal.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Predatory mammals generally have short olecranon processes because they need to move forelimbs quickly when attacking. Megatherium had a short olecranon process.
- **Conclusion:** In all probability, Megatherium was a predator.
- **Evaluate:** The argument reverses the conditional. It establishes: predator -> short olecranon process (generally). From Megatherium having a short olecranon process, it concludes Megatherium was a predator. But many non-predatory mammals could also have short olecranon processes for other reasons.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that identifies the reversal: the argument fails to consider that most mammals with short olecranon processes might not be predators.

**Answer choices**
**A. It fails to address the possibility that most mammals with short olecranon processes have not been predators.** — Correct (60% of students)
The argument only shows predators tend to have short olecranon processes, not that most mammals with short olecranon processes are predators. Many non-predators could share this trait for other reasons, making the inference from trait to predation unjustified.

**B. It takes for granted that the only reason predators need to move their forelimbs quickly is to attack prey.** — Bad Assumption (25% of students)
Whether predators also use fast forelimbs for other purposes does not affect the argument's flaw. The problem is reversing predator -> short OP, not whether predators have multiple reasons for fast forelimbs.

**C. It overlooks the possibility that Megatherium could have been a successful predator even if it did not have a short olecranon process.** — Irrelevant (4% of students)
Whether Megatherium could have been a predator without a short OP is irrelevant. The argument's flaw is inferring predation from the trait, not assuming the trait is required for predation.

**D. It takes for granted that, on average, the olecranon processes of extinct mammals were no shorter than those of living mammals are.** — Irrelevant Comparison (6% of students)
The argument's premises apply to mammals generally, not specifically living ones. There is no implicit comparison between extinct and living mammals that could constitute a flaw.

**E. It fails to address the possibility that a longer olecranon process may confer some other advantage, such as greater strength.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
Benefits of a longer olecranon process are irrelevant. The argument is about what a short one indicates. Advantages of long processes do not address the reversal flaw in the argument.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Predators need fast arms, so they generally have short olecranon processes. Megatherium had a short olecranon process.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore Megatherium was probably a predator.
- **Evaluate:** Predators have short olecranon processes, so anything with a short olecranon process is a predator? That is like saying basketball players are tall, so anyone tall must play basketball. Megatherium might have had fast arms for digging, climbing, or fending off predators. The argument sees one shared trait and jumps to the explanation it likes best without considering alternatives.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that calls out the reversal: predator -> short OP does not mean short OP -> predator.

**Answer choices**
**A. It fails to address the possibility that most mammals with short olecranon processes have not been predators.** — Correct (60% of students)
Most basketball players are tall. Does that mean most tall people play basketball? Obviously not. Same logic problem here. Most predators have short olecranon processes, but maybe most mammals with short olecranon processes are diggers, climbers, or something else entirely. Megatherium — a giant ground sloth, for what it is worth — might have used those fast arms for tearing branches off trees. The argument never checked whether having the trait actually predicts predation, and this answer calls out that exact blind spot.

**B. It takes for granted that the only reason predators need to move their forelimbs quickly is to attack prey.** — Bad Assumption (25% of students)
Maybe predators also use fast arms for chasing or wrestling — so what? That just gives predators more reasons to have short olecranon processes, which actually makes the predator-to-short-OP link stronger. The argument's problem is not about what predators do with their arms. It is about whether having short olecranon processes means you are a predator. This answer is looking at the wrong side of the equation.

**C. It overlooks the possibility that Megatherium could have been a successful predator even if it did not have a short olecranon process.** — Irrelevant (4% of students)
Could Megatherium have hunted even without fast arms? Maybe, but who cares? The argument is not saying "you need a short olecranon process to be a predator." It is saying "you have a short olecranon process, so you are a predator." Those are completely different claims, and this answer is attacking the wrong one. It is like hearing someone say "you must be a doctor because you wear a white coat" and responding with "but some doctors do not wear white coats." True, irrelevant.

**D. It takes for granted that, on average, the olecranon processes of extinct mammals were no shorter than those of living mammals are.** — Irrelevant Comparison (6% of students)
Were extinct mammals' arm bones different from living ones' on average? The argument does not care. It says "predatory mammals have short olecranon processes" — all mammals, living or extinct. The argument's problem is not that it compared the wrong groups. It is that it saw a trait common in predators and assumed anything with that trait must be a predator. This answer is looking for the flaw in the wrong neighborhood.

**E. It fails to address the possibility that a longer olecranon process may confer some other advantage, such as greater strength.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
Maybe long arm bones give you Popeye arms. Cool fact, wrong conversation. The argument is about what short olecranon processes indicate, not about the perks of having long ones. Whether a longer process means you can bench-press more does not change whether a shorter process means you are a predator. This answer wandered into a different argument entirely.

---

## S2 · Q21 — Principle-Strengthen

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Essayist: Commitment to relationships or careers is commonly held to be virtuous. But all commitments should be seen as morally neutral. After all, what one is committed to might be either good or bad; for example, commitment to a relationship that benefits none of the people involved deserves no praise. Often, commitment is nothing more than involvement that has outlasted its original justification.

### Prompt
Which one of the following principles, if valid, most helps to justify the essayist’s reasoning?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Something that one is committed to might be either good or bad. Commitment to a relationship that benefits none of the people involved deserves no praise. Often, commitment is nothing more than involvement that has outlasted its original justification.
- **Conclusion:** All commitments should be seen as morally neutral.
- **Evaluate:** We can reach this conclusion if we assume that anything which has the capacity to be either good or bad should be seen as morally neutral: (could potentially be connected to good or bad → morally neutral) We can strengthen the argument by making a similar assumption about something that deserves no praise or has outlasted its original justification, since those are also descriptors that the argued applied to "commitments". (some of them deserve no praise → morally neutral) or (often outlasted original justification → morally neutral)

**Answer choices**
**A. Any commitment that is morally neutral either has outlasted its original justification or deserves no praise, or both.** — Reversal (17% of students)
This answer says anything morally neutral either has outlasted its justification or deserves no praise. Diagrammed: morally neutral -> outlasted or no praise. The argument needs the opposite direction: outlasted or no praise -> morally neutral. This answer starts with moral neutrality and derives characteristics from it, but the argument needs to start with characteristics (some commitments deserve no praise) and derive moral neutrality. The conditional runs backward relative to the argument's needs.

**B. Commitment to a relationship or career is virtuous when, but only when, the relationship or career is good.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (12% of students)
This answer says commitment is virtuous when, and only when, the relationship or career is good. This limits when commitment is virtuous but does not support the conclusion that commitment is morally neutral. If the relationship is bad, this answer tells us commitment is not virtuous — but "not virtuous" could mean bad, harmful, or neutral. The answer does not specifically support moral neutrality. It also does not address the general category of commitment; it only addresses commitment to relationships and careers. The conclusion requires all commitments to be morally neutral, and this answer points in an indeterminate direction when the commitment is to something bad.

**C. If a commitment deserves no praise, then that commitment is morally neutral.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (13% of students)
This answer says if a commitment deserves no praise, it is morally neutral. Diagrammed: deserves no praise -> morally neutral. This does connect one piece of evidence (some commitments deserve no praise) to the conclusion (morally neutral). However, the evidence only identifies one type of praiseworthy-less commitment: commitment to a relationship benefiting no one. This principle would only establish that this specific type of commitment is morally neutral, not that all commitments are. The conclusion is universal — all commitments are morally neutral — and this answer only supports the neutrality of a subset. The correct answer must bridge the gap for all commitments, not just the unpraiseworthy ones.

**D. If a commitment has outlasted its original justification, then it cannot be virtuous.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (18% of students)
This answer says if a commitment has outlasted its original justification, it cannot be virtuous. The evidence states that commitment "often" is involvement that has outlasted its justification, but not always. So this principle only covers commitments that have outlasted their justification, not all commitments. And "cannot be virtuous" does not equal "morally neutral" — a commitment that is not virtuous could still be morally negative rather than neutral. Like answer C, this gets the direction right but lacks the scope and precision needed to support the universal conclusion.

**E. All commitments are morally neutral if there are any commitments that are undeserving of praise.** — Correct (40% of students)
This answer states that all commitments are morally neutral if there are any commitments that are undeserving of praise. Diagrammed: any commitment deserves no praise -> all commitments are morally neutral. The evidence establishes that at least one type of commitment deserves no praise (commitment to a relationship benefiting no one). Applying this principle: since at least one commitment is undeserving of praise, all commitments are morally neutral. This is the only answer that bridges the gap between evidence about some commitments and a conclusion about all commitments. The principle's structure is designed to generalize from the existence of any unpraiseworthy commitment to universal moral neutrality — exactly what the essayist's reasoning requires.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** What one is committed to might be good or bad. Commitment to a harmful relationship deserves no praise. Often, commitment is just involvement that has outlasted its justification.
- **Conclusion:** All commitments should be seen as morally neutral.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence shows that some commitments are bad or undeserving of praise. The conclusion says all commitments are morally neutral. We need a principle that bridges "some commitments are bad" to "all commitments are morally neutral."
- **Goal:** Find a principle stating that if any commitments are undeserving of praise (or could be bad), then all commitments should be considered morally neutral.

**Answer choices**
**A. Any commitment that is morally neutral either has outlasted its original justification or deserves no praise, or both.** — Reversal (17% of students)
This says: morally neutral -> outlasted or no praise. The argument needs: outlasted or no praise -> morally neutral. Wrong conditional direction.

**B. Commitment to a relationship or career is virtuous when, but only when, the relationship or career is good.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (12% of students)
This says commitment is virtuous only when the career or relationship is good. But when it is bad, commitment could be harmful, negative, or neutral — this does not specifically support moral neutrality.

**C. If a commitment deserves no praise, then that commitment is morally neutral.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (13% of students)
This connects "deserves no praise" to "morally neutral," but only covers one type of commitment. The conclusion says all commitments are neutral, not just the ones that deserve no praise.

**D. If a commitment has outlasted its original justification, then it cannot be virtuous.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (18% of students)
This covers only commitments that outlasted their justification (not all commitments), and "not virtuous" does not specifically mean "morally neutral." Too narrow in scope and imprecise in conclusion.

**E. All commitments are morally neutral if there are any commitments that are undeserving of praise.** — Correct (40% of students)
If any commitment deserves no praise, then all commitments are morally neutral. The evidence shows at least one commitment deserves no praise. Therefore, all commitments are morally neutral. This perfectly bridges the some-to-all gap.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Commitments can be good or bad. Commitment to a useless relationship deserves no praise. A lot of the time, commitment is just inertia — you stuck around past the point where it made sense.
- **Conclusion:** All commitments are morally neutral.
- **Evaluate:** "Some commitments are bad" to "all commitments are neutral" is a big jump. The evidence proves commitment is not always great. The conclusion says commitment is never morally loaded — not good, not bad, just neutral. We need a principle that says something like "if even one commitment is bad, then commitment itself is morally neutral."
- **Goal:** Find the principle that makes the leap from "some commitments are praiseworthy and some are not" to "commitment as a category is morally neutral."

**Answer choices**
**A. Any commitment that is morally neutral either has outlasted its original justification or deserves no praise, or both.** — Reversal (17% of students)
If something is morally neutral, it either outlasted its justification or deserves no praise? The argument is trying to prove things are morally neutral, not figure out what follows from being morally neutral. This answer starts where the argument ends. The evidence says some commitments deserve no praise. The conclusion says all commitments are neutral. This answer says neutral things deserve no praise. That is the road from B to A when the argument needs the road from A to B.

**B. Commitment to a relationship or career is virtuous when, but only when, the relationship or career is good.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (12% of students)
So commitment is virtuous when the thing you are committed to is good, and not virtuous when it is bad? That is a reasonable-sounding principle, but it does not land on "morally neutral." Not virtuous could mean bad, useless, harmful — lots of things besides neutral. And what about commitment to something good? This answer says that is actually virtuous, which contradicts the essayist's claim that all commitments are morally neutral. This answer argues for a sliding scale; the essayist wants a flat line.

**C. If a commitment deserves no praise, then that commitment is morally neutral.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (13% of students)
Deserves no praise means morally neutral? Okay, that handles the bad commitments. But what about commitment to a relationship that is wonderful? That commitment presumably deserves praise, so this principle says nothing about it. The essayist wants all commitments to be neutral — the good ones, the bad ones, the ones that outlasted their welcome, all of them. This answer only neutralizes the losers and leaves the winners untouched. It is half a bridge.

**D. If a commitment has outlasted its original justification, then it cannot be virtuous.** — Weaker Conclusion Match (18% of students)
Commitment that has hung around too long cannot be virtuous? Sure, but "not virtuous" and "morally neutral" are different destinations. Not virtuous could mean actively bad. And "often" outlasted justification is not "always." The essayist needs a principle that covers every commitment, not just the ones that should have ended three years ago. This answer knocks some commitments off the virtuous pedestal but does not land them in the morally neutral zone, and it only addresses some commitments anyway.

**E. All commitments are morally neutral if there are any commitments that are undeserving of praise.** — Correct (40% of students)
One bad apple makes the whole barrel morally neutral? That is a wild philosophical principle, but it is exactly what the essayist needs. The evidence proves that at least one type of commitment deserves no praise. This principle says that is enough to declare all commitments morally neutral. Boom — some-to-all gap closed. The other answers tried to neutralize individual commitments one at a time and kept running out of scope. This answer does it wholesale: one unpraiseworthy commitment triggers universal neutrality for the entire category.

---

## S2 · Q22 — Sufficient Assumption

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Because of the ubiquity of television in modern households, few children today spend their free time reading stories, which lack the visual appeal of flashy television programs. Thus, few children today will develop a lifelong interest in literature.

### Prompt
The conclusion drawn above follows logically if which one of the following is assumed?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Few children today spend their free time reading stories.
- **Conclusion:** Few children today will develop a lifelong interest in literature.
- **Evaluate:** We can reach this conclusion if we assume that children who do not spend their free time reading stories will not develop a lifelong interest in literature: (~ spend free time reading → ~ lifelong interest in lit)
- **Goal:** We'll keep our eyes open for an answer containing the statement above or its contrapositive: (lifelong interest in lit → spend free time reading) We can also eliminate any answer that doesn't include the idea of developing a lifelong interest in literature.

**Answer choices**
**A. No children who spend their free time reading stories fail to develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Negation (17% of students)
This answer says no children who spend free time reading stories fail to develop a lifelong interest in literature. Cutting through the double negative: children who read -> lifelong interest. This makes reading sufficient for developing interest, but the argument needs reading to be necessary for interest, not sufficient. The argument's gap is: few read -> few develop interest. To validate this, we need: developing interest requires reading (interest -> read). This answer instead says: reading guarantees interest (read -> interest). Knowing that readers always develop interest does not tell us anything about whether non-readers might also develop interest through other means. This is the classic sufficient-necessary confusion.

**B. Only those people who currently spend their free time reading stories will develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Correct (52% of students)
This answer says only those who currently spend their free time reading stories will develop a lifelong interest in literature. The word "only" establishes reading as a necessary condition: lifelong interest -> currently reads stories. Contrapositive: does not currently read -> will not develop lifelong interest. The evidence tells us few children currently read. Combined with this assumption: few children read, and only readers develop lifelong interest, therefore few children will develop lifelong interest. The conclusion follows directly. This is the precise bridge needed: reading during childhood is the only pathway to lifelong literary interest.

**C. No children who grow up in a household that lacks a television fail to spend their free time reading stories.** — Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
This answer says children growing up without a television in the household do not fail to read stories. Parsing the double negative: no TV -> reads stories. This describes what happens in TV-free homes but says nothing about developing a lifelong interest in literature. The conclusion is about lifelong literary interest, and this answer never mentions that concept. Even if we know that TV-free children read, we still have no link between reading and developing lifelong interest. The assumption needs to connect reading behavior to the conclusion's outcome, and this answer stops at the reading step without reaching the literary interest step.

**D. Few people who watch a great deal of television develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Bad Evidence Match (22% of students)
This answer says few people who watch a great deal of television develop lifelong literary interest. The evidence states that children prefer TV over reading because of its visual appeal, but it does not say children watch "a great deal" of television. A child might watch only a small amount of TV but still find it more appealing than reading. This answer addresses heavy TV watchers, while the evidence describes a broader group — children who simply do not read, regardless of how much TV they watch. The assumption does not accurately connect to the evidence's population. Additionally, this answer does not establish reading as necessary for literary interest; it only says heavy TV watching prevents it.

**E. Few children who spend their free time reading stories watch television.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
This answer says few children who spend free time reading stories watch television. This describes reading children's TV habits but says nothing about developing lifelong literary interest. The conclusion is about whether children will develop a lifelong interest in literature, and this answer never addresses that concept. Whether young readers watch TV is irrelevant to bridging the gap between "few read" and "few will love literature." Like answer C, this answer operates entirely in the reading-and-TV-watching domain without ever reaching the literary-interest domain that the conclusion requires.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Few children today spend their free time reading stories.
- **Conclusion:** Few children today will develop a lifelong interest in literature.
- **Evaluate:** The gap: the evidence says few children read, and the conclusion says few will develop a lifelong interest in literature. We need: children who do not read will not develop a lifelong interest in literature. Equivalently: lifelong interest in literature -> reads stories (reading is necessary for developing that interest).
- **Goal:** Find the answer that makes reading stories a necessary condition for developing a lifelong interest in literature. Look for "only" language establishing reading as a requirement.

**Answer choices**
**A. No children who spend their free time reading stories fail to develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Negation (17% of students)
This says: reads -> develops interest (reading is sufficient). The argument needs: develops interest -> reads (reading is necessary). Wrong conditional direction — this does not prevent non-readers from developing literary interest through other means.

**B. Only those people who currently spend their free time reading stories will develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Correct (52% of students)
"Only those who read" makes reading necessary for lifelong interest. Contrapositive: not reading -> no lifelong interest. Since few children read, few will develop lifelong interest. The conclusion follows directly.

**C. No children who grow up in a household that lacks a television fail to spend their free time reading stories.** — Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
This says TV-free children read, but never connects reading to developing lifelong literary interest. The conclusion's key concept — lifelong interest in literature — is completely unaddressed.

**D. Few people who watch a great deal of television develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Bad Evidence Match (22% of students)
The evidence says children prefer TV over reading, not that they watch "a great deal" of television. This answer targets heavy TV watchers, a narrower group than the argument addresses. It also does not establish reading as necessary for literary interest.

**E. Few children who spend their free time reading stories watch television.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
This describes reading children's TV habits but never mentions lifelong literary interest. The conclusion's key concept is completely absent from this answer.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Kids today prefer flashy TV over reading stories. So few of them actually read in their free time.
- **Conclusion:** Few kids will develop a lifelong love of literature.
- **Evaluate:** Not reading now means not loving literature later? That is only true if reading stories as a kid is the only way to develop that love. Maybe someone discovers literature in college. Maybe a great teacher sparks interest at age thirty. The argument needs reading during childhood to be a requirement for lifelong literary interest.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that says reading stories is a necessary condition for developing lifelong literary interest. Look for "only" as the keyword.

**Answer choices**
**A. No children who spend their free time reading stories fail to develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Negation (17% of students)
Every kid who reads develops a lifelong love of literature? Heartwarming, but not what the argument needs. The argument says few kids read, so few will love literature. That only works if reading is required for loving literature. This answer says reading is sufficient for loving literature. Big difference. Under this answer, reading kids always become book lovers, but kids who never read could still discover literature later through some other path. The non-readers are the argument's concern, and this answer says nothing about them.

**B. Only those people who currently spend their free time reading stories will develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Correct (52% of students)
"Only" is the LSAT's favorite word for establishing necessary conditions, and it does its job perfectly here. Only readers become lifelong literature fans. Not reading? Not becoming a literature fan. Most kids are not reading. Therefore, most kids will not become literature fans. Chain complete. This is the exact assumption that turns "few read" into "few will love literature" — no other pathway to literary interest exists if reading is the only way in.

**C. No children who grow up in a household that lacks a television fail to spend their free time reading stories.** — Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
Kids without TVs read stories. Okay, nice to know, but the argument is not struggling to explain why kids do or do not read — the TV-ubiquity premise already covered that. The argument's problem is connecting "does not read" to "will not love literature forever." This answer addresses the wrong gap. It talks about the input (who reads) without connecting it to the output (who develops lifelong interest).

**D. Few people who watch a great deal of television develop a lifelong interest in literature.** — Bad Evidence Match (22% of students)
The stimulus says kids prefer TV over books, not that they are binge-watching eight hours a day. A kid might watch thirty minutes of TV and still not bother reading. This answer targets the heavy-TV-watching population when the argument is about the non-reading population. Those might overlap a lot, but they are not the same group. And even within the right group, this answer does not provide the missing link: that reading is required for lifelong literary interest.

**E. Few children who spend their free time reading stories watch television.** — Unrelated to Goal (5% of students)
Kids who read do not watch much TV. That is a fun sociological factoid that has absolutely nothing to do with whether anyone develops a lifelong love of literature. The argument needs to know whether reading leads to lifelong interest, not whether reading and TV watching are mutually exclusive activities. This answer is operating in the wrong conversation.

---

## S2 · Q23 — Principle-Strengthen

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Filmmaker: I use hidden cameras when filming documentaries, because people behave differently when they are aware of being filmed. Although my subjects have been told that a camera is present, they remain unaware of its location and act naturally. Hence, my documentaries are more worthwhile than documentaries in which, for example, people speak directly to the camera.

### Prompt
Which one of the following principles, if valid, most helps to justify the filmmaker’s argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The filmmaker's subjects remain unaware of the camera's location and therefore act naturally.
- **Conclusion:** The filmmaker's documentaries are more worthwhile than other documentaries such as those where people speak directly to the camera.
- **Evaluate:** The argument shifts from evidence about subjects acting naturally to a conclusion about how worthwhile a documentary is. But there could easily be other factors which influence how worthwhile a documentary is. Maybe the most worthwhile documentaries are about events that impact a large number of people. Maybe those are more worthwhile even if the people who appear in the documentary are speaking directly to the camera and not behaving in a way that's completely natural. We can strengthen this argument by stating that documentaries in which people act naturally are more worthwhile than other documentaries. (act naturally → more worthwhile)

**Answer choices**
**A. A documentary with no purpose other than authentically depicting the behavior of its human subjects can be legitimate.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (1% of students)
This answer says a documentary with no purpose other than authentically depicting human behavior can be "legitimate." Two problems. First, the filmmaker's documentary is not described as having "no purpose other than" depicting behavior — it might serve additional purposes. Second, the conclusion is about documentaries being "worthwhile," not "legitimate." Legitimacy and worthiness are different evaluative standards; a documentary can be legitimate without being worthwhile, and vice versa. This answer uses the wrong evaluative term and describes a narrower category of documentary than the filmmaker's.

**B. A documentary can be authentic only if it does not include any subjects speaking directly to a camera.** — Weaker Evidence Match / Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
This answer says a documentary can be authentic only if subjects do not speak directly to the camera. This addresses documentary authenticity, not documentary worthwhileness. Even if we established that the filmmaker's documentary is authentic and others are not, we still would not know that authenticity makes a documentary more worthwhile. The conclusion's key concept — being "more worthwhile" — is absent from this answer. Additionally, this answer addresses documentary-level authenticity, while the evidence discusses subject-level behavior (acting naturally). Being authentic as a documentary and having subjects who act naturally are related but distinct concepts.

**C. Makers of documentaries do not have a moral obligation to reveal to their subjects the location of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
This answer addresses whether filmmakers have a moral obligation to reveal camera locations. The argument is not about the ethics of hidden cameras — it is about the relative worthwhileness of documentaries. Whether the filmmaker's practice is morally permissible is a separate question from whether it produces better documentaries. This answer defends the filmmaker's method against an ethical critique that the argument never raises. The argument's gap is between natural behavior and documentary worth, not between hidden cameras and moral permissibility.

**D. The more authentically a documentary depicts its subjects, the more worthwhile it is.** — Correct (93% of students)
This answer states that the more authentically a documentary depicts its subjects, the more worthwhile it is. The filmmaker's evidence establishes that subjects act naturally when filmed with hidden cameras, meaning the documentary depicts them authentically. This principle bridges directly from authentic depiction to worthwhileness: more authentic depiction equals more worthwhile documentary. Since the filmmaker's subjects act more naturally than subjects who speak directly to the camera, the filmmaker's documentaries depict subjects more authentically, and therefore — by this principle — are more worthwhile. This is the precise bridge between the evidence (natural behavior / authentic depiction) and the conclusion (more worthwhile).

**E. Subjects of documentaries should not try to discover the locations of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
This answer says documentary subjects should not try to discover hidden camera locations. The argument does not discuss what subjects should or should not do regarding camera locations. The argument is about the relative worthwhileness of documentaries based on how naturally subjects behave. Whether subjects should or should not search for cameras is an entirely different issue from whether documentaries featuring natural behavior are more worthwhile. This answer addresses subject behavior regarding camera discovery, not the relationship between natural behavior and documentary quality.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The filmmaker's subjects remain unaware of the camera's location and therefore act naturally, unlike in documentaries where people speak directly to the camera.
- **Conclusion:** The filmmaker's documentaries are more worthwhile than those in which subjects speak directly to the camera.
- **Evaluate:** The argument jumps from "subjects act naturally" to "documentaries are more worthwhile." We need a principle linking natural behavior to documentary value.
- **Goal:** Find a principle stating that documentaries depicting subjects authentically (acting naturally) are more worthwhile than those that do not.

**Answer choices**
**A. A documentary with no purpose other than authentically depicting the behavior of its human subjects can be legitimate.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (1% of students)
The filmmaker's documentary is not limited to "no purpose other than" depicting behavior, and "legitimate" is different from "worthwhile." Both the evidence category and the evaluative standard fail to match.

**B. A documentary can be authentic only if it does not include any subjects speaking directly to a camera.** — Weaker Evidence Match / Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
This connects camera-awareness to documentary authenticity but never addresses worthwhileness. The conclusion is about documentaries being more worthwhile, and this answer does not mention that concept.

**C. Makers of documentaries do not have a moral obligation to reveal to their subjects the location of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
The argument is about documentary worthwhileness, not the ethics of hidden cameras. Moral obligation to reveal camera locations is a different question entirely from whether the resulting documentary is more worthwhile.
