**D. The more authentically a documentary depicts its subjects, the more worthwhile it is.** — Correct (93% of students)
More authentic depiction means more worthwhile. The filmmaker's hidden cameras produce more authentic depiction (subjects act naturally). Therefore the filmmaker's documentaries are more worthwhile. This bridges the exact gap between naturalness and documentary value.

**E. Subjects of documentaries should not try to discover the locations of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Whether subjects should try to find hidden cameras is irrelevant. The argument is about whether natural behavior makes documentaries more worthwhile, not about what subjects should do about camera locations.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Hidden cameras mean the subjects do not know where the camera is. They act naturally. Meanwhile, in other documentaries, people talk straight to the camera and clearly know they are being filmed.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore the hidden-camera documentaries are more worthwhile.
- **Evaluate:** The argument says "my subjects act naturally" and concludes "my documentaries are better." But why does natural behavior make a documentary better? Maybe the most worthwhile documentary is one about an important topic where people speak directly to the camera with prepared, thoughtful statements. The argument needs a principle that says natural = worthwhile.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that bridges authentic behavior to documentary quality.

**Answer choices**
**A. A documentary with no purpose other than authentically depicting the behavior of its human subjects can be legitimate.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (1% of students)
"No purpose other than" depicting behavior? The filmmaker never said that was the only goal. And "legitimate" is not "worthwhile." A documentary about paint drying could be legitimate without being worthwhile. This answer describes the wrong kind of documentary and evaluates it on the wrong scale. Two swings, two misses.

**B. A documentary can be authentic only if it does not include any subjects speaking directly to a camera.** — Weaker Evidence Match / Unrelated to Goal (4% of students)
Documentaries where people talk to the camera are not authentic? Maybe. But so what? The filmmaker is not trying to prove the documentary is authentic — the filmmaker is trying to prove it is more worthwhile. "My documentary is authentic" and "my documentary is more worthwhile" are different claims. This answer helps with the first claim but completely ignores the second, which happens to be the actual conclusion.

**C. Makers of documentaries do not have a moral obligation to reveal to their subjects the location of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
Is it okay to hide cameras? That is an ethics question, and nobody asked it. The filmmaker is arguing that hidden cameras produce better documentaries, not that hidden cameras are morally acceptable. Getting permission to hide the camera does not make the documentary more or less worthwhile. This answer is preparing for a debate about filmmaking ethics when the actual debate is about filmmaking quality.

**D. The more authentically a documentary depicts its subjects, the more worthwhile it is.** — Correct (93% of students)
More real equals more worthwhile. The filmmaker's subjects act naturally (real behavior), other documentaries have people performing for the camera (less real behavior). By this principle, real beats performative on the worthwhileness scale. The word "authentically" is the bridge between "act naturally" (the evidence) and "more worthwhile" (the conclusion). Simple, clean, and exactly the principle the argument was missing.

**E. Subjects of documentaries should not try to discover the locations of the cameras filming them.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Should documentary subjects go looking for the hidden camera? Who cares, at least for this argument? The filmmaker is not worried about subjects playing hide-and-seek with the camera. The filmmaker wants to prove that natural behavior makes better documentaries. Whether subjects should or should not hunt for cameras has nothing to do with whether the footage produced is worthwhile.

---

## S2 · Q24 — Necessary Assumption

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
The more profitable a corporation is, the more valuable its managers’ time is. As a result, it is especially costly for highly profitable corporations to have their managers spend time monitoring employees. Such corporations can save money by reducing this monitoring, as long as the employees are given strong incentives to keep working hard. So highly profitable corporations can save money by giving their employees expensive bonuses.

### Prompt
The argument requires the assumption that

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Highly profitable corporations can save money by reducing the monitoring done by managers, as long as the employees are given strong incentives to keep working hard.
- **Conclusion:** Highly profitable corporations can save money by giving their employees expensive bonuses.
- **Evaluate:** To reach this conclusion about the employees at these particular corporations, we need to assume that there is a relationship between giving them strong incentives to keep working hard and giving them expensive bonuses. We have to assume that these bonuses would motivate them to keep working hard even when the managers aren't watching them closely.

**Answer choices**
**A. only a few corporations give their employees bonuses that provide strong enough incentives for the employees to keep working hard even when they are not being monitored** — No Impact (1% of students)
This answer says only a few corporations currently give bonuses that provide strong enough incentives. The argument is about what highly profitable corporations could do to save money, not about what corporations currently do. Whether many or few corporations already use effective bonuses does not affect whether the strategy would work if implemented. The argument makes a prescriptive claim about a potential policy, and the current adoption rate of that policy is irrelevant to its potential effectiveness. Additionally, this answer does not specify whether the bonuses are expensive or whether the corporations are highly profitable.

**B. if a highly profitable corporation could save money by giving its employees expensive bonuses, it is because giving such bonuses would reduce the amount of time its managers must spend monitoring those employees** — Reversal (18% of students)
This answer says if a corporation can save money by giving bonuses, it is because bonuses reduce monitoring time. Diagrammed: saves money via bonuses -> reduces monitoring. This uses the conclusion as the trigger (sufficient condition) and provides an explanation for why it works. But the argument needs to establish that the conclusion is true, not explain why it would be true if it were. A necessary assumption should be something that, if false, would destroy the argument. This answer, if negated ("it is not necessarily because of reduced monitoring"), does not destroy the argument — the argument has already established the monitoring-reduction mechanism in its premises.

**C. the more valuable the managers’ time is at a corporation, the less likely it is that the corporation will actually have those managers spend time monitoring employees** — Opposite (if anything) (4% of students)
This answer says the more valuable managers' time is, the less likely the corporation will have managers monitor employees. At highly profitable corporations (where managers' time is most valuable), this suggests monitoring is already minimal. If monitoring is already low, there is little monitoring to reduce, which means less potential savings from the bonus strategy. This weakens rather than supports the argument. The argument depends on highly profitable corporations currently spending substantial manager time on monitoring, so there is something to save by reducing it. An answer suggesting they already do not monitor much undermines the premise that monitoring savings are available.

**D. for people who are employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard** — Correct (70% of students)
This answer states that for employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard. This is the precise bridge the argument needs. The evidence says corporations can save money by reducing monitoring if employees get strong incentives. The conclusion says they can save money by giving expensive bonuses. This assumption connects the two by establishing that expensive bonuses are strong incentives for the relevant employees. Negation test: if expensive bonuses do not constitute strong incentives for these employees, then giving bonuses would not replace the need for monitoring, and no money would be saved. The argument collapses.

**E. a highly profitable corporation can save money by reducing its managers’ monitoring of employees only if its employees are given expensive bonuses** — Too Strong (7% of students)
This answer says corporations can save money by reducing monitoring only if employees receive expensive bonuses. The word "only" makes expensive bonuses the sole method for saving money through reduced monitoring. The argument only needs bonuses to be one effective method, not the only method. Maybe strong incentives could also come from stock options, promotions, or other rewards. The argument does not need to assume bonuses are the exclusive solution — it only needs to assume they are an effective one. Negation test: "Corporations can also save money through reduced monitoring using incentives other than expensive bonuses." This does not destroy the argument because bonuses can still work even if alternatives also work.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Highly profitable corporations can save money by reducing managers' monitoring time, as long as employees are given strong incentives to keep working hard.
- **Conclusion:** Highly profitable corporations can save money by giving employees expensive bonuses.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence says strong incentives can replace monitoring. The conclusion says expensive bonuses achieve this. The gap: we need expensive bonuses to constitute strong incentives for these particular employees to keep working hard.
- **Goal:** Find the assumption that expensive bonuses are strong incentives for employees at highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced.

**Answer choices**
**A. only a few corporations give their employees bonuses that provide strong enough incentives for the employees to keep working hard even when they are not being monitored** — No Impact (1% of students)
How many corporations currently give effective bonuses does not affect whether the strategy would work if adopted. The argument is about potential savings, not current practices.

**B. if a highly profitable corporation could save money by giving its employees expensive bonuses, it is because giving such bonuses would reduce the amount of time its managers must spend monitoring those employees** — Reversal (18% of students)
This uses the conclusion (saving money via bonuses) as the trigger and explains why it works. The argument needs an assumption that helps prove the conclusion, not one that starts from the conclusion being true.

**C. the more valuable the managers’ time is at a corporation, the less likely it is that the corporation will actually have those managers spend time monitoring employees** — Opposite (if anything) (4% of students)
If valuable manager time means less monitoring already happens, there is little monitoring to cut and little money to save. This weakens the argument by suggesting the savings opportunity is already minimal.

**D. for people who are employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard** — Correct (70% of students)
Expensive bonuses are strong incentives for these employees. This bridges "strong incentives save money" (evidence) to "expensive bonuses save money" (conclusion). Without this, bonuses might not motivate employees, and monitoring would still be needed.

**E. a highly profitable corporation can save money by reducing its managers’ monitoring of employees only if its employees are given expensive bonuses** — Too Strong (7% of students)
The argument only needs expensive bonuses to be one effective method, not the only method. "Only if" makes bonuses exclusively necessary, which is stronger than what the argument requires.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** At highly profitable companies, managers' time is expensive. Monitoring employees eats that time. If you give employees strong incentives to work hard, you can cut the monitoring and save money.
- **Conclusion:** These companies can save money by handing out expensive bonuses.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence says "strong incentives" and the conclusion says "expensive bonuses." The argument assumes these are the same thing. But what if the employees at these companies do not care about bonuses? What if the bonuses cost more than the monitoring savings? The argument needs bonuses to actually motivate these specific employees enough to replace supervision.
- **Goal:** Find the assumption that connects expensive bonuses to strong incentives for employees at these corporations.

**Answer choices**
**A. only a few corporations give their employees bonuses that provide strong enough incentives for the employees to keep working hard even when they are not being monitored** — No Impact (1% of students)
Only a few companies currently do this? So what? The argument is not about what companies do — it is about what they could do. The fact that most companies have not tried expensive-bonuses-instead-of-monitoring does not mean it would not work. Maybe most companies just have not thought of it yet. Current adoption rates reveal nothing about theoretical effectiveness.

**B. if a highly profitable corporation could save money by giving its employees expensive bonuses, it is because giving such bonuses would reduce the amount of time its managers must spend monitoring those employees** — Reversal (18% of students)
If bonuses save money, it is because of reduced monitoring? The argument already explained that. The premises lay out the whole chain: managers' time is expensive, monitoring wastes it, bonuses replace monitoring. This answer just restates the argument's own logic as if it were an assumption. What the argument actually needs is the link it skipped: that expensive bonuses would actually motivate these employees. This answer starts where the argument ends.

**C. the more valuable the managers’ time is at a corporation, the less likely it is that the corporation will actually have those managers spend time monitoring employees** — Opposite (if anything) (4% of students)
So profitable companies with expensive managers already skip the monitoring? Then there is nothing to save by replacing monitoring with bonuses — the monitoring was already reduced on its own. This answer is like saying "the more valuable your time, the less you waste it." If that is true, these corporations have already solved the monitoring problem without bonuses, and the bonuses would just be an unnecessary expense. This hurts the argument, not helps it.

**D. for people who are employees of highly profitable corporations where monitoring is reduced, expensive bonuses constitute strong incentives to keep working hard** — Correct (70% of students)
The argument says "strong incentives" and the conclusion says "expensive bonuses." This answer says those are the same thing for these particular employees. Expensive bonuses motivate the workers at profitable companies to keep hustling even when the boss is not watching. Without this assumption, you could hand out gold-plated bonuses and employees might still slack off the moment monitoring stops. The bonuses have to actually work as motivation, and this answer confirms they do. Negation test: "Expensive bonuses do not motivate these employees" — argument destroyed.

**E. a highly profitable corporation can save money by reducing its managers’ monitoring of employees only if its employees are given expensive bonuses** — Too Strong (7% of students)
Bonuses are the only way? What about stock options, promotions, profit-sharing, or pizza parties? (Well, maybe not pizza parties.) The argument says bonuses work, not that nothing else works. "Only if" is exclusivity language, and the argument never claims exclusivity. If the company could also motivate employees with stock options, the argument's conclusion about bonuses would still hold. This answer demands a monopoly on motivation that the argument never requires.

---

## S2 · Q25 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Columnist: Consent forms filled out by subjects prior to their participation in tests of experimental medicines designed to treat the diseases from which they are suffering show that almost all subjects accept the risk of receiving ineffective substances. This casts doubt on the claim made by some medical ethicists that many test subjects resent being given placebos (as happens to roughly half of all test subjects) or resent being given medicines that turn out to be ineffective (as also often occurs).

### Prompt
The reasoning in the columnist’s argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Consent forms that medical test subjects fill out before medical tests show that almost all of these subjects accept the risk of receiving ineffective substances.
- **Conclusion:** This casts doubt on the claim that many test subjects resent being given placebos or resent being given medicines that turn out to be ineffective.
- **Evaluate:** It's very possible that the test subjects could accept the risk of receiving ineffective substances and then still feel resentment later on. So, the forms that they complete before the tests do not cast doubt on a claim about them feeling resentment at a later time.

**Answer choices**
**A. infers that two phenomena are associated merely from the claim that there are many instances in which both phenomena are present** — Bad Flaw Description (10% of students)
This answer describes a correlation-to-association flaw: inferring two things are associated merely because they often co-occur. The columnist's argument does not involve any claim of association or correlation between two phenomena. Instead, the columnist uses evidence about one attitude (pre-test acceptance) to cast doubt on a different attitude (post-test resentment). The reasoning error is about conflating two different time points and mental states, not about inferring association from co-occurrence. This is the wrong flaw category for this argument.

**B. uses as evidence the opinions of people who are unlikely to be qualified to make informed judgments about the scientific value of placebos** — Bad Flaw Description (5% of students)
This answer says the argument uses opinions from people unqualified to judge the scientific value of placebos. But the argument does not rely on subjects' understanding of placebos' scientific value. The evidence is about subjects' willingness to accept risk, not about their scientific expertise. The conclusion is about whether subjects feel resentment, not about whether placebos have scientific value. The argument would be equally flawed if every subject had a PhD in pharmacology — the problem is the gap between pre-test acceptance and post-test resentment, not the subjects' qualifications.

**C. uses evidence drawn from a sample that is likely to be unrepresentative** — Bad Flaw Description (9% of students)
This answer says the argument uses an unrepresentative sample. But the evidence states that "almost all subjects" accept the risk, and the subjects described are the same people the conclusion discusses — test subjects in experimental medicine trials. There is no indication that the sample is different from or unrepresentative of the relevant population. The people filling out consent forms are the same people who may or may not feel resentment later. The argument's problem is not about who was surveyed but about what the survey measures (pre-test acceptance) versus what the conclusion claims (post-test resentment).

**D. takes for granted that most test subjects do not change their attitudes toward the chance of taking ineffective substances once the substances they take turn out to be ineffective** — Correct (69% of students)
This answer identifies the argument's central flaw: it takes for granted that subjects who accept the risk of receiving ineffective substances before the test will not change their attitudes once the substances they receive turn out to be ineffective. The consent forms measure pre-test willingness to accept a risk. The resentment claim is about post-test emotional reaction to an actual outcome. These are psychologically distinct states. A subject can rationally accept a 50% chance of receiving a placebo while simultaneously feeling deep resentment when they actually receive one — especially when dealing with a serious disease. The argument assumes attitudinal consistency across the pre-test/post-test boundary, and this answer correctly identifies that unwarranted assumption.

**E. draws a conclusion that is essentially about a moral issue by appealing to evidence that is purely factual** — Bad Flaw Description (7% of students)
This answer says the argument draws a moral conclusion from purely factual evidence. But the conclusion is not a moral claim. The conclusion says the evidence "casts doubt" on the claim that subjects feel resentment — this is an empirical dispute about whether subjects experience a particular emotional state, not a moral judgment about whether they should or should not feel resentment. Both the evidence (subjects accept risk) and the conclusion (subjects probably do not resent outcomes) are factual/empirical claims. The argument stays within the empirical domain throughout. There is no is-ought transition.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Consent forms filled out before medical tests show that almost all subjects accept the risk of receiving ineffective substances.
- **Conclusion:** This casts doubt on the claim that many test subjects resent being given placebos or ineffective medicines.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence is about what subjects accept before the tests. The conclusion is about what subjects resent during or after the tests. Accepting a risk in advance does not mean you will not resent the outcome when it actually happens. The argument conflates pre-test acceptance with post-test attitude.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that identifies the temporal/attitudinal gap: the argument assumes pre-test acceptance means subjects will not later resent the actual outcome.

**Answer choices**
**A. infers that two phenomena are associated merely from the claim that there are many instances in which both phenomena are present** — Bad Flaw Description (10% of students)
The argument does not infer an association between two phenomena. It uses one attitude (pre-test acceptance) to dispute a different attitude (post-test resentment). Wrong flaw category entirely.

**B. uses as evidence the opinions of people who are unlikely to be qualified to make informed judgments about the scientific value of placebos** — Bad Flaw Description (5% of students)
The argument does not depend on subjects' scientific expertise. The flaw is about conflating pre-test risk acceptance with post-test emotional response, not about the qualifications of the people providing evidence.

**C. uses evidence drawn from a sample that is likely to be unrepresentative** — Bad Flaw Description (9% of students)
The sample is the test subjects themselves — the same population the conclusion discusses. There is no sampling problem. The flaw is about what the evidence measures, not about who it measures.

**D. takes for granted that most test subjects do not change their attitudes toward the chance of taking ineffective substances once the substances they take turn out to be ineffective** — Correct (69% of students)
The argument assumes pre-test risk acceptance means subjects will not resent the actual outcome. Accepting a risk in the abstract is psychologically different from experiencing the negative outcome. This answer correctly identifies the temporal/attitudinal gap.

**E. draws a conclusion that is essentially about a moral issue by appealing to evidence that is purely factual** — Bad Flaw Description (7% of students)
The conclusion is empirical (subjects probably do not feel resentment), not moral. Both evidence and conclusion are factual claims about subjects' attitudes. There is no fact-to-value transition.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Before the tests, subjects signed consent forms saying they accept the risk of getting a placebo or ineffective medicine. Almost all of them were fine with it.
- **Conclusion:** So the claim that many subjects resent getting placebos or ineffective medicines is doubtful.
- **Evaluate:** Signing a form before the test saying "I accept the risk" is very different from actually getting a sugar pill while your disease gets worse. Someone can accept a coin flip beforehand and still be upset when they lose. The argument assumes that pre-game acceptance translates to post-game contentment, which anyone who has ever lost a bet can tell you is not how emotions work.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that names this flaw: treating pre-test acceptance of a risk as evidence against post-test resentment about the outcome.

**Answer choices**
**A. infers that two phenomena are associated merely from the claim that there are many instances in which both phenomena are present** — Bad Flaw Description (10% of students)
This describes a "things happen together so they must be connected" flaw. But the columnist's argument is not about two things happening together. It is about one attitude (accepting risk) being used to disprove a different attitude (feeling resentment). There is no correlation being drawn, no association being inferred. The flaw is about confusing "I am okay with the possibility" with "I am okay with the reality." Wrong flavor of bad reasoning.

**B. uses as evidence the opinions of people who are unlikely to be qualified to make informed judgments about the scientific value of placebos** — Bad Flaw Description (5% of students)
Are the subjects unqualified to accept risk? They are the ones taking the risk — they are uniquely qualified to say whether they are okay with it. The issue is not "these people do not understand placebos." The issue is "being okay with a risk before it happens is not the same as being happy about it after it happens." The subjects could be Nobel Prize-winning pharmacologists and the flaw would be identical.

**C. uses evidence drawn from a sample that is likely to be unrepresentative** — Bad Flaw Description (9% of students)
Unrepresentative sample? The evidence is about medical test subjects and the conclusion is about medical test subjects. Same people. The problem is not who was asked but when they were asked and what the answers actually prove. "I accept the risk" on a form before the test does not tell you whether "I resent the outcome" after the test. The sample is fine; the measurement is the issue.

**D. takes for granted that most test subjects do not change their attitudes toward the chance of taking ineffective substances once the substances they take turn out to be ineffective** — Correct (69% of students)
Before the coin flip: "Sure, I accept a 50-50 chance." After losing the coin flip while suffering from a serious disease: "This is outrageous." Sound familiar? That is exactly what this argument ignores. Signing a form that says "I know I might get a sugar pill" is a very different emotional experience from actually getting the sugar pill while your condition worsens. The argument assumes that pre-game acceptance means post-game contentment, which is about as psychologically realistic as assuming that agreeing to play Monopoly means you will not flip the board when you land on Boardwalk with a hotel.

**E. draws a conclusion that is essentially about a moral issue by appealing to evidence that is purely factual** — Bad Flaw Description (7% of students)
Is "subjects probably do not feel resentment" a moral claim? No — it is a claim about whether people experience a particular emotion. That is psychology, not ethics. The conclusion is not saying subjects should not feel resentment or that resentment is wrong. It is saying the resentment probably does not exist. Both the evidence and conclusion are in the factual lane. The flaw here is about timing (pre-test vs. post-test attitudes), not about jumping from facts to morals.

---

# Section 3 (Logical Reasoning)

## S3 · Q1 — Strengthen

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Over the long run, apple orchards that use organic farming methods cost no more to run than, and are just as productive as, apple orchards using conventional methods. However, organically grown apples tend to be better tasting, and thus, organic orchards are likely to be more profitable than conventional orchards in the long run.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Over the long run, apple orchards that use organic farming methods cost no more to run than, and are just as productive as, apple orchards using conventional methods. Organically grown apples tend to be better tasting.
- **Conclusion:** Organic orchards are likely to be more profitable than conventional orchards in the long run.
- **Evaluate:** To reach this conclusion, we need to assume that an orchard which grows apples that taste better is guaranteed to be more profitable. `better tasting → more profitable`. This link is missing from the argument as it's written.
- **Goal:** We're looking for an answer which makes this missing link more likely to be true. We want more evidence showing that better tasting apples are more profitable.

**Answer choices**
**A. The use of organic farming methods has recently increased in the commercial apple farming industry.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
An increased use of organic farming methods does not establish that organic orchards are more profitable. There could be many reasons for the trend — environmental concerns, government subsidies, consumer pressure — none of which necessarily connect to profitability. More importantly, this answer does nothing to strengthen the specific link the argument needs: that better-tasting apples generate more revenue. The argument's gap is between taste and profit, and an industry trend toward organic methods does not bridge that gap.

**B. The apple orchards that tend to be most profitable in the long run tend to be those that are most profitable in the short run.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
This answer introduces a relationship between short-term and long-term profitability. Even if orchards that are profitable in the short term tend to remain profitable in the long term, this does not help the argument. The argument never discusses short-term profitability, and we have no information about whether organic orchards are more profitable in the short term. Without that information, this answer has no clear connection to the argument's conclusion. The gap the argument needs to bridge is between taste and profit, not between short-term and long-term profitability.

**C. Demand for a given kind of apple is based primarily on flavor.** — Correct (91% of students)
This answer directly bridges the argument's gap. The argument establishes that organic and conventional orchards have equal costs and equal productivity, with the only difference being that organic apples taste better. The conclusion that organic orchards are more profitable requires an assumption that better taste leads to higher revenue. If demand for apples is primarily based on flavor, then better-tasting organic apples will generate more demand and therefore more revenue. With equal costs and higher revenue, organic orchards would indeed be more profitable. This answer provides exactly the missing link between taste and profit.

**D. Most people prefer to buy apples that have the lowest price.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Price sensitivity among consumers does not help the argument. The argument does not provide any information about the price of organic versus conventional apples. If most people prefer the lowest price, organic orchards would need to price their apples competitively. But we do not know whether organic apples cost more, less, or the same as conventional apples at retail. This answer fails to connect to the taste-to-profit link that the argument requires. Even if organic apples were cheaper, this answer still would not explain why they are more profitable — lower prices could mean lower revenue per unit.

**E. Many consumers prefer the appearance of apples grown using conventional methods to that of apples grown organically.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Consumer preferences about the appearance of apples are not directly relevant to the argument. The argument is about taste, not appearance. There might be some relationship between how an apple looks and how it tastes, but neither the argument nor this answer establishes that connection. Without knowing whether appearance influences purchasing decisions more than taste does, or whether appearance correlates with taste, this information does not clearly strengthen or weaken the link between the superior flavor of organic apples and higher profitability. If anything, by suggesting conventional apples look better, it might slightly weaken the argument — but that depends on assumptions not provided.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Organic orchards are likely to be more profitable than conventional orchards in the long run.
- **Evidence:** Organic orchards cost no more to run and are just as productive as conventional orchards. Organically grown apples tend to be better tasting.
- **Evaluate:** The argument leaps from "better tasting" to "more profitable" without connecting taste to revenue. We need a reason to believe that better flavor translates into higher sales or higher prices.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that strengthens the link between better taste and greater profitability.

**Answer choices**
**A. The use of organic farming methods has recently increased in the commercial apple farming industry.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
A trend toward organic farming does not prove organic orchards are more profitable. The argument needs a link between taste and profit, and this answer does not provide one.

**B. The apple orchards that tend to be most profitable in the long run tend to be those that are most profitable in the short run.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
The argument says nothing about short-term profitability. Even if short-term profits predict long-term profits, we do not know whether organic orchards are more profitable in the short term. This does not help.

**C. Demand for a given kind of apple is based primarily on flavor.** — Correct (91% of students)
If consumers buy apples primarily based on flavor, better-tasting organic apples will generate more demand and revenue. With equal costs and higher revenue, organic orchards are more profitable — exactly what the conclusion claims.

**D. Most people prefer to buy apples that have the lowest price.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Price preferences do not help because the argument gives no information about apple prices. The argument needs a link between taste and profit, not between price and purchasing behavior.

**E. Many consumers prefer the appearance of apples grown using conventional methods to that of apples grown organically.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Apple appearance is a different attribute from taste. The argument is about flavor driving profit, and consumer preferences about looks do not address that link.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Organic orchards will out-earn conventional ones in the long run. The organic apples are destined for financial glory.
- **Evidence:** Organic orchards cost the same to run and produce the same amount. The only edge? The organic apples taste better.
- **Evaluate:** Costs are equal. Output is equal. So the entire profit argument hangs on taste. But since when does tasting better automatically mean making more money? Plenty of things taste great without being profitable — just ask anyone who has tried to sell homemade jam at a farmers' market. The argument needs a reason to believe that apple buyers actually care enough about flavor to open their wallets wider.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that connects deliciousness to dollars.

**Answer choices**
**A. The use of organic farming methods has recently increased in the commercial apple farming industry.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
More people are going organic — cool. But "popular" and "profitable" are not synonyms. People might switch to organic farming because they care about the environment, because regulations pushed them, or because organic sounds trendy at dinner parties. None of that establishes whether better-tasting apples actually make more money. The argument needs a taste-to-cash pipeline, and this answer is still stuck at the farm gate.

**B. The apple orchards that tend to be most profitable in the long run tend to be those that are most profitable in the short run.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
Short-term profits predicting long-term profits — fine, but who said anything about the short term? The argument is laser-focused on one thing: organic apples taste better, therefore organic orchards make more money. This answer wanders off into a different economic relationship entirely. It is answering a question nobody asked.

**C. Demand for a given kind of apple is based primarily on flavor.** — Correct (91% of students)
There it is — the missing link. If people buy apples based on flavor above all else, then the better-tasting organic apples will fly off the shelves. Same costs, same output, but more demand? That is a recipe for higher profits. The argument was one premise short of making sense, and this answer is that premise. Flavor drives demand, demand drives revenue, and revenue minus equal costs equals more profit for the organic orchard.

**D. Most people prefer to buy apples that have the lowest price.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
If everyone is bargain-hunting for the cheapest apple, then who cares how good the organic ones taste? This answer might actually hurt the argument more than help it. But setting that aside, the fundamental problem is that the argument says nothing about apple prices. The argument needs evidence that flavor sells apples, and this answer is talking about price tags instead.

**E. Many consumers prefer the appearance of apples grown using conventional methods to that of apples grown organically.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Conventional apples are prettier — noted. But the argument is about taste, not beauty pageants. Unless there is a rule that ugly apples cannot be delicious (spoiler: there is not), appearance preferences do not connect to the taste-profit link the argument needs. If anything, this suggests conventional apples have a visual advantage, which is the last thing the argument wants to hear. Wrong attribute, wrong direction.

---

## S3 · Q2 — Main Conclusion

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Banking industry visionaries foresee a bright day in the near future when customers will be able to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from the comfort of their own homes. But that may be more of a paradise for banks than for their customers. As banks eliminate their branch offices and customer-service employees, customers will have to serve as their own tellers—and pay more transaction fees for their efforts.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion of the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Evidence:** Banking industry visionaries foresee a bright day in the near future when customers will be able to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from the comfort of their own homes.
- **Conclusion:** That [transacting all financial business from home by means of computers or telephones] may be more of a paradise for banks than for their customers.
- **Evicence:** As banks eliminate their branch offices and customer-service employees, customers will have to serve as their own tellers—and pay more transaction fees for their efforts.
- **Goal:** A correct answer will indicate that conducting financial business from home by computer or telephone is not as beneficial for customers as it is for banks.

**Answer choices**
**A. In the near future, bank customers will be able to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This answer restates the opposing view presented in the first sentence of the argument — the banking industry's optimistic vision of home banking. The author introduces this view only to push back against it with "but." The conclusion is the author's own position, not the position being challenged. This answer captures the setup, not the punchline.

**B. Enabling bank customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes may be more beneficial to banks than to their customers.** — Correct (90% of students)
This answer accurately paraphrases the author's conclusion from the second sentence. The author states that home banking "may be more of a paradise for banks than for their customers." This answer restates that idea: enabling home banking "may be more beneficial to banks than to their customers." The wording is different but the meaning is identical — a comparative claim that home banking disproportionately serves banks' interests. This is exactly what a correct Main Conclusion answer should do: capture the same idea in slightly different language.

**C. As banks eliminate branch offices and customer service employees, bank customers will have to serve as their own tellers and pay more fees.** — Premise (6% of students)
This answer restates the evidence from the third sentence of the argument. The fact that customers will serve as their own tellers and pay more fees is the reason the author believes home banking benefits banks more than customers. It is the support for the conclusion, not the conclusion itself. In the argument's structure, this information explains why the author holds the view expressed in the second sentence.

**D. Eliminating branch offices and customer-service employees would benefit the banking industry.** — Too Specific (2% of students)
This answer captures only half of the conclusion. The author's point is comparative — home banking benefits banks more than customers. This answer only addresses the bank side: eliminating branches would benefit banks. It omits the customer side entirely and drops the crucial comparison. The conclusion is not just that banks will benefit; it is that banks will benefit more than customers will. Additionally, the conclusion is about the broader phenomenon of home banking, not just about eliminating branch offices specifically.

**E. Enabling customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes would allow banks to eliminate branch offices and customer-service employees.** — Word Salad (1% of students)
This answer cobbles together language from different parts of the argument without accurately representing any single claim the author makes. It connects home banking to the elimination of branches and employees, blending the opposing view (home banking) with the evidence (eliminating branches) into a causal claim the author never explicitly states. The conclusion is about who benefits more from home banking, not about what home banking enables banks to do. This answer sounds like it could be part of the argument, but it does not match the actual conclusion.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Transacting all financial business from home by computer or telephone may be more of a paradise for banks than for their customers.
- **Evidence:** As banks eliminate branch offices and customer-service employees, customers will have to serve as their own tellers and pay more transaction fees.
- **Evaluate:** The first sentence presents an opposing view — banking visionaries see home banking as a bright future. The second sentence pivots with "but" to introduce the author's conclusion. The third sentence provides evidence for that conclusion.
- **Goal:** Identify the answer that restates the conclusion: home banking benefits banks more than customers.

**Answer choices**
**A. In the near future, bank customers will be able to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This restates the first sentence — the banking visionaries' optimistic view — not the author's conclusion. The conclusion pushes back against this view.

**B. Enabling bank customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes may be more beneficial to banks than to their customers.** — Correct (90% of students)
This paraphrases the conclusion: home banking benefits banks more than customers. It matches the second sentence's meaning in different words, which is standard for correct Main Conclusion answers.

**C. As banks eliminate branch offices and customer service employees, bank customers will have to serve as their own tellers and pay more fees.** — Premise (6% of students)
This restates the evidence from the third sentence — the supporting reason for the conclusion, not the conclusion itself.

**D. Eliminating branch offices and customer-service employees would benefit the banking industry.** — Too Specific (2% of students)
This captures only the bank-benefit half of a comparative conclusion. It misses the crucial comparison — that customers benefit less than banks — and focuses too narrowly on branch closures.

**E. Enabling customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes would allow banks to eliminate branch offices and customer-service employees.** — Word Salad (1% of students)
This mixes language from the evidence and the opposing view into a causal claim the author never makes. The conclusion is about relative benefit, not about what home banking enables.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Home banking sounds great — for the banks. For customers, not so much. That is the author's actual point.
- **Evidence:** Banks get to close branches and fire employees. Customers get to do more work and pay more fees for the privilege. Some paradise.
- **Evaluate:** The structure here is a classic bait-and-switch. Sentence one paints a rosy picture — banking from the couch, the future is now. Then "but" drops like a plot twist, and the author reveals the real winners. Sentence three explains why: banks cut costs while customers pick up the slack. The conclusion is the "but" sentence, not the evidence that follows it.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that matches "home banking is a better deal for banks than for customers."

**Answer choices**
**A. In the near future, bank customers will be able to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes.** — Opposing Idea (1% of students)
This is the banking industry's sales pitch — the shiny brochure version of the future. But the author is not selling this vision; the author is poking holes in it. The conclusion comes after the "but," where the author says, essentially, "nice for the banks, not so nice for you." Picking this answer is like reading a movie review that says "some people loved this film, but it was terrible" and concluding the reviewer loved the film.

**B. Enabling bank customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes may be more beneficial to banks than to their customers.** — Correct (90% of students)
Ding ding ding. "More beneficial to banks than to their customers" is just a dressed-up version of "more of a paradise for banks than for their customers." Same idea, fancier suit. The LSAT loves to rephrase conclusions in slightly different language to see if test-takers actually understood the point rather than just memorized the words. This answer understood the assignment.

**C. As banks eliminate branch offices and customer service employees, bank customers will have to serve as their own tellers and pay more fees.** — Premise (6% of students)
This is the "here is why" part of the argument, not the "here is what the conclusion claims" part. Customers doing their own banking and paying more fees is the evidence supporting the conclusion. It is the engine, not the destination. The conclusion is "home banking is better for banks than for customers." This answer explains why that is the case.

**D. Eliminating branch offices and customer-service employees would benefit the banking industry.** — Too Specific (2% of students)
"Banks will benefit from closing branches." Sure, that is part of the picture, but it is like summarizing Romeo and Juliet as "a guy from Verona had a bad week." The actual conclusion is about the comparison — banks win, customers lose. This answer only covers the first half and zooms in on branch closures when the conclusion is about the whole home-banking arrangement.

**E. Enabling customers to transact all their financial business by means of computers or telephones from their own homes would allow banks to eliminate branch offices and customer-service employees.** — Word Salad (1% of students)
This answer raided the argument's word bank and assembled the pieces into something that sounds official but is not actually what the author concluded. "Home banking would let banks close branches" — sure, that is probably true, but the conclusion is "home banking is a better deal for banks than for customers." This answer describes the mechanics; the conclusion delivers the verdict.

## S3 · Q3 — Agree/Disagree

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Bernard: We should not invite Carl to speak at the forum. Carl's views are clearly false, and worse, dangerous. To encourage their consideration will not only legitimize them but also help to promulgate them, both of which we should avoid, since we wish not to support their adoption.

Ayla: The best way to combat false views is to challenge them in public. Once Carl's views are subjected to the kind of public scrutiny the forum provides, people will see them for what they are because the forum will provide convincing arguments against them.

### Prompt
The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Bernard and Ayla disagree about whether

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Bernard's Claims:**
  1. We should not invite Carl to speak at the forum.
  2. Carl's views are clearly false, and worse, dangerous.
  3. To encourage their consideration will not only legitimize them but also help to promulgate them.
  4. We should avoid legitimizing or promulgating Carl's views, since we wish not to support their adoption.
- **Ayla's Responses:**
  1. Disagrees
  2. no comment
  3. Disagrees
  4. no comment, possibly agrees
- **Evaluate:** Ayla doesn't explicitly state that Carl should be invited to speak at the forum. However, her statements provide support for the idea that she disagrees with Bernard's first claim and believes that Carl should be allowed to speak. Ayla also doesn't explicitly state that that we should avoid legitimizing or promulgating Carl's views and not support their adoption. However, her statements provide some support for the idea that she agrees with Bernard about this. Ayla talks about combating "false views" and about seeing Carl's views for what they are. Bernard states that Carl's views are false. We can infer that Ayla agrees. They appear to disagree about allowing Carl to speak at the forum. Bernard states that Carl should not be invited, but Ayla appears to suggest that it would be best to allow Carl to speak so that people can publicly challenge his views. "Promulgate" means to promote an idea or make it widely known. If you didn't know this when reading Bernard's statements, but had a sense that it might mean "promote" or "spread," that's totally fine.
- **Goal:** We'll look for an answer that addresses claim #1 or #3.

**Answer choices**
**A. one should always avoid legitimizing a view whose adoption one wishes not to support** — Too Strong (4% of students)
The word "always" makes this answer too strong to attribute to either speaker. Bernard argues that Carl's views should not be legitimized, but his argument is specifically about Carl's case. We cannot conclude that Bernard believes one should "always" avoid legitimizing views whose adoption one opposes. Furthermore, even if Bernard did hold this absolute position, Ayla might agree with the general principle — she just thinks that allowing Carl to speak will not actually legitimize his views but rather expose them. Their disagreement is about the consequences of public expression, not about whether one should avoid legitimizing harmful views as a general rule.

**B. people will see the falsity of every dangerous idea for which they are furnished with forceful counterarguments** — Too Strong / Half Scope (3% of students)
The word "every" makes this answer too strong. Ayla suggests that public scrutiny at the forum will produce convincing arguments against Carl's views, allowing people to see them for what they are. But we cannot conclude that Ayla believes this works for "every" dangerous idea. She may only believe this about Carl's particular views or about the particular forum being discussed. Additionally, Ayla calls Carl's views "false" but never calls them "dangerous" — that is Bernard's characterization. We do not know whether Ayla considers Carl's views dangerous, so any answer involving "dangerous" ideas is at best a half-scope match for Ayla's position.

**C. the best way to fight erroneous ideas involves allowing the public expression of these ideas** — Correct (90% of students)
This answer captures the core disagreement. Bernard believes that allowing Carl to speak at the forum would legitimize and help spread his views — meaning public expression of erroneous ideas is counterproductive. Ayla believes the opposite: "the best way to combat false views is to challenge them in public," which requires allowing their public expression. Bernard opposes public expression of false views as a means of fighting them; Ayla endorses it. This is a clean disagreement where both speakers hold clear, opposing positions on whether allowing public expression of erroneous ideas is an effective strategy for combating them.

**D. people who hold false views tend to pose a danger to society** — Too Strong / Half Scope (1% of students)
Bernard says Carl's views are "false, and worse, dangerous," but this does not mean he believes there is a general tendency for people with false views to pose a danger to society. He is making a specific claim about Carl, not a generalization about all people with false views. Even if we could attribute this general belief to Bernard, we do not know Ayla's position on it. Ayla acknowledges that Carl's views are false but never addresses whether false views generally make their holders dangerous to society. Without clear positions from both speakers, this cannot be the point at issue.

**E. one should not encourage the adoption of dangerous views** — Half Scope (1% of students)
Both Bernard and Ayla appear to agree with this statement, or at least neither clearly disagrees. Bernard argues against legitimizing and promulgating Carl's views "since we wish not to support their adoption," which aligns with not encouraging adoption of dangerous views. Ayla discusses combating "false views" and seeing them "for what they are," which also suggests she opposes their adoption. Since both speakers appear to be on the same side of this proposition, it is not a point of disagreement. Additionally, Ayla never uses the word "dangerous" — she discusses "false views" — so attributing a position about "dangerous" views to her is unsupported.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Bernard: Carl should not be invited to speak. Ayla: The best way to combat false views is to challenge them publicly.
- **Evidence:** Bernard argues that allowing Carl to speak would legitimize and promulgate his dangerous views. Ayla argues that public scrutiny will expose Carl's views for what they are.
- **Evaluate:** Bernard and Ayla agree that Carl's views are false. They disagree about whether allowing public expression of those views is the best way to fight them. Bernard says no — public expression legitimizes. Ayla says yes — public expression allows refutation.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that captures their disagreement: whether publicly expressing false views is the best way to combat them.

**Answer choices**
**A. one should always avoid legitimizing a view whose adoption one wishes not to support** — Too Strong (4% of students)
"Always" is too strong. Bernard's argument is about Carl's specific case, and even if he holds a broader principle, Ayla might agree with it while disagreeing about whether speaking at the forum actually legitimizes views.

**B. people will see the falsity of every dangerous idea for which they are furnished with forceful counterarguments** — Too Strong / Half Scope (3% of students)
"Every" is too strong for Ayla's position, and "dangerous" is Bernard's word, not Ayla's. Ayla discusses "false views" and may not consider Carl's views dangerous at all.

**C. the best way to fight erroneous ideas involves allowing the public expression of these ideas** — Correct (90% of students)
Bernard thinks public expression of Carl's views legitimizes them. Ayla thinks public expression lets people refute them. They directly disagree about whether allowing public expression of erroneous ideas is an effective way to fight them.

**D. people who hold false views tend to pose a danger to society** — Too Strong / Half Scope (1% of students)
Bernard says Carl's views are false and dangerous, but that is specific to Carl — not a claim about people with false views generally. And Ayla never addresses whether false views make people dangerous to society.

**E. one should not encourage the adoption of dangerous views** — Half Scope (1% of students)
Both speakers likely agree that adoption of dangerous views should not be encouraged. Ayla also discusses "false views," not "dangerous" ones, so her position on dangerous views specifically is unclear.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Bernard: Do not let Carl near a microphone. Ayla: Hand Carl the microphone and let the audience destroy his arguments.
- **Evidence:** Bernard thinks giving Carl a platform legitimizes his nonsense. Ayla thinks giving Carl a platform lets everyone see the nonsense for what it is. Classic "sunlight is the best disinfectant" versus "do not give bad ideas oxygen" debate.
- **Evaluate:** They agree on one thing: Carl's views are wrong. They disagree on strategy. Bernard is the "ban him from the conference" camp. Ayla is the "let him speak so we can publicly dismantle his arguments" camp. Neither one thinks Carl is right — they just disagree about the best tactical response to wrongness.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that nails the actual disagreement: whether letting someone publicly air false views is the best way to fight those views.

**Answer choices**
**A. one should always avoid legitimizing a view whose adoption one wishes not to support** — Too Strong (4% of students)
"Always" is the answer's downfall. Bernard says not to legitimize Carl's views in this situation, but he never declared a universal law of anti-legitimization. And Ayla might completely agree with the principle — she just thinks a public forum is where bad ideas go to die, not where they get legitimized. Their fight is about strategy, not about whether legitimizing bad views is generally undesirable.

**B. people will see the falsity of every dangerous idea for which they are furnished with forceful counterarguments** — Too Strong / Half Scope (3% of students)
"Every dangerous idea" — whoa, slow down. Ayla said public scrutiny would work on Carl's views at this forum. She did not declare a universal law covering every bad idea in existence. Plus, "dangerous" is Bernard's label, not Ayla's. She called them "false," which is a different complaint. For all the passage reveals, Ayla thinks Carl's views are as dangerous as a kitten with a yarn ball — just wrong, not threatening.

**C. the best way to fight erroneous ideas involves allowing the public expression of these ideas** — Correct (90% of students)
This is the debate in a nutshell. Bernard says "keep Carl off the stage — public expression legitimizes bad ideas." Ayla says "put Carl on the stage — public expression demolishes bad ideas." Same question, opposite answers. Should false views be aired publicly to fight them? Bernard says absolutely not. Ayla says it is literally the best strategy available. Clean disagreement, no ambiguity.

**D. people who hold false views tend to pose a danger to society** — Too Strong / Half Scope (1% of students)
Bernard thinks Carl's views are dangerous. That is about Carl specifically — not a thesis statement about everyone who has ever been wrong posing a threat to civilization. And Ayla? She never touches the "dangerous" question at all. She is focused on "false," which is a different word with a different meaning. No clear positions from both speakers means no point at issue.

**E. one should not encourage the adoption of dangerous views** — Half Scope (1% of students)
This is the one thing they actually agree on. Bernard does not want dangerous views adopted. Ayla does not want false views adopted. They are on the same team with the same goal — they just disagree about the playbook. Also, "dangerous" is Bernard's word, not Ayla's. She sticks to "false." So even if they did disagree here, there is no way to confirm Ayla's position on "dangerous" views specifically.

---

## S3 · Q4 — Principle-Strengthen

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Principle: It is unethical for someone who has bought an item to return it to the store after getting all the use from it that he or she ever intended to get.

Application: James purchased a video camera and returned it to the store two weeks later. This return was unethical.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most justifies the above application of the principle?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Principle:** If someone buys an item, gets all of the use from it they intended to get, and returns it, then they are acting in an unethical way: `buy, use fully, and return → unethical`
- **Evicence in Application:** James bought an item and later returned it.
- **Conclusion:** The return was unethical.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence in the application doesn't state why James returned the video camera. The principle only allows us to conclude that the return was unethical if we have evidence that James got all of the use from the camera that he intended to get.
- **Goal:** Look for an answer indicating that James got all of the use from the cameral that he intended.

**Answer choices**
**A. Before returning the camera, James used it extensively to record members of his family engaged in funny activities.** — Bad Trigger Match (6% of students)
Using the camera "extensively" to record family members does not establish that James got all the use he intended from it. Extensive use is not the same as complete intended use. He might have bought the camera for a different purpose entirely, and the family recordings were just a bonus. Or he might have intended to use the camera extensively for many different occasions over a long period, making the family recordings only a fraction of his intended use. This answer provides evidence of significant use but not evidence that his intended use was fulfilled — which is what the principle requires.

**B. James bought the camera to avoid having to hire a professional videographer for his sister's graduation party, and he returned the camera immediately after the party.** — Correct (90% of students)
This answer establishes exactly what the principle requires. James bought the camera specifically to avoid hiring a videographer for his sister's graduation party. He returned the camera immediately after the party. This means James's entire intended use — recording the graduation party — was complete before the return. He got all the use he ever planned to get from the camera and then returned it, which satisfies all three conditions of the principle: purchase, fulfillment of intended use, and return. The return is therefore unethical under the stated principle.

**C. James used the camera several times but returned it after he had dropped it and then discovered that some of the features did not work.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
This answer does not tell us whether James got all his intended use from the camera. He used it several times, but the return was prompted by dropping it and discovering broken features — not by having completed his intended use. If anything, this suggests James returned the camera because it no longer worked properly, which is a legitimate reason for a return that falls outside the principle's scope. The principle addresses returns after getting full intended use, not returns due to product malfunction. The ethics of returning a damaged item is a separate question the principle does not address.

**D. James returned the camera after he discovered that the same model was on sale at another store at a considerably lower price.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
James returned the camera because he found a lower price at another store, not because he completed his intended use. This does not tell us whether James used the camera at all, let alone whether he got all his intended use from it. The ethics of returning an item to repurchase it at a lower price is an entirely different question from the one the principle addresses. The principle is about returning items after getting full intended use, not about price-matching strategies.

**E. James used the camera in the manner for which he bought it, and, though he generally enjoyed it, he thought a different one might work better with his new computer.** — Bad Trigger Match (2% of students)
This answer is cleverly worded but falls short. "Used the camera in the manner for which he bought it" is not the same as "got all the use from it that he ever intended to get." Consider someone who buys a camera to record an entire YouTube series. Recording one episode is using it "in the manner intended," but it is not getting all the intended use. The principle requires that the full scope of intended use be completed, not merely that the item was used for its intended purpose. James used the camera as intended but thought a different one would work better — suggesting he planned to continue using a camera, meaning his intended use was not fulfilled.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** James's return of the video camera was unethical.
- **Evidence:** The principle: it is unethical to return an item after getting all intended use from it. James bought a video camera and returned it two weeks later.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence only says James bought and returned a camera. It does not say he got all his intended use from it. To justify applying the principle, we need evidence that James used the camera for its intended purpose and then returned it.
- **Goal:** Find an answer showing that James got all the use from the camera that he intended before returning it.

**Answer choices**
**A. Before returning the camera, James used it extensively to record members of his family engaged in funny activities.** — Bad Trigger Match (6% of students)
Extensive use does not mean James got all his intended use. He could have planned to use the camera for much more than just recording family activities.

**B. James bought the camera to avoid having to hire a professional videographer for his sister's graduation party, and he returned the camera immediately after the party.** — Correct (90% of students)
James bought the camera specifically for the graduation party and returned it right after. His intended use was completely fulfilled before the return, satisfying all conditions of the principle.

**C. James used the camera several times but returned it after he had dropped it and then discovered that some of the features did not work.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
James returned the camera because it broke after being dropped, not because he finished his intended use. Returning a malfunctioning product falls outside this principle's scope.

**D. James returned the camera after he discovered that the same model was on sale at another store at a considerably lower price.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
James returned the camera for a price reason, not because he completed his intended use. This does not satisfy the principle's conditions and raises a different ethical question entirely.

**E. James used the camera in the manner for which he bought it, and, though he generally enjoyed it, he thought a different one might work better with his new computer.** — Bad Trigger Match (2% of students)
"Used in the manner intended" is not the same as "got all intended use." James could have intended much more use of the same type. The fact that he wants a different camera suggests ongoing intended use that was not fulfilled.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** James returned a video camera and that makes him unethical. The moral police have spoken.
- **Evidence:** The principle: buying something, using it for everything you planned, and then returning it is wrong. James bought a camera and brought it back two weeks later.
- **Evaluate:** Hold on — the principle requires that James got all the use he wanted from the camera. The evidence just says he bought it and returned it. Maybe the camera broke. Maybe he changed his mind. Maybe he realized he is not the aspiring filmmaker he thought. Without knowing that James fulfilled his intended purpose, the principle cannot be applied. The answer needs to close this gap.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that proves James used the camera for exactly what he planned and then returned it — making the return clearly unethical under the principle.

**Answer choices**
**A. Before returning the camera, James used it extensively to record members of his family engaged in funny activities.** — Bad Trigger Match (6% of students)
James recorded his family doing funny things — extensively. Good for James. But "used it a lot" and "got everything I wanted from it" are different claims. Maybe James bought the camera for a year-long vlogging project and the family videos were just the appetizer. Extensive use is not the same as mission accomplished.

**B. James bought the camera to avoid having to hire a professional videographer for his sister's graduation party, and he returned the camera immediately after the party.** — Correct (90% of students)
James bought the camera for one reason: film his sister's graduation party without paying a videographer. Party happens, James films it, James returns the camera the next morning. That is textbook "got all the use I intended and then returned it." He essentially rented a camera for free using the store's return policy as his personal equipment library. The principle was practically written for this exact scenario.

**C. James used the camera several times but returned it after he had dropped it and then discovered that some of the features did not work.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
James dropped the camera and it broke. Then he returned it. That is a completely different ethical question from the one the principle addresses. The principle is about people who use something for exactly what they wanted and then return it like they are running a free rental service. James is returning a broken product, which is just normal consumer behavior. Wrong ethical dilemma entirely.

**D. James returned the camera after he discovered that the same model was on sale at another store at a considerably lower price.** — Bad Trigger Match / Out of Scope (1% of students)
James found the same camera cheaper at another store and returned the first one. Savvy shopping, maybe. Relevant to this principle? Not remotely. The principle cares about whether James got all his intended use before returning the camera, and this answer does not reveal whether James even turned the camera on. Price-hunting and use-fulfillment are completely different conversations.

**E. James used the camera in the manner for which he bought it, and, though he generally enjoyed it, he thought a different one might work better with his new computer.** — Bad Trigger Match (2% of students)
This one is the trap. "Used it in the manner for which he bought it" sounds like a match for the principle, but it is not. Think of it this way: if James bought the camera to vlog for a year, recording one video is using it "in the manner intended." But he intended to record 365 videos, not one. Using something correctly is different from using it completely. Plus, James wants a different camera that works better with his computer — which means he still plans to use a camera. His intended use is not finished; he just wants a better tool for it.

---

## S3 · Q5 — Principle-Conform

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Taylorism, the early-twentieth-century industrial efficiency movement pioneered by Frederick Taylor, has had a profound effect on industrialized societies. Increased productivity resulting from greater efficiency has led to increases in most workers' standards of living. At the same time, Taylor's methods have tended to make these workers' jobs routine and repetitive, thereby robbing their work of some of the intrinsic worth it possessed when it emphasized creativity and talent.

### Prompt
The situation described above most closely conforms to which one of the following generalizations?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Idea:** Increased productivity resulting from greater efficiency [by using Taylor's methods] has led to increases in most workers' standards of living.
- **Evidence:** Taylor's methods have tended to make these workers' jobs routine and repetitive.
- **Conclusion:** Taylor's methods have robbing their work of some of the intrinsic worth it possessed when it emphasized creativity and talent.
- **Evaluate:** It's important to notice the leap from "routine and repetitive" in the evidence to the claim in the conclusion about "robbing their work of some of the intrinsic worth it possessed." The argument is making an assumption here. Does making work more routine and repetitive necessarily rob it of intrinsic worth? Intrinsic worth for whom? The workers? "Routine and repetitive" work can be simple and easy. Lots of people don't mind getting paid to do simple, easy work. And we were told that workers' standard of living has increased, so there's no evidence in this argument that workers are being paid poorly for this "routine and repetitive" work. Does the work have less intrinsic worth for the employers? The argument states that greater efficiency has resulted in increased productivity. We don't know if employers have anything to complain about here. Work is not guaranteed to be less worthwhile, for either the workers or their employers, just because the work has become routine and repetitive.
- **Goal:** A correct answer will be a generalization supporting the idea that, in spite of the benefits of Taylorism—greater productivity and efficiency, and a higher standard of living for workers—the work being done has also become less worthwhile.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sometimes measures that appear to promote efficiency are actually deleterious to it.** — Bad Conclusion Match (2% of students)
The word "it" at the end of this answer refers back to "efficiency." This answer claims that some measures that appear to promote efficiency are actually harmful to efficiency itself. But the stimulus does not claim that Taylorism harms efficiency — quite the opposite. The stimulus explicitly states that Taylorism resulted in "greater efficiency" and "increased productivity." The negative consequence of Taylorism described in the stimulus is reduced intrinsic worth of work, not reduced efficiency. This answer misidentifies what was harmed.

**B. Some developments in industry can benefit workers financially while making their work less rewarding in other respects.** — Correct (86% of students)
This answer perfectly captures the dual nature of Taylorism as described in the stimulus. Taylorism was a "development in industry" that benefited workers "financially" by increasing their standard of living, while simultaneously making their work "less rewarding in other respects" by stripping it of creativity and intrinsic worth. The phrase "in other respects" correctly identifies that the negative impact was non-financial — it was about the quality and meaningfulness of the work itself. This generalization fits the specific situation described in the stimulus like a glove.

**C. Increased efficiency in industrial settings is sometimes accompanied by decreases in the amount of effort workers devote to their jobs.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
This answer claims that increased efficiency is "sometimes accompanied by decreases in the amount of effort workers devote to their jobs." But the stimulus does not describe workers devoting less effort — it describes their jobs becoming routine and repetitive. These are different things. A job can be routine and repetitive while still requiring significant effort. The stimulus says Taylorism changed the character of the work, not the effort workers put into it. Additionally, this answer frames the decreased effort as something workers choose, which is not how the stimulus describes the situation — Taylorism changed the nature of the jobs, not the workers' level of engagement.

**D. An increase in workers' standards of living will likely involve an accompanying decrease in how much they value their jobs overall.** — Bad Evidence Match (6% of students)
This answer claims that an increase in workers' standard of living "will likely involve" a decrease in how much they value their jobs.
