But the stimulus describes a more specific causal structure: both the increased standard of living and the decreased intrinsic worth of work were caused by a third factor — Taylorism. This answer frames the relationship as direct causation between the two outcomes, omitting the common cause. Additionally, "how much they value their jobs overall" is broader than what the stimulus describes — the stimulus identifies a specific reduction in intrinsic worth related to creativity and talent, not a general decrease in job valuation.

**E. Workers who have greater than average creativity and talent are sometimes less efficient than workers who have average creativity and talent.** — Unsupported Comparison (1% of students)
The stimulus does not compare creative workers to non-creative workers in terms of their efficiency. It states that Taylorism reduced the role of creativity and talent in workers' jobs. That is a change in the nature of work over time, not a comparison between different types of workers at the same time. Nothing in the stimulus supports the claim that more creative workers are less efficient than average workers. The stimulus is about a tradeoff between financial benefit and intrinsic work quality, not about relative efficiency levels of different worker types.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Taylor's methods have robbed workers' jobs of some of their intrinsic worth by making them routine and repetitive.
- **Evidence:** Taylorism increased productivity and most workers' standards of living, but made jobs routine and repetitive, reducing the creativity and talent previously involved.
- **Evaluate:** The situation describes a development that produced both a financial benefit (higher standard of living) and a non-financial cost (less rewarding work). A correct generalization will capture this dual-edged quality.
- **Goal:** Find a general principle that matches: a single development can benefit workers financially while making their work less fulfilling.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sometimes measures that appear to promote efficiency are actually deleterious to it.** — Bad Conclusion Match (2% of students)
The stimulus does not claim Taylorism harmed efficiency — it explicitly says efficiency increased. The negative consequence was to the intrinsic worth of work, not to efficiency itself.

**B. Some developments in industry can benefit workers financially while making their work less rewarding in other respects.** — Correct (86% of students)
Taylorism benefited workers financially (higher standard of living) while making work less rewarding (routine, repetitive, less creative). This answer captures both sides of that tradeoff perfectly.

**C. Increased efficiency in industrial settings is sometimes accompanied by decreases in the amount of effort workers devote to their jobs.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
The stimulus says jobs became routine and repetitive, not that workers put in less effort. Routine work can still require significant effort. This answer mischaracterizes the nature of the change.

**D. An increase in workers' standards of living will likely involve an accompanying decrease in how much they value their jobs overall.** — Bad Evidence Match (6% of students)
In the stimulus, both effects — higher pay and less rewarding work — were caused by Taylorism. This answer incorrectly suggests a direct causal link between higher standards of living and lower job valuation, missing the common cause.

**E. Workers who have greater than average creativity and talent are sometimes less efficient than workers who have average creativity and talent.** — Unsupported Comparison (1% of students)
The stimulus does not compare creative workers' efficiency to average workers' efficiency. It describes a historical change in the nature of work, not a comparison between types of workers.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Taylor's efficiency methods sucked the soul out of work. Jobs became repetitive assembly-line tedium, robbing them of the creativity and talent they once required.
- **Evidence:** On the bright side: workers make more money. On the not-so-bright side: their jobs are now mind-numbingly boring. Taylorism gave workers bigger paychecks and smaller reasons to care about what they do all day.
- **Evaluate:** This is a classic "good news, bad news" scenario. Good news: higher standard of living. Bad news: the work itself became a creativity-free zone. The correct generalization needs to capture both halves — something that helps workers financially while hurting the quality of their work experience.
- **Goal:** Find the principle that says: sometimes getting richer at work means enjoying work less.

**Answer choices**
**A. Sometimes measures that appear to promote efficiency are actually deleterious to it.** — Bad Conclusion Match (2% of students)
This answer says efficiency measures can backfire and actually reduce efficiency. But Taylorism did not reduce efficiency — it supercharged it. The problem is that efficiency came at the cost of meaningful work, not that efficiency failed. This answer is describing a boomerang effect that simply did not happen in the stimulus. Wrong target for the criticism.

**B. Some developments in industry can benefit workers financially while making their work less rewarding in other respects.** — Correct (86% of students)
Nailed it. Taylorism made workers richer but their jobs duller. This answer says exactly that in general terms: some industrial developments pad the wallet while draining the soul. Financial benefit, check. Less rewarding work, check. "In other respects" correctly identifies that the downside was not about money — it was about the work itself becoming a creativity desert. Clean match, no loose ends.

**C. Increased efficiency in industrial settings is sometimes accompanied by decreases in the amount of effort workers devote to their jobs.** — Bad Evidence Match (4% of students)
This answer says workers started putting in less effort. The stimulus says their jobs became boring. Those are not the same thing. A factory worker doing the same motion 10,000 times a day is exerting plenty of effort — the work is just mind-numbingly repetitive. Taylorism changed what the work looked like, not how hard people were working. This answer confuses "the job got duller" with "workers got lazier."

**D. An increase in workers' standards of living will likely involve an accompanying decrease in how much they value their jobs overall.** — Bad Evidence Match (6% of students)
This answer says getting a raise makes people like their jobs less. That is not what happened. Taylorism did both things simultaneously — it was the common cause of both the financial improvement and the creative decline. The higher standard of living did not cause the boring work; Taylorism caused both independently. This answer draws a direct causal arrow between the two effects when it should be pointing both arrows back at their shared origin.

**E. Workers who have greater than average creativity and talent are sometimes less efficient than workers who have average creativity and talent.** — Unsupported Comparison (1% of students)
Where did this comparison come from? The stimulus never pits creative workers against average workers in an efficiency contest. It says Taylorism changed what jobs look like — less creativity, more repetition. That is about how work evolved, not about which workers are faster. This answer invented a comparison that the stimulus never made and then tried to generalize it as a principle. A swing and a miss.

---

## S3 · Q6 — Weaken

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Winchester Township cannot afford to keep its several small branch libraries supplied with a wide selection of current books. However, if the branch libraries were closed, then Winchester could afford to open one large library, which could carry broader and more current selections. Thus, Winchester would better meet its residents' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the argument above?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** If the branch libraries were closed, then Winchester could afford to open one large library. The large library could carry broader and more current selections [books].
- **Conclusion:** Winchester would better meet its residents' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library.
- **Evaluate:** The conclusion makes a recommendation about building a new library. It does this by introducing a new concept of better meeting residents' needs. A couple of assumptions are required to reach this conclusion. First, the argument assumes that one large library would better meet residents' needs than several smaller ones. We can easily envision a situation where it's convenient for residents to visit the branch libraries that are close to their neighborhoods, while a single main library would be farther away and therefore less accessible. The argument also assumes that a broader and more current selection of books would better meet residents' needs. But people use libraries for many reasons other than borrowing books. Libraries often have computers, study rooms, and meeting rooms that residents can use. Libraries often offer classes and events. And people are likely to borrow older books from libraries, not just current ones.
- **Goal:** A correct answer is likely to focus on one of these unintended consequences of closing branch libraries and having one large library, a consequence which would prevent one large library from serving residents better than the existing branch libraries.

**Answer choices**
**A. Many Winchester residents never use the branch libraries because of the irregular hours each branch keeps.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
If many residents never use the branch libraries because of irregular hours, this actually supports closing them. Residents who are not using the branches have nothing to lose from their closure and might gain access to a larger library with more consistent hours. This answer strengthens the argument for consolidation by suggesting that the branch libraries are already failing to serve some residents.

**B. Most Winchester residents have complained about the selection of books at the branch libraries.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
If residents are already complaining about the book selection at branch libraries, this supports the argument for a larger library with better selection. The complaints validate the argument's premise that branch libraries are inadequately supplied and suggest residents would welcome a better-stocked alternative.

**C. The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.** — Correct (88% of students)
This answer identifies the key unintended consequence of consolidation. If the only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents, then the improved book selection becomes irrelevant for the majority of the community. A library that people cannot easily reach does not meet their needs regardless of how well-stocked it is. The branch libraries, despite their limited selections, at least provided convenient local access. Replacing accessible but poorly supplied libraries with an inaccessible but well-supplied library could easily leave residents worse off overall.

**D. It would cost Winchester a significant amount of money to build a larger library.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
The argument already accounts for the financial dimension by stating that closing branches would allow Winchester to afford a larger library. The word "significant" does not tell us whether the cost would exceed the savings from closing branch libraries. Without information about whether the cost exceeds available resources, this answer does not clearly weaken the argument.

**E. Each of Winchester's branch libraries attempts to cater to the tastes of the residents of the entire township.** — Too Weak (5% of students)
The phrase "attempts to cater" significantly weakens this answer. If each branch library merely attempts to serve the entire township's tastes, that does not mean it succeeds. The branch libraries could be attempting and failing to meet diverse tastes, which would actually support the argument for a larger, better-stocked library. This answer would be stronger if it said each branch library successfully caters to local neighborhood tastes.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Winchester would better meet its residents' needs by closing its small branch libraries and opening one larger, well-supplied library.
- **Evidence:** Winchester cannot afford to keep branch libraries well-supplied. Closing them would fund one large library with broader and more current book selections.
- **Evaluate:** The argument assumes a larger library would better meet residents' needs. But "better meeting needs" involves more than book selection — accessibility matters too. If the single library is far from most residents, the improved selection could be offset by reduced convenience.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that identifies an unintended consequence of consolidating into one library — most likely an accessibility problem.

**Answer choices**
**A. Many Winchester residents never use the branch libraries because of the irregular hours each branch keeps.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
If residents are not using branches due to irregular hours, closing them causes less disruption. This supports rather than undermines the consolidation plan.

**B. Most Winchester residents have complained about the selection of books at the branch libraries.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
Complaints about branch library selections support the case for a larger library with a better collection. This strengthens the argument.

**C. The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.** — Correct (88% of students)
If the only possible location for the new library is hard for most residents to reach, better books do not matter — people cannot use a library they cannot get to. This directly undermines the claim that consolidation would better meet needs.

**D. It would cost Winchester a significant amount of money to build a larger library.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
The argument already says closing branches would fund the new library. "Significant" cost does not tell us whether it exceeds available resources.

**E. Each of Winchester's branch libraries attempts to cater to the tastes of the residents of the entire township.** — Too Weak (5% of students)
"Attempts to cater" is too weak — attempting is not succeeding. The branch libraries could be trying and failing, which would support rather than undermine consolidation.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Close all the little libraries, build one big one. Problem solved — or so the argument claims.
- **Evidence:** The branch libraries cannot afford good books. A single large library could. More books, more current titles, happier residents. At least, that is the theory.
- **Evaluate:** There is a small problem with the "one big library fixes everything" plan: people have to actually get there. Multiple small libraries mean most residents have one nearby. One large library might be a trek for the majority of the town. A library with the world's best book collection does not help anyone who cannot reach it. The argument treats "better books" as the only thing residents need, ignoring the radical concept of convenience.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that rains on the consolidation parade — most likely by pointing out that the new library would be inconveniently located.

**Answer choices**
**A. Many Winchester residents never use the branch libraries because of the irregular hours each branch keeps.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
People are not using the branch libraries anyway because the hours are all over the place. So closing them is... a problem? This is practically an argument for consolidation. "The branch libraries are so inconvenient that nobody goes there" is not a compelling reason to keep them open.

**B. Most Winchester residents have complained about the selection of books at the branch libraries.** — Opposite (if anything) (1% of students)
Residents are already complaining about the books at the branch libraries. So the argument says "let us build a bigger library with better books" and this answer replies "people hate the current book selection." That is not a weakness — that is the argument's best supporting evidence.

**C. The only possible site for a new library is not readily accessible to most Winchester residents.** — Correct (88% of students)
The beautiful new library with its magnificent collection of current titles is located in... the one spot nobody can get to. What good is the world's greatest library if most residents need a GPS and a packed lunch to reach it? The branch libraries might have had dusty old books, but at least people could actually walk in the door.

**D. It would cost Winchester a significant amount of money to build a larger library.** — Out of Scope (5% of students)
"It would cost a lot" — but the argument already said Winchester could afford it if the branches close. "Significant" sounds alarming but reveals nothing specific. Maybe it costs a lot but still less than running several branch libraries.

**E. Each of Winchester's branch libraries attempts to cater to the tastes of the residents of the entire township.** — Too Weak (5% of students)
"Attempts to cater" — tries. Not succeeds. Tries. There is a meaningful difference between "each branch library brilliantly serves its local community" and "each branch library is really trying its best, bless its heart." An A for effort does not weaken the case for consolidation.

---

## S3 · Q7 — Must be True

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
News report: Some recently invented television screens are built out of small tiles seamlessly joined together, each tile a separate miniature screen. Television sets with these compound screens are just a few inches thick. For a noncompound screen in a set of this thickness, the larger the screen is, the dimmer it is. However, each tile in a compound screen is small enough to be quite bright. Moreover, an unlimited number of the tiles can be joined together, without making the resulting screen any less bright or the set any thicker.

### Prompt
Which one of the following can be properly inferred from the information in the news report?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:**
  - Some recently invented television screens are built out of small tiles seamlessly joined together.
  - Each tile is a separate miniature screen.
  - Television sets with these compound screens are just a few inches thick.
  - For a noncompound screen in a set of this thickness, the larger the screen is, the dimmer it is.
  - However, each tile in a compound screen is small enough to be quite bright.
  - Moreover, an unlimited number of the tiles can be joined together, without making the resulting screen any less bright or the set any thicker.
- **Evaluate:** The stimulus provides a comparison between noncompound screens and the new, recently invented compound screens. A noncompound screen that is just a few inches thick will get dimmer as it is made larger. However, since each tile in a new compound screen is bright, and an unlimited number of tiles can be joined together without reducing the brightness, it follows that the new compound screens can be made very large without becoming dimmer. Also, joining tiles together doesn't make the new compound screens any thicker, so we can make large, bright compound screens that are still only a few inches thick.

**Answer choices**
**A. The technology used to make compound television screens is not appropriate for television sets with relatively small screens.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
Nothing suggests compound screen technology is inappropriate for small screens. The ability to go large does not mean small is impossible. A few tiles would make a small, bright screen.

**B. There is a great consumer demand for television sets that are just a few inches thick and that have very large screens.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
The stimulus describes technological capability but says nothing about consumer demand. Whether consumers want these TVs is a market question the stimulus does not address.

**C. In a television set with a noncompound screen, the thicker the television set, the brighter the screen.** — Unsupported Relationship / Too Strong (6% of students)
The stimulus describes the size-brightness relationship at one thickness. It does not describe how changing thickness affects brightness. "The thicker the set, the brighter the screen" asserts an unbounded correlation the stimulus cannot support. Maybe thickness helps to a point, maybe not.

**D. Television sets that are just a few inches thick can now be made with screens that are both bright and very large.** — Correct (81% of students)
This follows directly from combining premises. Compound screens are a few inches thick. Each tile is bright. Unlimited tiles can be added without losing brightness or gaining thickness. Therefore: thin TVs can now have screens that are both bright and very large. Each element of this answer corresponds to a supported proposition, and the conjunction follows without additional assumptions.

**E. Television sets with compound screens do not have any disadvantages relative to sets with noncompound screens.** — Too Speculative (8% of students)
The stimulus describes advantages of compound screens but never claims they have no disadvantages. There could be many drawbacks not mentioned: higher cost, lower resolution, visible seams, shorter lifespan. Silence on disadvantages does not prove their absence.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Compound screens are made of small tiles joined seamlessly. Sets with compound screens are just a few inches thick. For noncompound screens at this thickness, larger means dimmer. Each compound tile is bright, and unlimited tiles can be joined without reducing brightness or increasing thickness.
- **Evaluate:** Compound screens overcome the size-brightness tradeoff. Since tiles stay bright regardless of quantity and do not add thickness, compound screens can be very large, bright, and thin simultaneously.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that follows logically from combining these facts.

**Answer choices**
**A. The technology used to make compound television screens is not appropriate for television sets with relatively small screens.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
The stimulus says compound screens can be very large, not that they must be. Nothing prevents using the technology for small screens.

**B. There is a great consumer demand for television sets that are just a few inches thick and that have very large screens.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
The stimulus describes technology, not consumer demand. Whether people want thin TVs with large screens is not addressed.

**C. In a television set with a noncompound screen, the thicker the television set, the brighter the screen.** — Unsupported Relationship / Too Strong (6% of students)
The stimulus describes size-brightness tradeoffs at one thickness, not how changing thickness affects brightness. This unbounded claim is unsupported.

**D. Television sets that are just a few inches thick can now be made with screens that are both bright and very large.** — Correct (81% of students)
Compound screens are thin, each tile is bright, and unlimited tiles can be joined without losing brightness or gaining thickness. Result: thin TVs can now be both bright and very large.

**E. Television sets with compound screens do not have any disadvantages relative to sets with noncompound screens.** — Too Speculative (8% of students)
The stimulus describes advantages but never claims zero disadvantages. Silence on drawbacks does not prove their absence.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** TV screens made of tiny bright tiles snapped together. Add more tiles, still bright. Add more tiles, still thin. Noncompound screens get dimmer as they get bigger, but compound screens laugh at that limitation.
- **Evaluate:** Compound screens can be enormous, bright, and only a few inches thick — the old rules do not apply.
- **Goal:** Find the answer stating the obvious: thin TVs can now be both large and bright.

**Answer choices**
**A. The technology used to make compound television screens is not appropriate for television sets with relatively small screens.** — Unsupported Distinction (2% of students)
Just because compound screens can go big does not mean they cannot go small. That is like saying airplanes are inappropriate for short trips because they can fly across oceans. The tiles are individually tiny — assembling a handful into a small screen seems perfectly reasonable.

**B. There is a great consumer demand for television sets that are just a few inches thick and that have very large screens.** — Out of Scope (3% of students)
The stimulus describes a cool technology. This answer says people want to buy it. Just because you can build a TV screen the size of a billboard does not mean consumers are lining up for one. The stimulus is a science article, not a market research report.

**C. In a television set with a noncompound screen, the thicker the television set, the brighter the screen.** — Unsupported Relationship / Too Strong (6% of students)
The stimulus says thin screens get dimmer as they get bigger. This answer concludes that making any screen thicker will make it brighter — forever. Maybe after six inches of thickness the brightness plateaus and you just have a very heavy, very thick TV. Wild extrapolation from one relationship to a completely different one.

**D. Television sets that are just a few inches thick can now be made with screens that are both bright and very large.** — Correct (81% of students)
Add tiles: bigger screen. Each tile stays bright: still bright. Adding tiles does not add thickness: still thin. Thin plus bright plus unlimited size equals a TV that is the size of a wall, blazingly bright, and thinner than a textbook. This answer just reads the instruction manual and states the obvious result.

**E. Television sets with compound screens do not have any disadvantages relative to sets with noncompound screens.** — Too Speculative (8% of students)
"No disadvantages at all." The stimulus talked about brightness, size, and thickness. It said nothing about price, picture quality, durability, or whether the tiles eventually start falling off. Just because the stimulus did not mention downsides does not mean there are none.

---

## S3 · Q8 — Role

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
Anthropologist: For early humans who moved from a hunter-gatherer lifestyle to agriculture, the transition must have been traumatic. There would have been increased incidence of disease and injury and a more homogeneous diet lacking vital nutrients more easily obtainable from the richly varied diet of hunter-gatherers. Thus, groups that made the transition were likely motivated by certain benefits that accompany the settled life of the agriculturist, such as opportunities for the accumulation of wealth by those with specialized social roles.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately describes the role played in the anthropologist's overall argument by the statement that for early humans who moved from a hunter-gatherer lifestyle to agriculture, the transition must have been traumatic?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Intermediate Conclusion:** For early humans who moved from a hunter-gatherer lifestyle to agriculture, the transition must have been traumatic.
- **Evidence:** There would have been increased incidence of disease and injury and a more homogeneous diet lacking vital nutrients more easily obtainable from the richly varied diet of hunter-gatherers.
- **Main Conclusion:** Groups that made the transition were likely motivated by certain benefits that accompany the settled life of the agriculturist, such as opportunities for the accumulation of wealth by those with specialized social roles.
- **Evaluate:** The statement that is quoted in the question stem is an intermediate conclusion. It is supported by the evidence in the second sentence, and supports the main conclusion in the final sentence.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a premise for which another premise is offered as support.** — Correct (80% of students)
The quoted statement supports the main conclusion and is itself supported by another premise (disease, injury, diet evidence). The LSAT's description — "a premise for which another premise is offered as support" — accurately captures both relationships. While we might call this an "intermediate conclusion," calling it a "premise" is not technically wrong since it does support the main conclusion.

**B. It is background information that plays no logical role in the argument.** — Background (3% of students)
The statement plays a direct logical role — it supports the main conclusion and is supported by evidence. Background information would be a neutral fact that sets the scene without participating in the argument's logic.

**C. It is a premise for which no support is offered.** — Premise (2% of students)
This claims no support is offered for the quoted statement. But the second sentence — increased disease, injury, and a less nutritious diet — directly supports the claim that the transition was traumatic. Support is clearly offered.

**D. It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole.** — Conclusion (14% of students)
The main conclusion is the final sentence, introduced by "thus." The quoted statement supports this conclusion but is not the conclusion itself.

**E. It is a claim that the rest of the argument seeks to rebut.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
The argument supports and builds upon the "traumatic" claim rather than seeking to rebut it. An opposing viewpoint would be contested by the argument, but this statement is firmly integrated into the argument's reasoning.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Groups that transitioned to agriculture were likely motivated by benefits of settled life, such as wealth accumulation.
- **Evidence:** The transition was traumatic — increased disease, injury, and a less nutritious diet. Despite these costs, groups still switched.
- **Evaluate:** The quoted statement is an intermediate conclusion supported by disease/diet evidence, and it supports the main conclusion about motivation by benefits.
- **Goal:** The quoted statement is a premise that is itself supported by another premise.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a premise for which another premise is offered as support.** — Correct (80% of students)
The quoted statement supports the main conclusion and is supported by the disease/diet evidence. This answer correctly identifies both relationships.

**B. It is background information that plays no logical role in the argument.** — Background (3% of students)
The quoted statement is not background — it actively supports the conclusion and is supported by evidence. It plays a direct logical role.

**C. It is a premise for which no support is offered.** — Premise (2% of students)
Support is offered — the evidence about disease, injury, and diet directly supports the "traumatic" claim.

**D. It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole.** — Conclusion (14% of students)
The main conclusion is the final sentence about groups being motivated by benefits. The "traumatic" claim supports the conclusion; it is not the conclusion.

**E. It is a claim that the rest of the argument seeks to rebut.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
The argument supports the "traumatic" claim and builds on it — it does not attempt to rebut it.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Early humans must have had a compelling reason to switch to farming — the anthropologist suggests the lure of settled-life perks like wealth.
- **Evidence:** Farming life was objectively worse: more disease, injuries, boring diet. The fact that groups switched despite suffering implies something good was pulling them.
- **Evaluate:** The "traumatic" claim is the logical middle child — supported by evidence below it, supporting the conclusion above it.
- **Goal:** Expect the answer to describe it as a claim supported by evidence that also supports the conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a premise for which another premise is offered as support.** — Correct (80% of students)
A premise that gets backed up by another premise — exactly right. "The transition was traumatic" supports the main conclusion and gets support from the disease-and-diet evidence. A supported supporter. The LSAT calling it a "premise" instead of "intermediate conclusion" is loose with terminology, but the description is accurate.

**B. It is background information that plays no logical role in the argument.** — Background (3% of students)
"Plays no logical role" — the statement that holds the entire argument together? The "traumatic transition" claim is the bridge between evidence and conclusion. Calling this background is like calling the foundation of a house "decorative."

**C. It is a premise for which no support is offered.** — Premise (2% of students)
"No support is offered" — did the second sentence take the day off? More disease, more injuries, worse diet — that is direct support for "traumatic." This answer ignores the evidence sitting right there.

**D. It is the conclusion of the argument as a whole.** — Conclusion (14% of students)
"Thus" in the last sentence is the LSAT's neon sign saying "main conclusion here." The traumatic claim supports the conclusion — it is the supporting act, not the headliner.

**E. It is a claim that the rest of the argument seeks to rebut.** — Opposing Idea (2% of students)
The argument does not fight the "traumatic" claim — it embraces it. It is an ally, not an adversary. The argument supports this claim and then uses it to reach the conclusion.

---

## S3 · Q9 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Most lawyers hold that violations of the rights of those who possess sites on the web are best prevented using copyright law. Yet many of the words used to describe such sites evoke ideas that are usually associated with real estate: for example, the word "site" itself and the term "visiting" as applied to sites. The common law of trespass usually applies to cases of encroachment on real estate. Thus, it is reasonable to extend that law to protect against encroachments on property in cyberspace.

### Prompt
The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Many of the words used to describe web sites evoke ideas that are usually associated with real estate. The common law of trespass usually applies to cases of encroachment on real estate.
- **Conclusion:** It is reasonable to extend that law [trespass] to protect against encroachments on property in cyberspace.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence notes a similarity between web sites and real estate. Many of the same words, like "site" and "visiting" are used when discussing both web sites and real estate. But then the argument gets cheeky. It uses this one similarity, which is pretty superficial, as a basis for claiming that web sites and real estate are similar in another way: the same types of laws should apply to both. When two types of activities happen to use similar terminology, does that necessarily mean that the same types of laws should apply to both? If I drive a car and also use Google Drive on my computer, do I have to obey local traffic laws while sitting at my desk using my laptop? If so, I guess I should put on a seatbelt and get rid of this glass of wine...

**Answer choices**
**A. fails to provide adequate evidence that property in cyberspace is widely considered to be real estate** — Out of Scope (12% of students)
The argument does not claim cyberspace property is "widely considered to be real estate." It draws an analogy based on shared terminology. This answer addresses a stronger claim than the argument makes.

**B. has a premise that presupposes what the argument attempts to show in the conclusion** — Circular Reasoning (2% of students)
No premise presupposes the conclusion. The premises about shared terminology and trespass law's real estate application are factual claims independent of whether trespass law should apply to cyberspace. The flaw is a weak analogy, not circular logic.

**C. itself provides significant evidence against the conclusion that it draws** — Self-Contradiction (2% of students)
The evidence does not contradict the conclusion — it is just weak. Shared terminology provides some minimal support for the legal analogy, not evidence against it. The problem is insufficiency, not self-contradiction.

**D. fails to provide evidence that the similarities that constitute the analogy are anything but merely verbal** — Correct (83% of students)
This precisely identifies the flaw. The argument's analogy between web sites and real estate rests entirely on shared vocabulary. No evidence is provided that web sites and real estate share substantive characteristics relevant to legal treatment — such as exclusive possession, physical boundaries, or interference with use. The answer correctly states that the argument fails to show the similarities go beyond verbal coincidence.

**E. defends a view solely on the grounds that the view is held by many experts** — Ad Hominem (1% of students)
The argument challenges the lawyers' view (copyright law) in favor of trespass law. It does not defer to expert opinion — it contradicts it. This answer reverses the argument's relationship to authority.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** It is reasonable to extend trespass law to protect against encroachments on property in cyberspace.
- **Evidence:** Web site terminology evokes real estate concepts. Trespass law applies to real estate encroachment.
- **Evaluate:** The analogy rests solely on shared terminology — a superficial verbal similarity rather than a substantive one.
- **Goal:** Identify the flaw: treating a merely verbal similarity as a basis for legal analogy.

**Answer choices**
**A. fails to provide adequate evidence that property in cyberspace is widely considered to be real estate** — Out of Scope (12% of students)
The argument draws an analogy, not a classification. It does not claim cyberspace is real estate.

**B. has a premise that presupposes what the argument attempts to show in the conclusion** — Circular Reasoning (2% of students)
No premise assumes the conclusion. The premises are factual observations that do not presuppose trespass law should apply online.

**C. itself provides significant evidence against the conclusion that it draws** — Self-Contradiction (2% of students)
The evidence weakly supports rather than contradicts the conclusion. Insufficiency is not the same as contradiction.

**D. fails to provide evidence that the similarities that constitute the analogy are anything but merely verbal** — Correct (83% of students)
The analogy relies entirely on shared words. This answer correctly identifies the flaw: no evidence the similarity goes beyond vocabulary to substantive properties justifying shared legal treatment.

**E. defends a view solely on the grounds that the view is held by many experts** — Ad Hominem (1% of students)
The argument contradicts the lawyers' view, advocating trespass law over their preferred copyright approach. It challenges rather than relies on expert opinion.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Trespass law should apply to the internet. Because they are called "sites" and people "visit" them, just like real estate.
- **Evidence:** Same words, therefore same laws. That is the entire argument.
- **Evaluate:** By this logic, since people "drive" Google Drive, a driver's license is needed to use it. Since people "stream" movies, fishing regulations apply. The analogy is built entirely on vocabulary overlap, not on any substantive similarity between web sites and physical property.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that calls out the verbal-only analogy.

**Answer choices**
**A. fails to provide adequate evidence that property in cyberspace is widely considered to be real estate** — Out of Scope (12% of students)
The argument never said web sites are real estate. It said they use similar language, therefore similar laws. This answer criticizes a claim the argument did not make.

**B. has a premise that presupposes what the argument attempts to show in the conclusion** — Circular Reasoning (2% of students)
Circular reasoning is "it is true because it is true" in disguise. But the premises genuinely attempt to support the conclusion — they just do a terrible job because the analogy is superficial. Bad support and circular support are different problems.

**C. itself provides significant evidence against the conclusion that it draws** — Self-Contradiction (2% of students)
The argument is not shooting itself in the foot. "Web sites use real-estate words" does not argue against applying real-estate law — it is trying to argue for it. The evidence is reaching in the right direction; it just cannot reach far enough.

**D. fails to provide evidence that the similarities that constitute the analogy are anything but merely verbal** — Correct (83% of students)
The argument's entire legal case rests on shared vocabulary. "Site." "Visiting." That is it. This answer calls it out: shared words are not shared substance. By this logic, "crashing" a computer should involve an insurance claim and "killing" a process should require a lawyer.

**E. defends a view solely on the grounds that the view is held by many experts** — Ad Hominem (1% of students)
The argument disagrees with the lawyers. It is the rebel here, arguing for trespass law against the professional consensus. This answer has the relationship backwards.

---

## S3 · Q10 — Strengthen (EXCEPT)

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Journalist: Contrary to popular opinion, it is more dangerous for an individual to drive during the day than during the night. A recent study found that in each of the last ten years, the number of traffic accidents that resulted in death was greater during the day than during the night.

### Prompt
Each of the following, if true, helps to strengthen the argument except:

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** A recent study found that in each of the last ten years, the number of traffic accidents that resulted in death was greater during the day than during the night.
- **Conclusion:** It is more dangerous for an individual to drive during the day than during the night.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence contains a comparison between the number of fatal traffic accidents that occur during the day and the number that occur at night. The evidence states that more fatal accidents occur during the day, but it's important to notice that this is only a correlation. Nothing in the evidence indicates what is causing more fatal accidents to occur during the day. The evidence claims that it is more dangerous for "an individual"—meaning anyone—to drive during the day. To reach that conclusion, we need make an assumption about whatever factors are causing more fatal accidents to occur during daytime. We need to assume that those factors will affect everyone who drives during the day. But we haven't been told what is actually causing more fatal accidents to occur during daytime, so we can't be sure that those factors will affect all individuals who drive during the day.
- **Goal:** An answer choice that strengthens the argument might describe a situation which makes driving more dangerous for everyone, but only occurs during the day, not at night. Since this is an EXCEPT question, we will expect four of the answers to strengthen the argument. The one correct answer will not strengthen the argument. It will either weaken the argument or—more likely—not have any clear impact on the argument.

**Answer choices**
**A. Only during the day are there more unsafe than safe vehicles on the road.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
More unsafe vehicles on the road only during the day creates a daytime-specific hazard, making daytime driving more dangerous for every individual. This strengthens the argument.

**B. There is decreased law enforcement presence during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
Fewer police during the day means more dangerous driving behavior during daylight hours. With less enforcement, risky driving goes unchecked, increasing per-driver danger. This strengthens the argument.

**C. Persons drive more cautiously during the night than during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
More cautious nighttime driving reduces per-driver risk at night relative to during the day. This differential in driving behavior strengthens the claim that daytime driving is more dangerous.

**D. The number of travelers per vehicle has increased over the past ten years.** — Correct (91% of students)
This does not strengthen the argument. More travelers per vehicle is a general trend with no stated difference between day and night. More passengers could increase fatalities per accident, but if this applies equally to both time periods, it does not make daytime driving comparatively more dangerous. Without a day-specific component, this answer has no differential impact on the argument's conclusion.

**E. Persons drive faster during the day than during the night.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
Faster daytime driving means more severe crashes during the day. Higher speeds increase stopping distances, impact forces, and reduce reaction time. This is a clear daytime-specific risk factor that strengthens the argument.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** It is more dangerous for an individual to drive during the day than at night.
- **Evidence:** In each of the last ten years, more fatal traffic accidents occurred during the day than at night.
- **Evaluate:** More total fatal accidents during the day could reflect more drivers, not more per-driver danger. Strengtheners should provide daytime-specific danger factors.
- **Goal:** Four answers strengthen by providing day-specific risk factors. The correct answer (the exception) will have no clear differential impact between day and night.

**Answer choices**
**A. Only during the day are there more unsafe than safe vehicles on the road.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
More unsafe vehicles during the day is a daytime-specific hazard. This strengthens the per-driver danger claim.

**B. There is decreased law enforcement presence during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
Less law enforcement during the day means more dangerous driving behavior during daylight. This strengthens the argument.

**C. Persons drive more cautiously during the night than during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
More cautious nighttime driving means less per-driver risk at night, making daytime comparatively more dangerous.

**D. The number of travelers per vehicle has increased over the past ten years.** — Correct (91% of students)
More travelers per vehicle is a general trend with no day-versus-night differential. Without a daytime-specific component, it does not strengthen the comparative claim.

**E. Persons drive faster during the day than during the night.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
Faster daytime driving means more dangerous crashes during the day. Clear daytime-specific risk factor.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Daytime driving is more dangerous. Hold the headlights.
- **Evidence:** More fatal accidents happen during the day. That is the entire basis.
- **Evaluate:** More daytime accidents could just mean more daytime drivers. The argument needs reasons why driving during the day is inherently more dangerous per person, not just busier.
- **Goal:** Four answers strengthen. One does not. Find the odd one out.

**Answer choices**
**A. Only during the day are there more unsafe than safe vehicles on the road.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
Daytime roads are crawling with unsafe vehicles, but nighttime roads are not. Clear daytime-specific hazard, clear strengthener.

**B. There is decreased law enforcement presence during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
No cops during the day means the road becomes a free-for-all. That makes daytime driving worse for everyone. Strengthener.

**C. Persons drive more cautiously during the night than during the day.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (4% of students)
People drive carefully at night and throw caution to the wind during the day. Daytime gets overconfident drivers, nighttime gets careful ones. Strengthener.

**D. The number of travelers per vehicle has increased over the past ten years.** — Correct (91% of students)
More people are carpooling. So what? This trend applies to both daytime and nighttime driving equally. The day-versus-night comparison does not budge. This is the odd one out — a general trend with no differential impact.

**E. Persons drive faster during the day than during the night.** — Strengthens (Opposite) (1% of students)
Faster during the day, deadlier crashes during the day. Speed kills, and drivers apparently step on it when they can see. Strengthener.

---

## S3 · Q11 — Role

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
Biologist: The evolutionary advantage of sexual reproduction is that it increases the range of genetic variation in a species, which is an advantage for the species as a whole. However, an increased range of genetic variation in a species is not advantageous for any individual member of the species. It follows that the sole reason that sexual reproduction has become the rule among both animals and plants is that natural selection has favored some entire species over others.

### Prompt
The claim that an increased range of genetic variation in a species is not advantageous for any individual member of the species plays which one of the following roles in the biologist's argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion (it follows that):** The sole reason that sexual reproduction has become the rule among both animals and plants is that natural selection has favored some entire species over others.
- **Evidence:** The evolutionary advantage of sexual reproduction is that it increases the range of genetic variation in a species, which is an advantage for the species as a whole. But an increased range of genetic variation in a species is not advantageous for any individual member of the species.
- **Evaluate:** The word "however" in the second sentence indicates a pivot from the idea in the first sentence to a different, contrasting idea. In the final sentence, the phrase "it follows that" indicates that this final sentence contains a conclusion, supported by evidence in the previous sentences.
- **Goal:** The statement quoted by the question stem is in the second sentence of the stimulus, which contains the argument's evidence or premise.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a claim offered in support of the argument's conclusion.** — Correct (65% of students)
The quoted statement is evidence — a claim that supports the conclusion. It establishes that genetic variation does not benefit individuals, which forces the explanation for sexual reproduction's prevalence to the species level. This directly supports the conclusion about species-level natural selection.

**B. It is presented to raise a question that sheds doubt on the argument's conclusion.** — Opposing Idea (5% of students)
The quoted statement does not raise doubt about the conclusion — it supports it. By showing that genetic variation does not benefit individuals, it strengthens the case for species-level selection. It is an ally of the conclusion, not a challenger.

**C. It is a claim that the argument is designed to call into question.** — Opposing Idea (9% of students)
The argument does not call the quoted statement into question — it embraces it as support for the conclusion. The argument builds upon this claim rather than attempting to undermine it.

**D. It describes an observed phenomenon for which the argument seeks an explanation.** — Conclusion (17% of students)
The conclusion explains why sexual reproduction has become the rule — the "observed phenomenon" if there is one. But the quoted statement is about whether genetic variation benefits individuals, which is evidence, not the phenomenon being explained. The phenomenon the argument seeks to explain is the prevalence of sexual reproduction, described in the conclusion itself.

**E. It is presented as the main explanation of the origin of an observed phenomenon.** — Conclusion (5% of students)
The main explanation of why sexual reproduction prevails is in the conclusion: species-level natural selection. The quoted statement is not the explanation — it is evidence that leads to the explanation. It establishes that individual-level selection cannot explain sexual reproduction, which then supports the species-level explanation in the conclusion.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** The sole reason sexual reproduction has become the rule is that natural selection has favored some entire species over others.
- **Evidence:** Sexual reproduction increases genetic variation, which benefits the species as a whole. But increased genetic variation is not advantageous for any individual member.
- **Evaluate:** The quoted statement is evidence supporting the conclusion. It establishes that the benefit of sexual reproduction operates at the species level, not the individual level, thereby supporting the conclusion about species-level selection.
- **Goal:** The quoted statement is a claim offered in support of the conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a claim offered in support of the argument's conclusion.** — Correct (65% of students)
The statement supports the conclusion by establishing that the benefit of genetic variation operates at the species level, not the individual level.

**B. It is presented to raise a question that sheds doubt on the argument's conclusion.** — Opposing Idea (5% of students)
The statement supports the conclusion rather than casting doubt on it.

**C. It is a claim that the argument is designed to call into question.** — Opposing Idea (9% of students)
The argument supports this claim, not challenges it. It is part of the argument's evidence.

**D. It describes an observed phenomenon for which the argument seeks an explanation.** — Conclusion (17% of students)
The phenomenon being explained is the prevalence of sexual reproduction, which is in the conclusion. The quoted statement is evidence, not the phenomenon.

**E. It is presented as the main explanation of the origin of an observed phenomenon.** — Conclusion (5% of students)
The explanation is in the conclusion (species-level selection). The quoted statement is evidence leading to that explanation, not the explanation itself.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Sexual reproduction won the evolutionary popularity contest because entire species that reproduced sexually beat out species that did not. Species-level natural selection did the work.
- **Evidence:** Genetic variation from sexual reproduction helps the species, not the individual. If it helped individuals, regular individual-level selection would explain it. Since it does not, the explanation must be at the species level.
- **Evaluate:** The quoted statement — that genetic variation does not help individuals — is the linchpin. It forces the explanation from the individual level up to the species level.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that says this statement supports the conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is a claim offered in support of the argument's conclusion.** — Correct (65% of students)
It is a claim. It supports the conclusion. That is what evidence does. The statement eliminates the individual-level explanation, forcing the conclusion toward species-level selection. Simple and correct.

**B. It is presented to raise a question that sheds doubt on the argument's conclusion.** — Opposing Idea (5% of students)
Sheds doubt? This statement is the conclusion's best friend. It clears the path for species-level selection by eliminating the individual-level alternative. No doubt-shedding here.

**C. It is a claim that the argument is designed to call into question.** — Opposing Idea (9% of students)
The argument is not trying to question this claim — it is using it as ammunition. Calling something into question means attacking it. The argument puts this claim on its team.

**D. It describes an observed phenomenon for which the argument seeks an explanation.** — Conclusion (17% of students)
The argument is trying to explain why sexual reproduction is everywhere. That is the phenomenon. The quoted claim about individuals not benefiting from genetic variation is a premise helping to explain it, not the thing being explained.

**E. It is presented as the main explanation of the origin of an observed phenomenon.** — Conclusion (5% of students)
The species-level selection theory in the conclusion is the explanation. The quoted statement is a supporting fact that helps build toward that explanation. It is the evidence, not the answer — the clue, not the solution.

---

## S3 · Q12 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Columnist: Vagrancy laws are supposed to reduce criminal activity, but they don't. Making vagrancy illegal means transforming many innocuous everyday occurrences into crimes. Thus, vagrancy laws increase crime while purporting to reduce it.

### Prompt
The reasoning in the columnist's argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that the argument

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Making vagrancy illegal means transforming many innocuous everyday occurrences into crimes.
- **Conclusion:** Vagrancy laws increase crime while purporting to reduce it.
- **Evaluate:** The argument presents evidence about "transforming many innocuous everyday occurrences into crimes" and then makes a leap to a new concept in the conclusion—increasing crime. The argument doesn't clearly explain what "increase crime" is intended to mean. It's reasonable to think this means that entirely new crimes take place, ones that did not occur before the vagrancy laws were passed. For example, in a certain town there might be 10 cases a month where someone actually trespasses on another person's property, but once vagrancy laws are passed that number jumps to 30 actual instances of trespassing. However, the evidence suggests that something different could be happening. It suggests that actions which were not illegal become illegal once vagrancy laws are passed. This could mean, for example, that napping under a tree in a public park is legal until vagrancy laws make it illegal. The actual number of people who nap under trees might stay exactly the same, or even decrease, but because it is now a crime, there is a sudden increase in the number of people who are arrested or fined for it. The argument doesn't clearly explain the link between innocuous occurrences becoming crimes and an increase in crime. It's not clear what "increase" actually means in this conclusion.
- **Goal:** A correct answer might point out how the phrase "increase crime" is used in an unclear way. It might point out that the argument doesn't establish a clear link between innocuous occurrences becoming crimes and an actual increase in crime.

**Answer choices**
**A. presumes, without providing any justification, that most innocuous everyday occurrences are harmful to society** — Opposite, if anything (2% of students)
The argument calls these everyday occurrences "innocuous," suggesting they are not harmful. It does not presume they are harmful to society — it says the opposite.

**B. mistakenly infers, from the claim that vagrancy laws are associated with an increase in many innocuous everyday occurrences, that vagrancy laws cause that increase** — Bad Evidence Match (21% of students)
The argument does not claim there is an increase in innocuous everyday occurrences. It says those occurrences are reclassified as crimes. The occurrences themselves may not increase at all — they simply receive a new legal classification. This answer mischaracterizes the evidence.

**C. fails to specify what is meant by "innocuous everyday occurrences"** — No Impact (6% of students)
The argument does not need to specify what the innocuous occurrences are. The flaw would exist regardless of whether examples were provided. Even if the argument named specific behaviors, it would still conflate reclassification with an actual increase in criminal activity.

**D. fails to consider the possibility that crime will increase even in the absence of vagrancy laws** — Out of Scope (8% of students)
Whether crime would increase without vagrancy laws is irrelevant to the argument's flaw. The argument focuses on what happens when vagrancy laws are enacted. External factors affecting crime rates independently of vagrancy laws do not address the internal logical problem of conflating reclassification with an actual increase in crime.

**E. does not adequately distinguish between an increase in criminal activity and the reclassification of certain occurrences as crimes** — Correct (63% of students)
This answer identifies the argument's core flaw. The argument uses "increase crime" in the conclusion without distinguishing between two very different meanings: (1) an actual increase in harmful criminal activity, and (2) the reclassification of previously legal behaviors as crimes. The evidence supports only meaning (2) — more things are now labeled as crimes — but the conclusion seems to claim meaning (1) — that there is more actual criminal activity. By failing to make this distinction, the argument equivocates on what "increase crime" means.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Vagrancy laws increase crime while purporting to reduce it.
- **Evidence:** Making vagrancy illegal transforms many innocuous everyday occurrences into crimes.
- **Evaluate:** The conclusion claims vagrancy laws "increase crime," but the evidence only shows that previously legal behaviors are reclassified as crimes. Reclassification is not the same as an actual increase in criminal activity — the same behaviors continue, just with a new legal label.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that identifies the ambiguity: the argument conflates reclassifying behavior as criminal with an actual increase in criminal activity.

**Answer choices**
**A. presumes, without providing any justification, that most innocuous everyday occurrences are harmful to society** — Opposite, if anything (2% of students)
The argument calls the occurrences "innocuous" — not harmful. This answer reverses the argument's characterization.

**B. mistakenly infers, from the claim that vagrancy laws are associated with an increase in many innocuous everyday occurrences, that vagrancy laws cause that increase** — Bad Evidence Match (21% of students)
The argument says occurrences are reclassified as crimes, not that occurrences increased. This mischaracterizes the evidence.

**C. fails to specify what is meant by "innocuous everyday occurrences"** — No Impact (6% of students)
Not specifying the occurrences is not the flaw. The argument would contain the same flaw even with specific examples.

**D. fails to consider the possibility that crime will increase even in the absence of vagrancy laws** — Out of Scope (8% of students)
Crime increasing without vagrancy laws is irrelevant. The argument's flaw is internal — conflating reclassification with actual crime increase.

**E. does not adequately distinguish between an increase in criminal activity and the reclassification of certain occurrences as crimes** — Correct (63% of students)
The argument conflates reclassifying behavior as criminal with an actual increase in criminal activity. This answer correctly identifies that equivocation.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Vagrancy laws increase crime. Take that, law and order.
- **Evidence:** Making vagrancy illegal turns previously legal everyday behaviors into crimes.
- **Evaluate:** Well, technically, yes — if you make sitting on a park bench illegal, you have more "crime" in the sense that more people are now breaking the law. But did crime actually increase? The same people are sitting on the same bench doing the same thing. The only thing that changed is the label. The argument treats relabeling as if it were an actual increase in bad behavior.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that calls out the sleight of hand: reclassification is not the same as more crime.

**Answer choices**
**A. presumes, without providing any justification, that most innocuous everyday occurrences are harmful to society** — Opposite, if anything (2% of students)
"Innocuous" literally means harmless. The argument says these everyday occurrences are not harmful. This answer says the argument assumes they are harmful. Those are opposite claims. Reading comprehension matters.

**B. mistakenly infers, from the claim that vagrancy laws are associated with an increase in many innocuous everyday occurrences, that vagrancy laws cause that increase** — Bad Evidence Match (21% of students)
The argument never said there are more people sitting in parks. It said people sitting in parks are now criminals. Reclassification, not multiplication. This answer misreads what the argument actually claims.

**C. fails to specify what is meant by "innocuous everyday occurrences"** — No Impact (6% of students)
"You did not specify which everyday things are now crimes." That is not the problem. The problem is equating "relabeling behavior as criminal" with "increasing criminal activity." Naming specific behaviors would not fix that logical error.

**D. fails to consider the possibility that crime will increase even in the absence of vagrancy laws** — Out of Scope (8% of students)
"What if crime increases anyway?" is a different conversation entirely. The argument's problem is not about external crime trends — it is about confusing "new label for old behavior" with "new bad behavior." Wrong diagnosis.

**E. does not adequately distinguish between an increase in criminal activity and the reclassification of certain occurrences as crimes** — Correct (63% of students)
The argument pulls a definitional bait-and-switch. "Crime increased" could mean "more bad stuff is happening" or "the same stuff is happening but now it is called a crime." The evidence supports the second meaning. The conclusion implies the first. The argument never acknowledges that these are completely different claims. Relabeling park-bench-sitting as a crime is not the same as an actual crime wave.

---

## S3 · Q13 — Necessary Assumption

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Political theorist: The purpose of government is to prevent individuals from injuring others in the pursuit of their own welfare. Hence, if most individuals take a reasoned approach to getting what they want, the power of government should be less.

### Prompt
Which one of the following is an assumption required by the political theorist's argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The purpose of government is to prevent individuals from injuring others in the pursuit of their own welfare.
- **Conclusion:** If most individuals take a reasoned approach to getting what they want, the power of government should be less.
- **Evaluate:** As is sometimes the case in a Necessary Assumption question, this argument contains more than one gap in logic. There are several new concepts in the conclusion that are different from concepts discussed in the evidence. The evidence mentions "injuring others" in the pursuit of our own welfare. The conclusion shifts to "a reasoned approach" to getting what we want. Many people would agree that taking a reasoned approach to something involves not injuring others, but that might not always be true. If someone tries to physically attack me, it might be reasonable for me to injure that person to prevent them from hurting me. This argument assumes that for most people, taking a reasoned approach to getting what we want involves not injuring others in pursuit of our own welfare. The evidence also mentions "the purpose of government," while the conclusion discusses "the power of government." These are different things. The argument assumes that the power of government should be reduced if individuals do not need the government to fulfill its purpose. We can get a sense for the structure of the argument, including the assumptions it makes, by putting it all together like this: Most individuals who take a reasoned approach to getting what they want will not injure others in the pursuit of their own welfare, so they will not need the government to fulfill its purpose. And if most individuals do not need the government to fulfill its purpose, the power of government should be less.

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments alone are not able to convince individuals to take a reasoned approach to getting what they want.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
Whether governments can convince individuals to be reasonable is not part of the argument. The evidence is about government preventing injury; the conclusion is about what happens if people are reasonable. How people become reasonable is outside the scope.

**B. Government can serve its purpose without diminishing people's ability to promote their own welfare.** — Out of Scope (6% of students)
Whether government can serve its purpose without diminishing people's ability to promote their welfare is not required by the argument. The argument is about what happens when people are reasonable — not about how government exercises its power. Even if government sometimes diminishes people's welfare while preventing injury, the argument's logic would still hold.

**C. People who take a reasoned approach to getting what they want are less likely to injure other people than are those who do not.** — Correct (88% of students)
This bridges the central gap. The evidence says government exists to prevent people from injuring others. The conclusion says reasonable people need less government. The missing link: reasonable people are less likely to injure others. If this is not true — if reasonable people injure others just as often — then being reasonable would not reduce the need for government's injury-prevention purpose. Negating this assumption destroys the argument.

**D. People who injure other people in the pursuit of their own welfare will eventually be treated in the same fashion by others.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Whether people who injure others will be treated the same way is about reciprocity or karma, not about the argument's logic. The argument concerns the relationship between reasonable behavior and government power, not what happens to people who injure others.

**E. The more interest people have in promoting their own welfare, the less likely they are to be injured by others.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
The argument discusses people injuring others. Whether interest in one's own welfare affects the likelihood of being injured oneself is a different question. The evidence is about preventing people from committing injury, not about factors that make people more or less likely to be victims.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** If most individuals take a reasoned approach to getting what they want, the power of government should be less.
- **Evidence:** The purpose of government is to prevent individuals from injuring others in pursuit of their own welfare.
- **Evaluate:** The argument leaps from "injuring others" in the evidence to "a reasoned approach" in the conclusion. It assumes that taking a reasoned approach reduces the likelihood of injuring others.
- **Goal:** Find a necessary assumption linking "reasoned approach" to "not injuring others."

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments alone are not able to convince individuals to take a reasoned approach to getting what they want.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
How people become reasonable is not the argument's concern. The argument is about what follows if they are reasonable.

**B. Government can serve its purpose without diminishing people's ability to promote their own welfare.** — Out of Scope (6% of students)
How government exercises its power is outside the argument's concern. The argument is about the relationship between reasonable behavior and the need for government power.

**C. People who take a reasoned approach to getting what they want are less likely to injure other people than are those who do not.** — Correct (88% of students)
If reasonable people are not less likely to injure others, there is no reason to think being reasonable reduces the need for government. This assumption is necessary for the argument.

**D. People who injure other people in the pursuit of their own welfare will eventually be treated in the same fashion by others.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
Reciprocal treatment of injurious people is outside the argument's scope. The argument is about reasonable behavior reducing the need for government.

**E. The more interest people have in promoting their own welfare, the less likely they are to be injured by others.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
The argument is about committing injury, not about being injured. Factors affecting victimization are outside the argument's scope.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** If people are reasonable, government can shrink. Less need for the government referee.
- **Evidence:** Government exists to stop people from hurting each other while pursuing their own interests.
- **Evaluate:** The magic leap: "reasonable" somehow equals "not hurting people." But history is full of very reasonable people who harmed others quite deliberately in pursuit of their goals. The argument needs to assume that being reasonable means being less injurious.
- **Goal:** Find the assumption that connects reasonableness to not hurting others.

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments alone are not able to convince individuals to take a reasoned approach to getting what they want.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
Who cares who convinced people to be reasonable? The argument says "if they are reasonable, government can be smaller." How they got reasonable is someone else's problem.

**B. Government can serve its purpose without diminishing people's ability to promote their own welfare.** — Out of Scope (6% of students)
This is about whether government is a gentle giant or a bull in a china shop while doing its job. The argument does not care — it is only asking whether reasonable people need less government in the first place.

**C. People who take a reasoned approach to getting what they want are less likely to injure other people than are those who do not.** — Correct (88% of students)
The missing connection: reasonable people hurt others less. Without this, the argument collapses. Government exists to prevent injury. Being reasonable should mean less injury. If reasonable people are just as injurious, being reasonable changes nothing about the need for government. Negate it and the argument falls apart — the textbook test for a necessary assumption.

**D. People who injure other people in the pursuit of their own welfare will eventually be treated in the same fashion by others.** — Out of Scope (0% of students)
"What goes around comes around" is a nice philosophy, but the argument is not about karma. It is about whether reasonable people hurt each other less and therefore need less government. Reciprocal treatment is a completely different topic.

**E. The more interest people have in promoting their own welfare, the less likely they are to be injured by others.** — Out of Scope (2% of students)
The argument is about people hurting others, not about people being hurt. Whether self-interested people are harder to victimize is interesting but completely beside the point.

---

## S3 · Q14 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Food critic: One of the chief competitors of Chris's restaurant claims that Chris's okra supplier cannot reliably supply fresh okra. If this claim were true, Chris's customers could not count on getting good seafood gumbo, Chris's specialty, because the best seafood gumbo requires fresh okra.

### Prompt
The food critic's argument is most subject to criticism because it

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The best seafood gumbo requires fresh okra.
- **Conclusion:** If this claim were true [Chris's okra supplier cannot reliably supply fresh okra], then Chris's customers could not count on getting good seafood gumbo.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence is a conditional statement: `best gumbo → fresh okra`. Using the contrapositive, we can conclude that if we don't have fresh okra, we cannot have the best seafood gumbo: `~ fresh okra → ~ best gumbo`. The argument tries to make a very similar conclusion, but there is an important term shift. The conclusion of this argument is about getting good gumbo, not the best gumbo: `~ fresh okra → ~ good gumbo`. It might be possible to make good seafood gumbo without fresh okra, just not the best seafood gumbo.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies upon testimony that is likely to be biased** — Ad Hominem (14% of students)
The conclusion is conditional: if the claim is true, then the consequence follows. The argument does not assume the competitor's claim is true. It evaluates what would happen if the claim were true, which avoids the bias concern.

**B. illicitly presumes that a dish must lack a certain quality if one of its ingredients lacks that quality** — Part vs. Whole (7% of students)
This would apply if the argument claimed the gumbo is not fresh because the okra is not fresh. But the argument is about quality levels — best versus good — not about transferring a quality from ingredient to dish.

**C. confuses a necessary condition for the best seafood gumbo with a sufficient condition for good seafood gumbo** — Necessary vs. Sufficient (15% of students)
This claims fresh okra is treated as sufficient for good gumbo. But the argument does not conclude that having fresh okra guarantees good gumbo. It concludes that lacking fresh okra prevents good gumbo. The actual flaw is conflating what is necessary for the best gumbo with what is necessary for good gumbo.

**D. takes for granted that a necessary ingredient of the best seafood gumbo is also a necessary ingredient of good seafood gumbo** — Correct (64% of students)
The evidence establishes fresh okra as a necessary ingredient for the best seafood gumbo. The conclusion assumes fresh okra is also necessary for good gumbo. But the best and good are different quality thresholds. Good gumbo might be achievable without fresh okra — you just cannot make the best gumbo.
