This answer precisely identifies the unjustified assumption that what is necessary for the highest quality is also necessary for adequate quality.

**E. fails to consider the possibility that on some days Chris's might be serving seafood gumbo made with fresh okra** — No Impact (1% of students)
Even if Chris occasionally gets fresh okra, the conclusion is about customers being able to count on good gumbo reliably. Occasional availability does not address the reliability concern or the best-versus-good distinction that is the actual flaw.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** If Chris's okra supplier cannot reliably supply fresh okra, Chris's customers cannot count on getting good seafood gumbo.
- **Evidence:** The best seafood gumbo requires fresh okra.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence says fresh okra is needed for the best gumbo. The conclusion shifts to good gumbo. Good gumbo might not require fresh okra — only the best does.
- **Goal:** Identify the flaw: confusing what is needed for the best gumbo with what is needed for good gumbo.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies upon testimony that is likely to be biased** — Ad Hominem (14% of students)
The argument uses a conditional — "if this claim were true" — rather than assuming biased testimony is accurate.

**B. illicitly presumes that a dish must lack a certain quality if one of its ingredients lacks that quality** — Part vs. Whole (7% of students)
The flaw is about best versus good quality levels, not about transferring an ingredient's quality to the whole dish.

**C. confuses a necessary condition for the best seafood gumbo with a sufficient condition for good seafood gumbo** — Necessary vs. Sufficient (15% of students)
The argument does not treat fresh okra as sufficient for good gumbo. It treats it as necessary — but for good gumbo rather than for the best gumbo.

**D. takes for granted that a necessary ingredient of the best seafood gumbo is also a necessary ingredient of good seafood gumbo** — Correct (64% of students)
Fresh okra is necessary for the best gumbo. The argument assumes it is also necessary for good gumbo. These are different standards, and the assumption is unjustified.

**E. fails to consider the possibility that on some days Chris's might be serving seafood gumbo made with fresh okra** — No Impact (1% of students)
Occasional fresh okra does not address the reliability concern. The actual flaw is the best-to-good term shift.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** No fresh okra, no good gumbo. Or so the food critic claims.
- **Evidence:** The best gumbo needs fresh okra. But who said anything about the best? The conclusion is about good gumbo. Plenty of things can be good without being the best.
- **Evaluate:** "Best" and "good" are not the same standard. A restaurant can make perfectly good gumbo without fresh okra — just not the absolute best gumbo. The argument smuggles in a higher standard than what the conclusion actually discusses.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that catches the best-to-good switch.

**Answer choices**
**A. relies upon testimony that is likely to be biased** — Ad Hominem (14% of students)
The argument says "if the competitor's claim is true." It is not blindly believing the competitor — it is running a hypothetical. Bias does not matter in a hypothetical.

**B. illicitly presumes that a dish must lack a certain quality if one of its ingredients lacks that quality** — Part vs. Whole (7% of students)
If the argument said "the okra is not fresh, therefore the gumbo is not fresh," that would be a part-to-whole fallacy. But the argument is about quality levels — best versus good. Different kind of error.

**C. confuses a necessary condition for the best seafood gumbo with a sufficient condition for good seafood gumbo** — Necessary vs. Sufficient (15% of students)
A sufficiency error would be: "fresh okra guarantees good gumbo." But the argument says the opposite: no fresh okra means no good gumbo. The real flaw is switching "best" to "good," not confusing necessary with sufficient.

**D. takes for granted that a necessary ingredient of the best seafood gumbo is also a necessary ingredient of good seafood gumbo** — Correct (64% of students)
The best gumbo needs fresh okra. Therefore good gumbo needs fresh okra? That is like saying only the best restaurant in town has salt, so no restaurant without salt can be good. "Best" and "good" are different bars, and clearing the lower one does not require everything the higher one demands.

**E. fails to consider the possibility that on some days Chris's might be serving seafood gumbo made with fresh okra** — No Impact (1% of students)
Sometimes having fresh okra is not the same as reliably having it. And this answer does not even touch the real flaw — the switch from "best" to "good." It is answering the wrong question about the wrong problem.

---

## S3 · Q15 — Weaken (Alternative Explanation)

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Ants sometimes live in hollow places in the roots of a certain orchid species. Those orchids of that species that house ants are far healthier than those that do not. Since the ants store organic matter in the orchids' roots, an explanation for the superior health of the orchids that house ants is that the organic matter stored by the ants provides those orchids with additional nutrients.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, is an alternative explanation for the better health of orchids that house the ants?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Curious Fact:** Why are the ant-hosting orchids of a given species healthier than the orchids that don't host ants?
- **Provided Explanation:** The ants store organic matter in the orchids' roots, which provides the orchids with additional nutrients, which makes them healthier?
- **Goal:** Our task is to get an alternate explanation for why there's this correlation between hosting ants and being a healthier orchid. Whenever we're seeking an alternate explanation for a correlation, we consider two things: Reverse Causality — Maybe the orchid's superior health comes first, and that causes ants to shelter there. Third Factor — Maybe some factor that is associated with the presence of ants (for example, a water supply) is also causing those orchids to grow better. It's also possible that we just get an alternate explanation where the ants are the cause of the orchid's health, but not because they're putting extra nutrients in the orchid's roots.

**Answer choices**
**A. Microscopic organisms that are present in the orchids' roots break down the organic matter stored there by the ants.** — No Impact (3% of students)
Microscopic organisms breaking down the organic matter addresses the mechanism of the provided explanation, not an alternative one. We need a different causal story, not more detail about the existing one.

**B. The nutrients present in the organic matter stored by the ants are the same as those present in the soil in which the orchids are found.** — No Impact (8% of students)
If the ant-deposited nutrients are the same as those in the surrounding soil, this weakens the provided explanation but does not offer an alternative. We need a different reason for the health correlation, not a reason to doubt the current one.

**C. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids prey on an insect species whose members eat the orchids' roots.** — Correct (87% of students)
This provides a completely different causal explanation. The ants prey on insects that eat orchid roots. If the ants protect the orchids from root-eating insects, orchids with ants would be healthier — not because of extra nutrients, but because their roots are not being eaten. This is a different mechanism connecting ants to orchid health: pest control rather than nutrient supplementation.

**D. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids do not play a role in pollinating the flowers of the plant.** — No Impact (0% of students)
The ants not playing a role in pollination has no causal power. We need an explanation where ants do something beneficial. Saying the ants do not do something provides no alternative explanation for the health correlation.

**E. Most plant species whose roots, stems, or leaves harbor insects are more prone to disease and rot than are other plants.** — Opposite, if anything (1% of students)
If most insect-harboring plants are more prone to disease, this suggests ants should make orchids less healthy, not more. This deepens the puzzle rather than explaining it, and potentially weakens any explanation involving ants benefiting orchids.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** The organic matter stored by ants provides orchids with additional nutrients, explaining their superior health.
- **Evidence:** Orchids housing ants are far healthier. Ants store organic matter in orchid roots.
- **Evaluate:** The provided explanation is that ants' organic matter gives extra nutrients. We need an alternative explanation for the correlation between ants and healthy orchids — perhaps reverse causation, a third factor, or a different causal mechanism.
- **Goal:** Find an alternative explanation for why orchids with ants are healthier.

**Answer choices**
**A. Microscopic organisms that are present in the orchids' roots break down the organic matter stored there by the ants.** — No Impact (3% of students)
This adds detail to the provided explanation rather than offering a different one.

**B. The nutrients present in the organic matter stored by the ants are the same as those present in the soil in which the orchids are found.** — No Impact (8% of students)
This weakens the provided explanation but does not supply an alternative. We need a different causal story.

**C. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids prey on an insect species whose members eat the orchids' roots.** — Correct (87% of students)
Ants eat insects that eat orchid roots. Orchids with ants have intact roots and are healthier — not because of nutrients, but because of pest protection. Different mechanism, different explanation.

**D. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids do not play a role in pollinating the flowers of the plant.** — No Impact (0% of students)
Ants not pollinating has no explanatory power. We need a story where ants do something helpful, not where they do not.

**E. Most plant species whose roots, stems, or leaves harbor insects are more prone to disease and rot than are other plants.** — Opposite, if anything (1% of students)
If insect-harboring plants are usually sicker, ants should hurt orchids, not help them. This deepens the puzzle rather than explaining the health correlation.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Ants make orchids healthier by depositing nutritious snack packs in their roots. Or so the story goes.
- **Evidence:** Orchids with ants are healthier. Ants store organic matter in orchid roots.
- **Evaluate:** Maybe the ants are helping for a completely different reason. Maybe healthier orchids attract ants. Maybe something else associated with ants is the real hero. The provided explanation is just one possibility.
- **Goal:** Find a different reason ants and healthy orchids go together.

**Answer choices**
**A. Microscopic organisms that are present in the orchids' roots break down the organic matter stored there by the ants.** — No Impact (3% of students)
This is like asking for a different explanation for why a plant grew and getting "the water was absorbed through osmosis." That is a detail about the current explanation, not a different one.

**B. The nutrients present in the organic matter stored by the ants are the same as those present in the soil in which the orchids are found.** — No Impact (8% of students)
The ants are just depositing the same stuff that is already in the soil. So the current explanation might be wrong — but this does not provide the right explanation. Debunking without replacing is not what the question asks for.

**C. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids prey on an insect species whose members eat the orchids' roots.** — Correct (87% of students)
The ants are not feeding the orchids — they are bodyguarding them. There are bugs that eat orchid roots, and the ants eat those bugs. No ants means the root-munchers have a free buffet. This is a completely different story from "ants bring nutritious snacks" — it is "ants eliminate the thugs."

**D. The ants that live in hollow places in the roots of orchids do not play a role in pollinating the flowers of the plant.** — No Impact (0% of students)
The ants do not pollinate the orchids. Cool. That covers what they do not do. The question requires knowing what they do that makes orchids healthier. A list of non-activities is not an explanation.

**E. Most plant species whose roots, stems, or leaves harbor insects are more prone to disease and rot than are other plants.** — Opposite, if anything (1% of students)
Most plants with bugs living in them get sicker. But orchids with ants get healthier. So instead of explaining the mystery, this answer just makes it weirder. "Usually this goes badly, but here it does not" is a deeper puzzle, not an explanation.

---

## S3 · Q16 — Principle-Conform

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Future overall demand for professors can be predicted with reasonable accuracy from current birth rates. But the accuracy of predictions of future demand for music professors is lower, and that for jazz studies professors lower still.

### Prompt
The situation described above best illustrates which one of the following principles?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Situation:** Future overall demand for professors can be predicted with reasonable accuracy from current birth rates.
- **However...:** The accuracy of predictions of future demand for music professors is lower, and that for jazz studies professors lower still.
- **Evaluate:** In this situation we're comparing a prediction about a general group, professors, to a prediction about a subset of that group, music professors. We then compare the prediction about music professors to a prediction about a subset of music professors, namely jazz studies professors. The demand for the overall group can be predicted reasonably well by birth rates. But as we look at each smaller, more specific subset, there's a decrease in the ability to accurately predict demand.
- **Goal:** We're expecting an answer to state that we have a greater ability to predict something about a general group, with less and less ability to make accurate predictions as we look at specific categories within groups.

**Answer choices**
**A. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more precise predictions based on that evidence are.** — Bad Trigger Match (5% of students)
The same evidence — birth rates — is used for all three predictions. The evidence does not change in detail or completeness. The accuracy changes because the target becomes more specific, not because the evidence becomes more detailed.

**B. The more general the level of a prediction, the more accurate it is.** — Correct (74% of students)
The situation shows exactly this: the most general prediction (all professors) is most accurate, and accuracy declines as the prediction targets more specific subgroups (music professors, then jazz studies professors). More general level of prediction, more accuracy.

**C. Predicting future trends becomes more difficult as the events those predictions concern become more temporally remote.** — Bad Outcome Match (10% of students)
The situation does not involve temporal distance. All predictions are about the future from the same present point. Accuracy varies because of specificity, not because some events are further in the future.

**D. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more confidence we can have in predictions based on that evidence.** — Bad Trigger Match (3% of students)
Same as answer A — the evidence does not vary in detail or completeness. Birth rates are the same evidence used for all three prediction levels. Confidence should vary with prediction specificity, not evidence quality.

**E. Predictions based only on general trends are unlikely to be accurate.** — Contradicted (8% of students)
The situation shows the opposite: predictions based on general trends (birth rates) are reasonably accurate for overall professor demand. This answer claims such predictions are "unlikely to be accurate," which directly contradicts the first sentence of the stimulus.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Situation:** Future demand for professors can be predicted from birth rates. But predictions for music professors are less accurate, and for jazz studies professors less accurate still.
- **Evaluate:** As the prediction gets more specific (all professors -> music -> jazz), accuracy decreases. More general predictions are more accurate.
- **Goal:** Find the principle: more general predictions are more accurate than more specific ones.

**Answer choices**
**A. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more precise predictions based on that evidence are.** — Bad Trigger Match (5% of students)
The evidence (birth rates) is the same across all predictions. Accuracy varies because of prediction specificity, not evidence quality.

**B. The more general the level of a prediction, the more accurate it is.** — Correct (74% of students)
General predictions are most accurate; specific predictions are least accurate. This matches the scenario perfectly.

**C. Predicting future trends becomes more difficult as the events those predictions concern become more temporally remote.** — Bad Outcome Match (10% of students)
Temporal distance is not the variable here — prediction specificity is. All predictions are about the same future timeframe.

**D. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more confidence we can have in predictions based on that evidence.** — Bad Trigger Match (3% of students)
The evidence (birth rates) is constant. Confidence varies because of prediction specificity, not evidence quality.

**E. Predictions based only on general trends are unlikely to be accurate.** — Contradicted (8% of students)
The stimulus says general predictions from birth rates are reasonably accurate. This answer says they are unlikely to be accurate — a direct contradiction.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Situation:** Birth rates can predict how many professors will be needed. Music professors? Less accurately. Jazz studies professors? Even less. The more you zoom in, the worse the prediction gets.
- **Evaluate:** General predictions work well because they average out the randomness. Specific predictions are at the mercy of niche trends that birth rates cannot capture.
- **Goal:** Find the principle that says: bigger picture, better prediction.

**Answer choices**
**A. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more precise predictions based on that evidence are.** — Bad Trigger Match (5% of students)
The evidence does not get more detailed — it is birth rates all the way down. The predictions get worse not because the evidence changes but because the target gets more specific.

**B. The more general the level of a prediction, the more accurate it is.** — Correct (74% of students)
Predict professors overall? Pretty accurate. Predict music professors? Less accurate. Predict jazz studies professors? Even less. The more you zoom in, the worse it gets. This answer just states that principle: bigger picture, better prediction.

**C. Predicting future trends becomes more difficult as the events those predictions concern become more temporally remote.** — Bad Outcome Match (10% of students)
Nobody is predicting further into the future for jazz professors than for all professors. The time frame is the same. The difference is specificity, not distance in time.

**D. The more detailed and complete the evidence, the more confidence we can have in predictions based on that evidence.** — Bad Trigger Match (3% of students)
Same problem as answer A: the evidence does not get more detailed or complete. It is birth rates for everything. The issue is how specific the target is, not how good the data is.

**E. Predictions based only on general trends are unlikely to be accurate.** — Contradicted (8% of students)
The stimulus literally says general predictions work pretty well. This answer says they do not. One of them is wrong, and the stimulus wins.

---

## S3 · Q17 — Flaw

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Anarchist: People can either fight for anarchy or they can tolerate totalitarian government control. But history shows that government control of all facets of human life destroys the spirit, making life miserable. So, people should fight for anarchy, which allows each individual the freedom to pursue a personal course of happiness.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument's reasoning?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** People can either fight for anarchy or they can tolerate totalitarian government control. History shows that government control of all facets of human life destroys the spirit, making life miserable. Anarchy allows each individual the freedom to pursue a personal course of happiness.
- **Conclusion:** People should fight for anarchy.
- **Evaluate:** "Should" tends to be a strong word on the LSAT. When the conclusion of an argument makes a recommendation, stating what someone "should" do, the evidence is supposed to clearly justify why that is the best course of action. As a general rule, we accept the evidence that we're given in LR questions. However, difficult LR questions will occasionally test our critical thinking skills in a more holistic way. To answer these questions correctly, we need to be flexible and take a big-picture view, rather than mechanically following general rules. Even though we can consider the first sentence of this argument as evidence, it stands out as rather extreme statement. Anarchy and totalitarian government control are extremes at the opposite ends of the spectrum. Most systems of government that exist fall somewhere in between. Is it reasonable to think that anarchy and totalitarian government control are the only two options available? No, it's not. There is a clear false choice flaw baked into this premise, which makes the argument questionable from the start.

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments provide many social goods, and the argument does not attempt to explain why anarchy is a desirable alternative to government control of all facets of human life.** — Contradicted (6% of students)
The argument does explain why anarchy is desirable — it allows freedom to pursue happiness. And it explains why totalitarianism is undesirable — it destroys the spirit. This answer mischaracterizes the argument.

**B. By asserting that people both should and should not tolerate totalitarian government, the argument contradicts itself.** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The argument does not say people should tolerate totalitarianism — it argues they should fight for anarchy instead. The first sentence presents toleration as one option; the conclusion recommends against it. There is no contradiction within the argument.

**C. The argument infers from the assertion that people should fight for anarchy that totalitarianism is objectionable.** — Reversal (5% of students)
This answer reverses the argument's actual logic. The argument infers from "totalitarianism is objectionable" that "people should fight for anarchy" — not the other way around. This answer has the direction of inference backwards.

**D. The argument offers no indication that there is evidence for its claim that totalitarianism destroys the spirit and makes life miserable.** — Contradicted (7% of students)
The argument says "history shows" that totalitarianism makes life miserable. "History" is offered as evidence. This answer claims no evidence is indicated, which contradicts the text.

**E. The argument sets out anarchy and totalitarianism as the only two political alternatives that are available.** — Correct (80% of students)
This identifies the false dichotomy. The argument presents only two options — anarchy and totalitarianism — as if nothing exists between them. In reality, most political systems occupy the vast middle ground: democracies, republics, constitutional monarchies, and numerous other forms of limited government. By excluding these alternatives, the argument artificially narrows the choice to make anarchy seem like the only reasonable option.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** People should fight for anarchy.
- **Evidence:** People can either fight for anarchy or tolerate totalitarian government control. Totalitarianism destroys the spirit. Anarchy allows freedom to pursue happiness.
- **Evaluate:** The argument presents a false dichotomy: anarchy and totalitarianism are not the only options. Most political systems fall between these extremes.
- **Goal:** Identify the false choice flaw.

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments provide many social goods, and the argument does not attempt to explain why anarchy is a desirable alternative to government control of all facets of human life.** — Contradicted (6% of students)
The argument does explain why anarchy is desirable (freedom) and totalitarianism is not (spirit destruction). This answer is factually wrong about the argument.

**B. By asserting that people both should and should not tolerate totalitarian government, the argument contradicts itself.** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The argument recommends against tolerating totalitarianism. It never says people should tolerate it.

**C. The argument infers from the assertion that people should fight for anarchy that totalitarianism is objectionable.** — Reversal (5% of students)
The argument goes from "totalitarianism is bad" to "fight for anarchy," not vice versa. This reverses the actual inference direction.

**D. The argument offers no indication that there is evidence for its claim that totalitarianism destroys the spirit and makes life miserable.** — Contradicted (7% of students)
The argument cites "history" as evidence. This answer says no evidence is offered — contradicting the text.

**E. The argument sets out anarchy and totalitarianism as the only two political alternatives that are available.** — Correct (80% of students)
The argument presents only two extremes. Most political systems fall between anarchy and totalitarianism. This false dichotomy is the argument's central flaw.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Fight for anarchy. That is the recommendation.
- **Evidence:** The only two options: anarchy or totalitarian oppression. Totalitarianism is terrible. Anarchy is freedom. Therefore: anarchy.
- **Evaluate:** This is about as false a dichotomy as they come. "Your only choices are no government or the worst possible government" conveniently ignores every form of governance between those two extremes. Most of human civilization operates in the vast middle ground this argument pretends does not exist.
- **Goal:** Find the false choice flaw.

**Answer choices**
**A. Governments provide many social goods, and the argument does not attempt to explain why anarchy is a desirable alternative to government control of all facets of human life.** — Contradicted (6% of students)
Did this answer read a different stimulus? The argument literally says totalitarianism makes life miserable and anarchy provides freedom. Those are explanations. Not great explanations, given the false dichotomy, but explanations nonetheless.

**B. By asserting that people both should and should not tolerate totalitarian government, the argument contradicts itself.** — Contradicted (2% of students)
The argument says "you can either fight for anarchy or accept totalitarianism — and you should fight for anarchy." That is consistent, not contradictory. It is picking one side of its own either/or, not arguing both sides simultaneously.

**C. The argument infers from the assertion that people should fight for anarchy that totalitarianism is objectionable.** — Reversal (5% of students)
The argument says totalitarianism is bad, therefore fight for anarchy. This answer says the argument goes the other direction: fight for anarchy, therefore totalitarianism is bad. Cause and effect are backwards.

**D. The argument offers no indication that there is evidence for its claim that totalitarianism destroys the spirit and makes life miserable.** — Contradicted (7% of students)
"History shows" is literally right there in the argument. That is an indication of evidence. Not a very specific one, but an indication nonetheless. This answer says there is no indication — wrong.

**E. The argument sets out anarchy and totalitarianism as the only two political alternatives that are available.** — Correct (80% of students)
Anarchy or totalitarianism. Those are your only options. Never mind democracy, republics, constitutional monarchies, or any of the systems most of the world actually uses. The argument conveniently erases everything between "no government" and "maximum government" so that rejecting one extreme forces you to embrace the other. Classic false dichotomy, beautifully executed.
