## S3 · Q18 — Paradox (EXCEPT)

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
Consumer demand for personal computers continues to increase each year, which might lead one to think that the profits earned selling personal computers at the retail level are very high relative to total retail sales of personal computers. Yet the retail profit margin on personal computer sales is extremely low compared to that of other popular high-technology items.

### Prompt
Each of the following, if true, helps to resolve the apparent discrepancy described above except:

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that ...:** Consumer demand for personal computers continues to increase each year.
- **Why is it that ...:** The retail profit margin on personal computer sales is extremely low compared to that of other high-tech items.
- **Evaluate:** Since more people want PC's every year, the producers of PC's could theoretically raise their prices and still have buyers, thus we might expect them to have high profit levels. But they don't. Maybe the price is already so high that even though people badly want a PC, if the price were raised any further, lot of people couldn't afford one. Maybe there is a ton of competition in the PC space (whereas there isn't for other high-tech items), since competition keeps prices lower.
- **Goal:** We need to find four reasons why PC's have a lower profit margin (or why other popular high-tech items have a higher profit margin).

**Answer choices**
**A. Rapid innovation in computer technology increases the likelihood of a store's stock becoming obsolete.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
Rapid innovation causing stock obsolescence means retailers lose money on unsold inventory. Even with rising demand, if yesterday's model is worthless tomorrow, retailers cannot maintain high margins. This explains why PC margins are lower than for more stable tech products.

**B. Satisfaction with their first personal computer tends to make customers very loyal to that particular brand.** — Correct (54% of students)
Brand loyalty might actually deepen the paradox rather than resolve it. If customers are loyal to a brand, that brand has less competitive pressure and could theoretically raise prices without losing customers — which would increase margins, not explain why they are low. High demand plus brand loyalty should mean higher margins, not lower ones. This does not explain why PC margins are low.

**C. A customer needs more help from store employees when buying a personal computer than when buying other high-technology items.** — Helps Explain (26% of students)
If PCs require more employee assistance than other tech items, retailers face higher labor costs per PC sale. Higher overhead means lower profit margins even with strong demand. Other tech items sold with less employee help have lower costs and thus higher margins.

**D. Many retail stores have low prices on personal computers in order to bring in customers who might buy software and accessories.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
PCs as loss leaders — priced low to attract customers who then buy high-margin accessories and software. Retailers intentionally keep PC margins low as a traffic-generation strategy, similar to how movie theaters price tickets low and profit on concessions.

**E. An increase in the number of discount retail outlets selling personal computers has intensified the competition for customers.** — Helps Explain (4% of students)
More discount outlets create intense price competition, preventing any single retailer from charging high margins. Even with rising demand, competition among many sellers keeps prices — and therefore margins — low. Other high-tech items may face less retail competition.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that...:** Consumer demand for PCs continues to increase each year.
- **Why is it that...:** Retail profit margins on PCs are extremely low compared to other popular high-tech items.
- **Evaluate:** Rising demand usually allows higher prices and profits. Four answers will explain why PC margins remain low despite rising demand. The correct answer (EXCEPT) will not resolve this discrepancy.
- **Goal:** Find the one answer that does not explain low PC profit margins.

**Answer choices**
**A. Rapid innovation in computer technology increases the likelihood of a store's stock becoming obsolete.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
Rapid obsolescence means unsold inventory loses value quickly, eating into margins despite rising demand.

**B. Satisfaction with their first personal computer tends to make customers very loyal to that particular brand.** — Correct (54% of students)
Brand loyalty should allow higher prices, not lower margins. This deepens rather than resolves the paradox.

**C. A customer needs more help from store employees when buying a personal computer than when buying other high-technology items.** — Helps Explain (26% of students)
More employee help per sale means higher labor costs, reducing PC margins compared to simpler-to-sell tech items.

**D. Many retail stores have low prices on personal computers in order to bring in customers who might buy software and accessories.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
PCs are priced low as loss leaders to attract customers for high-margin software and accessories. Intentionally low margins, not accidental.

**E. An increase in the number of discount retail outlets selling personal computers has intensified the competition for customers.** — Helps Explain (4% of students)
More discount retailers competing on PC prices drives margins down despite rising demand. Competition explains low margins.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given that...:** Everyone wants PCs. Demand keeps rising.
- **Why is it that...:** PC stores barely make money on each sale compared to other tech products.
- **Evaluate:** High demand usually means fat profits. Something is keeping PC margins thin. Four answers explain what. One does not.
- **Goal:** Find the odd one out — the answer that does not explain low PC profits.

**Answer choices**
**A. Rapid innovation in computer technology increases the likelihood of a store's stock becoming obsolete.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
Last year's PC is this year's paperweight. Rapid innovation means stock rots on shelves, eating into profits. Rising demand does not help if what people demand is always the newest model.

**B. Satisfaction with their first personal computer tends to make customers very loyal to that particular brand.** — Correct (54% of students)
Loyal customers who keep coming back should mean you can charge more, not less. If Apple fans will pay whatever Apple charges, that should help margins, not hurt them. Brand loyalty is the opposite of an explanation for low margins.

**C. A customer needs more help from store employees when buying a personal computer than when buying other high-technology items.** — Helps Explain (26% of students)
Every PC sale requires a 30-minute conversation with a store employee. That employee costs money. Meanwhile, someone buying a Roomba just grabs the box. Higher service costs per sale eat into PC margins.

**D. Many retail stores have low prices on personal computers in order to bring in customers who might buy software and accessories.** — Helps Explain (8% of students)
The PC is the bait; the cables, cases, and software are the hook. Retailers price PCs low to get people in the door, then make their real money on the accessories. It is the popcorn-at-the-movies model applied to electronics.

**E. An increase in the number of discount retail outlets selling personal computers has intensified the competition for customers.** — Helps Explain (4% of students)
When every store on the block sells PCs, they all race to the bottom on price. Rising demand does not help margins if there are too many sellers fighting for each customer. Competition is the classic margin killer.

---

## S3 · Q19 — Parallel Flaw

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
If a film is accepted by the festival committee, then one of the distributors attending the festival buys it. If a distributor buys a film, the film's financial backers are assured of recouping their investment. This film was not accepted by the festival committee, so this film's financial backers will not recoup their investment.

### Prompt
Which one of the following arguments employs a flawed pattern of reasoning most similar to the flawed pattern of reasoning in the argument above?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** If a film is accepted by the festival committee, then one of the distributors attending the festival buys it. `accepted → distributor buys` If a distributor buys a film, the film's financial backers are assured of recouping their investment. `distributor buys → recoup` This film was not accepted by the festival committee. `~accepted`
- **Conclusion:** This film's financial backers will not recoup their investment. `~recoup`
- **Evaluate:** By connecting the first two premises, we can conclude that if a film is accepted by the festival committee, the film's financial backers are assured of recouping their investment: `accepted → recoup`. To reach the argument's conclusion, we need to assume that if a film is not accepted by the festival committee, the film's financial backers are not assured of recouping their investment: `~accepted → ~recoup`. This is an illegal negation.
- **Goal:** We're looking for an argument that makes an assumption which is an illegal negation of the statements given as evidence.

**Answer choices**
**A. If a film has a good story, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well at the box office. This film did not do well at the box office, so it must not have a good story.** — Bad Validity Match (8% of students)
This argument negates the necessary condition (not do well at the box office) and concludes the sufficient condition is negated (not a good story). That is the contrapositive — a valid inference. The original argument commits an illegal negation by denying the sufficient condition (not accepted) and concluding the necessary condition is negated (not recoup). A valid argument cannot match the pattern of a flawed argument. The structure here is: good story leads to praised, praised leads to do well, not do well, therefore not good story. That chain of contrapositives is logically sound.

**B. If a film features several stars, it will be successful at the box office. A box office success has a long run at theaters. This film features only one star, so it will not have a long run at theaters.** — Correct (67% of students)
This argument replicates the original's flawed structure precisely. The chain is: several stars leads to successful, successful leads to long run. The argument then negates the sufficient condition (not several stars — only one star) and concludes the final necessary condition is negated (not long run). This is the same illegal negation as the original: accepted leads to distributor buys leads to recoup, not accepted, therefore not recoup. In both cases, the sufficient condition is denied and the argument incorrectly infers that the necessary condition must also be denied. The film with one star could still have a long run for reasons unrelated to its star count, just as the original film's backers could recoup through means other than festival acceptance.

**C. If a film is directed by a talented artist, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well on video. This film did well on video, so it was directed by a talented artist.** — Bad Flaw Match (4% of students)
This argument affirms the necessary condition (did well on video) and concludes the sufficient condition is true (directed by a talented artist). That is an affirming the consequent error — a different flaw from the original. The original denies the sufficient condition and concludes the necessary is denied (illegal negation). This answer commits a reversal flaw, not the same illegal negation. The structure is: talented director leads to praised leads to do well, did well, therefore talented director. Both are conditional logic errors, but they are different errors.

**D. If a film is a box office success, then one of its stars is nominated for an award. If a star receives an award, the film is assured of doing well on video. This star was not nominated for an award, so the film will not do well on video.** — Bad Evidence Match (17% of students)
This argument has a more fundamental problem than just the conditional flaw. The first premise says if a film is a box office success, then a star is "nominated" for an award. The second premise says if a star "receives" an award, the film does well on video. "Nominated" and "receives" are different conditions — being nominated for an award is not the same as receiving one. The original argument's chain is seamless: the necessary condition of the first premise (distributor buys) is the sufficient condition of the second premise (distributor buys). Here, the chain is broken by a term shift between "nominated" and "receives." This additional flaw makes the pattern a poor match.

**E. If a film has a big budget, it will be heavily promoted. A heavily promoted film will be released all over the country at once. This film has a big budget, so it will be released all over the country at once.** — Bad Validity Match (5% of students)
This argument correctly connects two conditionals: big budget leads to heavily promoted, heavily promoted leads to released countrywide. It then affirms the sufficient condition (big budget) and concludes the final necessary condition (released countrywide). This is a perfectly valid inference — affirming the antecedent and following the chain forward. The original argument is flawed because it denies the antecedent. This argument is valid because it affirms the antecedent. A valid argument cannot parallel a flawed one.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** This film's financial backers will not recoup their investment.
- **Evidence:** If accepted by the festival committee, a distributor buys the film. If a distributor buys it, backers recoup their investment. This film was not accepted.
- **Evaluate:** The argument chains two conditionals — accepted leads to distributor buys, which leads to recoup — then negates the first condition and concludes the last condition is also negated. That is an illegal negation. The film could still be bought by a distributor through some other means.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that commits the same illegal negation: negating the sufficient condition and concluding the necessary condition is negated.

**Answer choices**
**A. If a film has a good story, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well at the box office. This film did not do well at the box office, so it must not have a good story.** — Bad Validity Match (8% of students)
This argument uses the valid contrapositive: not do well leads to not good story. The original argument illegally negates the sufficient condition. A valid argument cannot match a flawed pattern.

**B. If a film features several stars, it will be successful at the box office. A box office success has a long run at theaters. This film features only one star, so it will not have a long run at theaters.** — Correct (67% of students)
Same flaw: several stars leads to successful leads to long run, not several stars, therefore not long run. This illegally negates the sufficient condition and concludes the necessary is negated — exactly matching the original's error.

**C. If a film is directed by a talented artist, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well on video. This film did well on video, so it was directed by a talented artist.** — Bad Flaw Match (4% of students)
This affirms the necessary condition (did well) and concludes the sufficient (talented director). That is a reversal, not an illegal negation. Different flaw from the original.

**D. If a film is a box office success, then one of its stars is nominated for an award. If a star receives an award, the film is assured of doing well on video. This star was not nominated for an award, so the film will not do well on video.** — Bad Evidence Match (17% of students)
The chain breaks: the first premise says "nominated" but the second says "receives." This term shift creates a different flaw than the original's clean illegal negation.

**E. If a film has a big budget, it will be heavily promoted. A heavily promoted film will be released all over the country at once. This film has a big budget, so it will be released all over the country at once.** — Bad Validity Match (5% of students)
This argument is valid: big budget leads to promoted leads to released countrywide, big budget, therefore released countrywide. Affirming the sufficient condition is correct logic, not a flaw.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** The film's backers will not get their money back. Case closed, apparently.
- **Evidence:** Festival acceptance leads to a distributor buying the film, which leads to recouping the investment. This film was not accepted.
- **Evaluate:** The logic here is like saying "if it rains, the ground gets wet; the ground is wet, so... wait, no — it did not rain, so the ground cannot be wet." The film was not accepted, true. But that does not mean a distributor cannot buy it some other way. The argument illegally negates the sufficient condition and assumes the necessary condition must also be negated. Classic conditional logic error.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that makes the exact same mistake: chaining two if-then statements, negating the first trigger, and incorrectly concluding the final result is negated.

**Answer choices**
**A. If a film has a good story, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well at the box office. This film did not do well at the box office, so it must not have a good story.** — Bad Validity Match (8% of students)
This argument actually gets the logic right. Not doing well at the box office means it was not praised, which means it did not have a good story. That is a perfectly valid chain of contrapositives. The original argument is broken; this one is not. Matching a flaw means committing the same mistake, not accidentally doing it correctly.

**B. If a film features several stars, it will be successful at the box office. A box office success has a long run at theaters. This film features only one star, so it will not have a long run at theaters.** — Correct (67% of students)
Bingo. Several stars leads to success leads to long run. This film only has one star, so no long run. That is the same illegal negation the original pulls: negating the trigger and concluding the end result is also negated. A film with one star could absolutely have a long run — maybe the one star is incredible, maybe the script is phenomenal. The argument just assumes the only path to a long run starts with several stars, which is exactly the mistake the original makes about festival acceptance.

**C. If a film is directed by a talented artist, it will be praised by critics. If a film is praised by critics, it will do well on video. This film did well on video, so it was directed by a talented artist.** — Bad Flaw Match (4% of students)
Close, but wrong flaw. This argument says the film did well on video, therefore it must have been directed by a talented artist. That is a reversal — affirming the consequent. The original argument denies the sufficient condition instead. Both are conditional logic mistakes, but they are different mistakes. It is like confusing a left turn with a right turn because both involve turning.

**D. If a film is a box office success, then one of its stars is nominated for an award. If a star receives an award, the film is assured of doing well on video. This star was not nominated for an award, so the film will not do well on video.** — Bad Evidence Match (17% of students)
Sneaky problem here: the first premise talks about being "nominated" for an award, but the second premise talks about "receiving" one. Those are not the same thing — ask anyone who has been nominated and lost. The original argument's chain links perfectly (distributor buys appears in both premises). This answer has a broken chain on top of the illegal negation, making it a messier flaw than the original.

**E. If a film has a big budget, it will be heavily promoted. A heavily promoted film will be released all over the country at once. This film has a big budget, so it will be released all over the country at once.** — Bad Validity Match (5% of students)
Big budget means heavily promoted, which means released everywhere. This film has a big budget, so it will be released everywhere. That is just... correct. The chain goes forward from an affirmed trigger. The original argument tries to go forward from a denied trigger, which is the error. This answer does not make any error at all.

---

## S3 · Q20 — Necessary Assumption

**Correct answer:** D

### Stimulus
Psychologist: In an experiment, business managers who normally drank coffee on a daily basis were given more than their normal amount. The managers got faster at processing new information, but were less able to integrate it with past information when making decisions. Because successful management depends more on integrating information than on speed, this study demonstrates that drinking more coffee than usual impairs overall management ability.

### Prompt
Which one of the following is an assumption on which the psychologist's argument depends?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Business managers who normally drank coffee on a daily basis were given more than their normal amount. The managers got faster at processing new information, but were less able to integrate it with past information when making decisions. Successful management depends more on integrating information than on speed.
- **Conclusion:** Drinking more coffee than usual impairs overall management ability.
- **Evaluate:** The evidence compares the speed of processing new information to the ability to integrate information. However, the evidence only compares these two specific factors. We aren't told that these two factors are the only ones that influence a person's success as a manager. Most people would agree that other abilities play an important role in successful management. Drinking more coffee than usual might drastically improve a managers' performance in one of these other areas, like attention to detail. The argument doesn't address this. We don't know if any other abilities were affected, or how. Based on evidence that only discusses those two specific factors, the conclusion makes a significant leap to a claim about overall management ability. We can't draw this conclusion about overall ability when it's very possible that other factors are at least equally as important. Since other factors are likely to be at least equally important, we need to know how coffee affects those factors.
- **Goal:** A correct answer is likely to rule out the possibility that drinking additional coffee improves other abilities that play an equal or even more important role in overall management ability.

**Answer choices**
**A. The business managers in the experiment were different from each other in both the speed at which they processed information and in their ability to integrate information.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
Differences between individual managers in the study are irrelevant to the argument's logic. The argument compares what happens when managers drink normal amounts of coffee versus extra coffee. Whether the managers differed from each other in baseline abilities does not affect whether extra coffee impairs overall management ability. The argument could hold whether the managers were identical or wildly different in their individual capacities.

**B. Drinking less coffee than usual would not impede a manager's overall management ability as much as would drinking more coffee than usual.** — Out of Scope (9% of students)
The argument is about what happens when managers drink more coffee than usual. What happens when they drink less coffee is a different question entirely. The conclusion could be true regardless of whether drinking less coffee helps, hurts, or has no effect. Negating this answer — even if drinking less coffee impeded management ability just as much — would not destroy the conclusion that extra coffee impairs management ability.

**C. There is no other factor that is more important to successful business management than either speed of processing information or ability to integrate it.** — Out of Scope (17% of students)
This answer is tempting because it addresses other factors in management. But it says no other factor is more important than speed or integration — it does not say anything about whether extra coffee affects those other factors. The argument's gap is not about the relative importance of factors but about whether coffee improves other factors enough to offset the integration loss. Even if speed and integration are the two most important factors, a modest improvement in a third factor could still offset the integration loss. The answer would need to address coffee's effect on other factors, not just their importance ranking.

**D. In the experiment, drinking more coffee than usual did not have beneficial effects on overall management ability that outweighed the reduction in ability to integrate past information.** — Correct (69% of students)
Apply the negation test: what if drinking more coffee did have beneficial effects on overall management ability that outweighed the reduction in integration? Then the conclusion — that extra coffee impairs overall management ability — would collapse. The conclusion depends on there being no offsetting benefits large enough to overcome the integration loss. This answer directly addresses the argument's gap: it rules out the possibility that extra coffee improves other management skills enough to produce a net positive effect. Without this assumption, the argument cannot move from "integration got worse" to "overall ability got worse."

**E. The amount of coffee one drinks is the most important factor influencing the speed at which one processes new information.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
Whether coffee is the most important factor affecting processing speed is irrelevant. The argument already establishes as experimental fact that extra coffee increased processing speed. The argument does not need coffee to be the primary driver of speed — it only needs to know the consequences of the observed speed increase and integration decrease on overall management ability. This answer supports a premise that is already established rather than bridging the gap to the conclusion.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Drinking more coffee than usual impairs overall management ability.
- **Evidence:** Managers given extra coffee processed information faster but integrated information with past knowledge worse. Successful management depends more on integration than speed.
- **Evaluate:** The argument only measures two factors — speed and integration — but concludes about overall management ability. Other factors could be affected by extra coffee in ways that improve overall ability, offsetting the integration loss.
- **Goal:** Find the assumption that the gains from extra coffee in other areas do not outweigh the integration loss.

**Answer choices**
**A. The business managers in the experiment were different from each other in both the speed at which they processed information and in their ability to integrate information.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
Individual differences between managers do not matter. The argument compares the effect of normal versus extra coffee, not differences between managers.

**B. Drinking less coffee than usual would not impede a manager's overall management ability as much as would drinking more coffee than usual.** — Out of Scope (9% of students)
The effect of drinking less coffee is irrelevant. The argument is only about drinking more coffee than usual.

**C. There is no other factor that is more important to successful business management than either speed of processing information or ability to integrate it.** — Out of Scope (17% of students)
Knowing that no other factor is more important does not help. The argument needs to know whether coffee improves those other factors, not how they rank in importance.

**D. In the experiment, drinking more coffee than usual did not have beneficial effects on overall management ability that outweighed the reduction in ability to integrate past information.** — Correct (69% of students)
Negate it: if extra coffee did have benefits that outweighed the integration loss, the conclusion fails. The argument must assume no such offsetting benefits exist.

**E. The amount of coffee one drinks is the most important factor influencing the speed at which one processes new information.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
The experiment already showed coffee increases processing speed. Whether coffee is the most important factor for speed is irrelevant — the argument needs to connect the measured effects to overall ability.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Extra coffee makes you a worse manager overall. Time to switch to decaf, apparently.
- **Evidence:** Caffeinated managers processed information faster but integrated it worse. And integration matters more than speed for management success.
- **Evaluate:** The study measured exactly two things: speed and integration. But management involves a lot more than those two skills. Maybe the extra coffee also made managers better at motivating their teams, catching errors, or staying focused in long meetings. The argument assumes that the study captured everything that matters, but it only captured two variables out of potentially dozens.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that plugs this gap — the one that says the unmeasured benefits of extra coffee do not outweigh the measured cost to integration.

**Answer choices**
**A. The business managers in the experiment were different from each other in both the speed at which they processed information and in their ability to integrate information.** — Out of Scope (1% of students)
So the managers were different from each other. In other news, people are different. The argument does not care whether Manager A integrates faster than Manager B. It cares about what happens when any of them drink extra coffee. Individual variation is background noise, not a load-bearing assumption.

**B. Drinking less coffee than usual would not impede a manager's overall management ability as much as would drinking more coffee than usual.** — Out of Scope (9% of students)
What happens when managers drink less coffee? The argument could not care less. It tested extra coffee and found it hurt integration more than it helped speed. Whether cutting back on coffee is also bad, worse, or the same is a completely separate research question that this argument does not need answered.

**C. There is no other factor that is more important to successful business management than either speed of processing information or ability to integrate it.** — Out of Scope (17% of students)
This seems like a winner at first glance — if speed and integration are the top two skills, maybe nothing else matters much. But that is not the gap. The gap is whether extra coffee improves other skills enough to make up for the integration loss. Even a less important skill, if massively improved by caffeine, could tip the overall balance. This answer identifies the leaderboard ranking but not whether coffee reshuffles the scores.

**D. In the experiment, drinking more coffee than usual did not have beneficial effects on overall management ability that outweighed the reduction in ability to integrate past information.** — Correct (69% of students)
This is the safety net the argument needs. If extra coffee turned managers into integration-impaired speed demons but also made them incredible leaders, brilliant communicators, and laser-focused decision-makers, the overall effect might still be positive. The argument needs to assume that does not happen. Negate this answer and the whole thing falls apart: extra coffee might make you worse at integration but better at everything else, leaving you a better manager overall. The argument cannot survive that possibility.

**E. The amount of coffee one drinks is the most important factor influencing the speed at which one processes new information.** — Out of Scope (4% of students)
Coffee is the number one speed booster? Great, but the argument already proved that in the experiment. The managers drank extra coffee and got faster. Done. What the argument still needs is a reason to believe that the speed-integration tradeoff means overall management gets worse. This answer is reinforcing a premise instead of building the bridge to the conclusion.

---

## S3 · Q21 — Must be True

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Orator: Moral excellence can be achieved only by repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do the wrong thing. Overcoming these inclinations is often difficult to do, even for a morally virtuous person, but the only way to become a morally virtuous person is through the achievement of moral excellence.

### Prompt
Which one of the following is a conclusion that follows logically from the orator's statements?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Moral excellence can be achieved only by repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do the wrong thing. `moral excel → overcome` Overcoming these inclinations is often difficult to do, even for a morally virtuous person. The only way to become a morally virtuous person is through the achievement of moral excellence. `moral virtue → moral excel`
- **Evaluate:** By connecting the final statement to the first statement, we can infer that if someone becomes a morally virtuous person, they have repeatedly overcome inclinations to do the wrong thing. `moral virtue → overcome`. We're also told that overcoming inclinations to do the wrong thing is often difficult to do. From this, we can infer that becoming a morally virtuous person is often difficult to do.
- **Goal:** Expect the correct answer to involve one of these two inferences that we made by connecting the statements.

**Answer choices**
**A. A morally virtuous person is incapable of doing the wrong thing.** — Too Strong (4% of students)
The stimulus says morally virtuous people have repeatedly overcome inclinations to do wrong — not that they are incapable of doing wrong. Overcoming an inclination means resisting a desire, which is entirely different from being unable to act on it. A person who repeatedly resists the urge to speed is not incapable of speeding. The word "incapable" introduces an absolute that the premises do not support.

**B. Most people who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous.** — Too Strong (12% of students)
From the premises, we know that moral virtue requires moral excellence. This means all morally virtuous people have achieved moral excellence. We can infer that some people who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous. But we cannot infer that most people who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous. There could be many people who achieve moral excellence without becoming morally virtuous — perhaps moral excellence is necessary but not sufficient on its own. "Most" exceeds what the premises logically guarantee.

**C. Someone who has no inclination to do anything that is wrong has achieved moral excellence.** — Reversal (9% of students)
The first premise says moral excellence requires repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do wrong. The contrapositive is: if someone has not repeatedly overcome inclinations to do wrong, they have not achieved moral excellence. This answer says someone with no inclination to do wrong has achieved moral excellence — which is neither the original conditional nor its contrapositive. Someone with no inclinations to do wrong has no inclinations to overcome, which means they have not met the condition for moral excellence. If anything, this answer contradicts the premises.

**D. Someone who is not morally virtuous is incapable of achieving moral excellence.** — Illegal Negation (14% of students)
The third premise says moral virtue requires moral excellence (moral virtue leads to moral excellence). The contrapositive is: no moral excellence leads to no moral virtue. This answer negates both sides without reversing: not morally virtuous leads to incapable of moral excellence. That is an illegal negation — from "virtue requires excellence," we cannot conclude "lack of virtue prevents excellence." Someone who is not yet morally virtuous could still achieve moral excellence; they just have not yet become virtuous through it.

**E. Every morally virtuous person has been inclined to do something that is wrong.** — Correct (60% of students)
Chain the premises: moral virtue requires moral excellence, and moral excellence requires repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do wrong. Therefore, every morally virtuous person has repeatedly overcome inclinations to do wrong. To overcome an inclination, one must first have that inclination. Therefore, every morally virtuous person has been inclined to do something wrong. This follows necessarily from the premises — there is no way to be morally virtuous without having had wrong inclinations to overcome.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Moral excellence requires repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do wrong. Overcoming these inclinations is often difficult. The only way to become morally virtuous is through moral excellence.
- **Evaluate:** Chain the conditionals: moral virtue requires moral excellence, which requires repeatedly overcoming inclinations to do wrong. So every morally virtuous person has repeatedly overcome such inclinations — meaning they must have had inclinations to do wrong in the first place.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that follows logically from connecting these statements.

**Answer choices**
**A. A morally virtuous person is incapable of doing the wrong thing.** — Too Strong (4% of students)
"Incapable" is too strong. Overcoming an inclination means resisting it, not being physically unable to act on it.

**B. Most people who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous.** — Too Strong (12% of students)
We can infer "some" who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous, but "most" goes beyond what the premises support. Moral excellence might be necessary but not sufficient for virtue.

**C. Someone who has no inclination to do anything that is wrong has achieved moral excellence.** — Reversal (9% of students)
Having no inclination to do wrong means there is nothing to overcome, which means moral excellence has not been achieved. This answer gets the logic backwards.

**D. Someone who is not morally virtuous is incapable of achieving moral excellence.** — Illegal Negation (14% of students)
This illegally negates the conditional. From "moral virtue requires moral excellence" we cannot conclude "lack of virtue prevents moral excellence."

**E. Every morally virtuous person has been inclined to do something that is wrong.** — Correct (60% of students)
Moral virtue requires moral excellence, which requires overcoming wrong inclinations. You cannot overcome an inclination you never had. So every virtuous person must have been inclined to do wrong.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** To be morally excellent, you have to keep overcoming the urge to do bad things. To be morally virtuous, you have to achieve moral excellence. And overcoming those urges is hard — even for virtuous people.
- **Evaluate:** Connect the dots: virtuous people achieved moral excellence, which means they repeatedly overcame inclinations to do wrong. You cannot overcome an inclination you never had. So every virtuous person has, at some point, wanted to do the wrong thing. Virtue is not the absence of temptation — it is winning the fight against it.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that captures this logical consequence: every morally virtuous person has been inclined to do wrong.

**Answer choices**
**A. A morally virtuous person is incapable of doing the wrong thing.** — Too Strong (4% of students)
If morally virtuous people were incapable of doing wrong, what exactly would they be "overcoming"? The whole point of the orator's argument is that virtuous people fight temptation repeatedly. Fighting implies the option to lose. "Incapable" removes the fight entirely and contradicts the premise that started this chain.

**B. Most people who achieve moral excellence are morally virtuous.** — Too Strong (12% of students)
"Most" is doing too much heavy lifting here. Every virtuous person achieved moral excellence, but there is no way to know what fraction of morally excellent people became virtuous. Maybe moral excellence is common but virtue is rare. The premises only guarantee "some," not "most."

**C. Someone who has no inclination to do anything that is wrong has achieved moral excellence.** — Reversal (9% of students)
No inclination to do wrong? Then there is nothing to overcome. And if there is nothing to overcome, you cannot achieve moral excellence through the method the orator describes. This answer is like saying "someone who has never been in a fight is a championship boxer." The whole point is that moral excellence comes from the struggle, not from the absence of it.

**D. Someone who is not morally virtuous is incapable of achieving moral excellence.** — Illegal Negation (14% of students)
This flips the logic. The orator says you need moral excellence to become virtuous. This answer says if you are not virtuous, you cannot achieve moral excellence. That is backwards. Maybe moral excellence is the first step and virtue comes later — you could be morally excellent but not yet virtuous. The arrow only points one direction.

**E. Every morally virtuous person has been inclined to do something that is wrong.** — Correct (60% of students)
The logic is airtight. Virtuous people achieved moral excellence. Moral excellence requires repeatedly fighting off the urge to do wrong. You cannot fight an urge you never had. So every virtuous person has, at some point, been tempted to do something wrong. Virtue is not saintly innocence — it is battle-tested resistance. No temptation, no overcoming. No overcoming, no moral excellence. No moral excellence, no moral virtue.

---

## S3 · Q22 — Role

**Correct answer:** C

### Stimulus
Philosopher: Philosophers usually treat emotions as nonrational. But emotion is not nonrational: it only seems that way because language lacks the ability to convey adequate conceptions of emotion. The words we use to refer to emotions name only very general kinds of inner experience—excitement, calm, joy, and so on. Thus, for example, there is no language for describing just how one joy differs from another.

### Prompt
Which one of the following most accurately describes the role played in the philosopher's argument by the proposition that there is no language for describing just how one joy differs from another?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Emotion is not nonrational.
- **Intermediate Conclusion:** Emotion only seems nonrational way because language lacks the ability to convey adequate conceptions of emotion.
- **Evidence:** The words we use to refer to emotions name only very general kinds of inner experience, such as excitement / calm / joy, but there is no language for describing just how one joy differs from another.
- **Evaluate:** Whenever we do Main Conclusion / Role, we listen for Rebuttal/Pivot conclusions (most of them are that style) or First Sentence conclusions (the 2nd most common style). Since this paragraph begins with an Attribution, "Philosophers usually think of emotions as X", we'd be waiting for a Pivot + Pushback, and so when we see "But emotion is not nonrational", we most likely have our rebuttal conclusion. Everything after that is support for the main conclusion. They're asking us about the last sentence, which is hella confusing because it's prefaced by Thus (which usually indicates conclusion) and by "for example" (which always indicates support). How can the last claim be a conclusion and a premise?! We need to remember that premise/conclusion indicator words can also be used to indicate Cause/Effect. "Since I have the flu, I have a fever." / "I have the flu. Thus, I have a fever." That's why we see a Thus in front of this supporting idea. The previous sentence causes this last claim to be true. The fact that we have words for only general emotional states is why we don't have specific words for different types of joy.
- **Goal:** We know this final claim plays a supporting role, and we know that the main conclusion is "Emotion is not nonrational", that's good enough to head to the answers.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is an example of the phenomenon that the argument seeks to explain.** — Bad Conclusion Match (27% of students)
When it comes to the main conclusion, the argument does not seek to explain a phenomenon. It seeks to oppose the idea that emotions are nonrational. Emotions are a phenomenon, but the author isn't trying to explain why emotions happen. She's just trying to classify them as rational or nonrational. (To explain a phenomenon means to provide a causal explanation for it) Within the subsidiary argument, the author is trying to provide a causal explanation for the phenomenon of why people think emotions are nonrational. But an example of that phenomenon would be a specific case where a person thought of an emotion as nonrational. The last sentence doesn't resemble that at all, so even if we were being charitable to this answer, it breaks down.

**B. It is the main conclusion of the argument.** — Wrong Role (7% of students)
The main conclusion is that emotion is not nonrational. The target statement — about one joy being indistinguishable from another in language — is evidence supporting the general claim about language's limitations, which in turn supports the conclusion. The word "thus" before this statement might suggest it is a conclusion, but "for example" immediately overrides that signal, identifying the statement as an illustration. The conclusion appears earlier in the argument, not at the end.

**C. It is a specific instance illustrating a general claim, thereby indirectly supporting the conclusion.** — Correct (57% of students)
The target statement illustrates a specific instance of the general claim that emotion-words name only very general kinds of experience. "Joy" is a general word, and there is no language to capture how one joy differs from another — that is a specific example of language's generality. This general claim about language, in turn, supports the conclusion that emotion is not nonrational but merely appears so because of linguistic limitations. The target statement thus indirectly supports the conclusion by illustrating the evidence that directly supports it.

**D. It is a concession to the view that the argument seeks to refute.** — Wrong Role (1% of students)
A concession would acknowledge the opposing view's validity. The opposing view is that emotions are nonrational. The target statement — about language's inability to distinguish joys — does not concede that emotions are nonrational. Instead, it supports the argument's rebuttal of that view by showing that language is inadequate, which is why emotions merely appear nonrational. The target statement works against the opposing view, not in its favor.

**E. It is the explanation proposed for the phenomenon the argument seeks to explain.** — Wrong Role (8% of students)
The explanation proposed for why emotions seem nonrational is that "language lacks the ability to convey adequate conceptions of emotion." That appears in the second sentence. The target statement — about one joy being indistinguishable from another — is an example illustrating that explanation, not the explanation itself. This answer confuses the general explanation with the specific illustration. The explanation is the broad claim about language; the target statement is one piece of supporting evidence for that claim.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Emotion is not nonrational — it only seems that way because language cannot adequately convey emotions.
- **Evidence:** Words for emotions name only general kinds of experience. For example, there is no language for describing how one joy differs from another.
- **Evaluate:** The statement in the question stem — about one joy not being describable as different from another — is an example supporting the general claim about language's limitations. It is evidence, not the conclusion.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that correctly identifies this statement as a specific example illustrating the general claim about language, which in turn supports the conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is an example of the phenomenon that the argument seeks to explain.** — Bad Description (27% of students)
The target statement illustrates why language is limited, not an example of emotions seeming nonrational. It is part of the explanation, not an example of the phenomenon being explained.

**B. It is the main conclusion of the argument.** — Wrong Role (7% of students)
The conclusion is "emotion is not nonrational." The target statement is evidence, not the conclusion. "For example" identifies it as an illustration, despite "thus" suggesting otherwise.

**C. It is a specific instance illustrating a general claim, thereby indirectly supporting the conclusion.** — Correct (57% of students)
The statement is a specific example of the general claim that emotion-words are too general. That general claim supports the conclusion. So the statement indirectly supports the conclusion through illustration.

**D. It is a concession to the view that the argument seeks to refute.** — Wrong Role (1% of students)
This statement supports the rebuttal of the opposing view, not a concession to it. It shows language is limited, which is evidence against the claim that emotions are nonrational.

**E. It is the explanation proposed for the phenomenon the argument seeks to explain.** — Wrong Role (8% of students)
The explanation is "language lacks the ability to convey emotions." The target statement is an example supporting that explanation, not the explanation itself.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Emotions are not nonrational. They just seem that way because the available words are too blunt to capture them properly.
- **Evidence:** Emotion-words like "joy," "excitement," and "calm" are hopelessly general. Case in point: there is no way to describe how one experience of joy differs from another. The word "joy" covers everything from finding a parking spot to holding a newborn.
- **Evaluate:** The target statement — no language for distinguishing joys — is a concrete example backing up the general claim. Do not let "thus" fool you into thinking it is the conclusion. "For example" immediately overrides that and confirms it is illustrative evidence. The conclusion is up top: emotions are not nonrational.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that says this statement is a specific illustration of a general claim, used to support the conclusion.

**Answer choices**
**A. It is an example of the phenomenon that the argument seeks to explain.** — Bad Description (27% of students)
The word "example" in this answer is a trap. Yes, the target statement is an example — but of the wrong thing. The phenomenon being explained is "emotions seem nonrational." The target statement is an example of "language cannot capture emotions" — which is the explanation for the phenomenon, not an instance of it. It is like confusing a symptom with a cause because both happen to involve examples.

**B. It is the main conclusion of the argument.** — Wrong Role (7% of students)
The last sentence is not always the conclusion — and this is a perfect example of that. "Thus, for example" is the LSAT's version of a red herring. "Thus" whispers "conclusion" while "for example" shouts "evidence." The conclusion is the earlier claim that emotion is not nonrational. This statement is just there to illustrate why language makes emotions look nonrational.

**C. It is a specific instance illustrating a general claim, thereby indirectly supporting the conclusion.** — Correct (57% of students)
Nailed it. "Joy" is one word for a thousand different feelings — and that is a specific example of the broader point that emotion-words are too general. That broader point is what actually supports the conclusion. So the target statement is one step removed from the conclusion: it backs up the general claim, which backs up the conclusion. Specific instance of a general claim, indirectly supporting the conclusion. Every word in this answer earns its keep.

**D. It is a concession to the view that the argument seeks to refute.** — Wrong Role (1% of students)
A concession would be the philosopher saying "okay, fair point, emotions do look nonrational." But the target statement is not admitting anything to the opposition. It is explaining why the opposition got fooled — language is too blunt to capture emotions, so emotions look nonrational. That is evidence for the philosopher's own position, not a concession to the other side.

**E. It is the explanation proposed for the phenomenon the argument seeks to explain.** — Wrong Role (8% of students)
Close, but one level off. The explanation for why emotions seem nonrational is "language cannot properly convey emotions" — that is the big-picture answer. The target statement is one example of that limitation. It is evidence for the explanation, not the explanation itself. It is like confusing a case study with the theory it supports.

---

## S3 · Q23 — Most Supported

**Correct answer:** E

### Stimulus
Superstring theory is a controversial new theory in physics that purports, unlike more established physical theories, to explain the nature and existence of gravity. A major problem with superstring theory is that to test it we would have to build a particle accelerator 100 trillion kilometers long. Another problem is that superstring theory has had no success in adequately explaining why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.

### Prompt
Which one of the following is most strongly supported by the information above?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Superstring theory is a controversial new theory in physics. Superstring theory is unlike more established physical theories in that it purports to explain the nature and existence of gravity. A major problem with superstring theory is that to test it we would have to build a particle accelerator 100 trillion kilometers long. Another problem is that superstring theory has had no success in adequately explaining why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.
- **Evaluate:** It's interesting that superstring theory is controversial and unlike more established theories. People who study it are probably fun to hang out with. But the statements seem to mainly focus on the goal of superstring theory—to explain the nature and existence of gravity—and on two problems with superstring theory. We would expect an answer to focus on those details.
- **Goal:** There aren't any clear conditional or quantified statements to link. We'll look for answers that seem closely related to the information we've been given, and watch out for incorrect answers that bring in irrelevant or unsupported details.

**Answer choices**
**A. Superstring theory would be more successful if superstring theorists attempted to explain why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Unsupported (17% of students)
The stimulus says superstring theory has had "no success" in explaining gravity's strength. This does not imply that theorists have not attempted to explain it — they may have tried and failed. "No success" describes an outcome, not a lack of effort. This answer assumes the problem is one of neglect rather than inability, which the stimulus does not support.

**B. Physical theories that are better established than superstring theory provide better explanations of physical phenomena than does superstring theory.** — Unsupported (11% of students)
The stimulus says superstring theory purports to explain gravity, unlike more established theories. It mentions two problems with superstring theory. But it does not compare the quality of explanations between superstring theory and established theories for physical phenomena generally. We do not know whether established theories explain other phenomena better, worse, or the same as superstring theory does. The stimulus only contrasts them on the specific point of claiming to explain gravity.

**C. Some physical theory more established than superstring theory has had at least some success in explaining why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Contradicted (19% of students)
The stimulus says superstring theory "purports, unlike more established physical theories, to explain the nature and existence of gravity." The phrase "unlike more established physical theories" indicates that established theories do not claim to explain gravity. If they do not even purport to explain gravity, it is unlikely they have had success explaining gravity's strength. This answer is undermined rather than supported by the stimulus.

**D. A physical theory cannot be true if testing that theory would require us to build a particle accelerator 100 trillion kilometers long.** — Too Strong (2% of students)
The inability to test superstring theory is described as a "problem," but the stimulus does not say that untestable theories cannot be true. A theory might be true even if we lack the means to verify it. The stimulus identifies practical obstacles to testing, not a logical impossibility of truth. This answer makes a much stronger claim than the stimulus supports — it equates untestability with impossibility of truth, which is a philosophical leap the stimulus does not take.

**E. A theory that purports to explain the nature of a force is deficient if it cannot account for the strength of that force.** — Correct (52% of students)
The stimulus says superstring theory purports to explain the nature and existence of gravity. It also says a problem with the theory is that it cannot adequately explain why gravity has its particular strength. The fact that this failure is characterized as a "problem" supports the general principle that a theory claiming to explain the nature of a force is deficient if it cannot account for that force's strength. The specific case of superstring theory illustrates this principle: it claims to explain gravity but cannot explain gravity's strength, and this is identified as a deficiency.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Superstring theory is a controversial new physics theory that, unlike more established theories, claims to explain gravity's nature and existence. Two problems: testing requires a 100-trillion-kilometer accelerator, and it has not adequately explained gravity's strength.
- **Evaluate:** The stimulus presents facts about superstring theory's goals and shortcomings. The theory aims to explain gravity but cannot explain gravity's strength, which is identified as a "problem." This suggests that failing to account for a force's strength is a deficiency for a theory claiming to explain that force.
- **Goal:** Find the answer best supported by these facts without going beyond what is stated.

**Answer choices**
**A. Superstring theory would be more successful if superstring theorists attempted to explain why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Unsupported (17% of students)
"No success" means they may have tried and failed, not that they have not attempted it. This answer assumes neglect rather than inability.

**B. Physical theories that are better established than superstring theory provide better explanations of physical phenomena than does superstring theory.** — Unsupported (11% of students)
The stimulus only contrasts the theories on whether they claim to explain gravity, not on which provides better explanations of physical phenomena generally.

**C. Some physical theory more established than superstring theory has had at least some success in explaining why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Contradicted (19% of students)
Established theories do not even claim to explain gravity, so they are unlikely to have explained its strength. The stimulus undermines rather than supports this answer.

**D. A physical theory cannot be true if testing that theory would require us to build a particle accelerator 100 trillion kilometers long.** — Too Strong (2% of students)
The stimulus says testing is impractical, not that an untestable theory cannot be true. This answer makes a philosophical claim the stimulus does not support.

**E. A theory that purports to explain the nature of a force is deficient if it cannot account for the strength of that force.** — Correct (52% of students)
Superstring theory claims to explain gravity but cannot explain its strength — and the stimulus calls this a "problem." This supports the principle that a theory explaining a force's nature should account for its strength.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Given:** Superstring theory wants to explain gravity. Big ambition. Two problems: you would need a particle accelerator roughly the diameter of the solar system to test it, and it cannot even explain why gravity is the strength it is.
- **Evaluate:** The theory says "it can explain gravity" and then cannot explain one of gravity's most basic properties — its strength. And the stimulus calls that a "problem." So apparently, if a theory claims to explain a force, it should be able to account for how strong it is. That seems like a reasonable inference the stimulus is nudging toward.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that captures this principle: claiming to explain a force but failing to explain its strength is a deficiency.

**Answer choices**
**A. Superstring theory would be more successful if superstring theorists attempted to explain why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Unsupported (17% of students)
"If they just tried, they would succeed" — that is a bold assumption. The stimulus says they have had no success, not that they have not tried. Maybe they have been trying for decades and keep hitting dead ends. "No success" is a results report, not a to-do list item they forgot to check off.

**B. Physical theories that are better established than superstring theory provide better explanations of physical phenomena than does superstring theory.** — Unsupported (11% of students)
The stimulus says established theories do not even try to explain gravity — they leave that to superstring theory. But "does not try to explain gravity" does not mean "explains everything else better." The comparison in the stimulus is about scope of ambition, not quality of results. Maybe established theories are great at their own thing, maybe not. The stimulus does not say.

**C. Some physical theory more established than superstring theory has had at least some success in explaining why the force of gravity is not stronger or weaker than it is.** — Contradicted (19% of students)
The stimulus says superstring theory is unique in claiming to explain gravity. Other theories do not even make that claim. So why would those other theories have had success explaining why gravity is a particular strength? That would be like saying a car mechanic has had success performing brain surgery — it is not in their job description. The stimulus points away from this answer, not toward it.

**D. A physical theory cannot be true if testing that theory would require us to build a particle accelerator 100 trillion kilometers long.** — Too Strong (2% of students)
Untestable does not mean untrue. The stimulus calls the testing difficulty a "problem" — which it is, for science. But plenty of things are true that cannot currently be tested. The stimulus is raising a practical concern, not declaring a universal law about truth requiring testability. This answer takes a reasonable observation and cranks it up to an absolute philosophical position the stimulus never endorses.

**E. A theory that purports to explain the nature of a force is deficient if it cannot account for the strength of that force.** — Correct (52% of students)
Superstring theory says it can explain gravity. But it cannot explain why gravity is as strong (or as weak) as it is. And the stimulus calls that a "problem." Put those pieces together and you get a pretty clear principle: if you claim to explain a force, you had better be able to explain how strong it is. The stimulus serves up this inference on a platter — the specific case of superstring theory is a textbook example of the general principle this answer describes.

---

## S3 · Q24 — Parallel

**Correct answer:** B

### Stimulus
A smaller number of short documentary films than of full-length science-fiction films are commercially successful, even though there are more short documentary films than there are science-fiction films. Therefore, a higher proportion of full-length science-fiction films than of short documentary films are commercially successful.

### Prompt
Which one of the following arguments is most similar in its pattern of reasoning to the argument above?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** The number of commercially successful short documentary films is smaller than the number of commercially successful full-length science-fiction films. There are more short documentary films than there are science-fiction films.
- **Conclusion:** The proportion of full-length science-fiction films that are commercially successful is higher the proportion of short documentary films that are commercially successful.
- **Evaluate:** It can be helpful to rephrase the statements in this argument as we've done here, hopefully stating them in a clearer way. When dealing with a question like this, we might also take some notes: `# com succ short doc < # com succ FL sci-fi`, `# short doc > # sci-fi`, Therefore, `% com succ FL sci-fi > % com succ short doc`. Notice that the argument starts with evidence based on the number of films of each type, then switches to a conclusion about proportions of films in each type. This could indicate a flaw, but let's not worry about that for a moment. We should also note that the evidence first mentions full-length science-fiction films, then switches to discussing science-fiction films in general.
- **Goal:** We'll look for answers that contain these same patterns in the evidence and conclusion. We can dig deeper to identify any flaws in this argument so we can match those to the correct answer choice, but let's only do that if we need to.

**Answer choices**
**A. A greater number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. Hence, a smaller percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
This answer has only one numerical comparison in its evidence: more small modern apartments than large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. It is missing the second comparison about total numbers that the original argument includes. The original has two premises (fewer successful, more total) leading to a proportion conclusion. This answer jumps from one premise to a proportion conclusion, making it structurally incomplete as a parallel.

**B. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are more modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments. Thus, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Correct (44% of students)
This answer replicates the original's structure precisely. First premise: more large Victorian apartments than modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs (parallel to: more full-length sci-fi films than short documentaries are successful). Second premise: there are more modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments (parallel to: there are more short documentaries than science-fiction films). Conclusion: a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs (parallel to: a higher proportion of full-length sci-fi films are successful). The modifier shift is also preserved: "large Victorian apartments" in the first premise becomes "Victorian apartments" in the second — mirroring the shift from "full-length science-fiction films" to "science-fiction films."

**C. A smaller number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. For even though there are more small modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (40% of students)
This answer reverses the relationship between evidence and conclusion. The word "for" in the second sentence is a premise indicator, meaning the proportion comparison and the total count comparison are both premises, while the raw number comparison is the conclusion. The original argument uses raw numbers and totals as premises to reach a proportion conclusion. This answer uses proportions as a premise to reach a number conclusion — the reasoning runs in the opposite direction.

**D. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, so there are more small modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments, since a higher percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
The conclusion indicator "so" and premise indicator "since" reveal the structure: the number comparison and the proportion comparison are premises, while the total count comparison is the conclusion. The original argument concludes with proportions; this answer concludes with total counts. The logical direction is different: the original uses totals as evidence for proportions, while this answer uses proportions as evidence for totals.

**E. A smaller number of modern apartments than of Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are fewer modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments. This establishes that a higher proportion of Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (10% of students)
This answer has several structural mismatches. The modifier shift goes in the wrong direction: it moves from "Victorian apartments" to "large Victorian apartments" in the evidence, whereas the original moves from "full-length science-fiction films" to "science-fiction films" (dropping the modifier). Also, the conclusion refers to "Victorian apartments" broadly rather than the modified form "large Victorian apartments," which is the opposite of the original's pattern where the conclusion restores the modifier. The overall numbers-to-proportions pattern is present, but the term shift pattern is reversed.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** Fewer short documentary films than full-length science-fiction films are commercially successful. There are more short documentary films than science-fiction films total.
- **Conclusion:** A higher proportion of full-length science-fiction films than of short documentary films are commercially successful.
- **Evaluate:** The argument moves from raw numbers to proportions. Also note the shift from "full-length science-fiction films" in the first premise to just "science-fiction films" in the second. The correct answer must match both the reasoning pattern and any term shifts.
- **Goal:** Find the answer with the same structure: fewer of A than B are X, more A than B exist total, therefore a higher proportion of B than A are X — with a parallel modifier shift.

**Answer choices**
**A. A greater number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. Hence, a smaller percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
This answer has only one numerical comparison, not two. The original argument uses two premises — fewer successful plus more total — to reach a proportion conclusion.

**B. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are more modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments. Thus, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Correct (44% of students)
Perfect structural match: two number comparisons leading to a proportion conclusion, with the same modifier shift ("large Victorian" to just "Victorian" parallels "full-length science-fiction" to just "science-fiction").

**C. A smaller number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. For even though there are more small modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (40% of students)
"For" marks the proportion and total count as premises, making the raw number comparison the conclusion. The original goes from numbers to proportions, not the reverse.

**D. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, so there are more small modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments, since a higher percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
"So" marks the total count as the conclusion, and "since" marks the proportion as a premise. The original concludes with proportions, not total counts.

**E. A smaller number of modern apartments than of Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are fewer modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments. This establishes that a higher proportion of Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (10% of students)
The modifier shift runs backwards: this adds "large" in the second premise, while the original drops "full-length." The conclusion also uses the wrong scope — broad "Victorian" instead of the modified form.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Evidence:** More sci-fi films succeed commercially than documentaries, but there are more documentaries in total than sci-fi films.
- **Conclusion:** Therefore, sci-fi films have a higher commercial success rate. Makes sense — fewer successes from a bigger pool means a lower rate.
- **Evaluate:** The reasoning is straightforward, but watch for the sneaky term shift: "full-length science-fiction films" in one premise becomes just "science-fiction films" in the next. The correct answer needs to match both the numbers-to-proportions logic and this kind of modifier shift.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that walks the same logical path with the same term shift pattern.

**Answer choices**
**A. A greater number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. Hence, a smaller percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match (3% of students)
The original argument has two building blocks before its conclusion: count of successful items and total count. This answer only has one building block — count of apartments with tubs — and then leaps to a proportion conclusion. That is like trying to build a sandwich with one slice of bread. The structure does not match.

**B. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are more modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments. Thus, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Correct (44% of students)
This is the match. More Victorian apartments with tubs than modern ones, more modern apartments in total, therefore a higher proportion of Victorians have tubs. Identical logic to the original. And the cherry on top: "large Victorian apartments" in one premise quietly becomes just "Victorian apartments" in the next — the same kind of modifier drop that the original pulls with "full-length science-fiction" becoming just "science-fiction." Structural twin, right down to the sneaky term shift.

**C. A smaller number of small modern apartments than of large Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs. For even though there are more small modern apartments than there are Victorian apartments, a higher proportion of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (40% of students)
Watch the word "for" — it is a premise indicator hiding in plain sight. It flips the structure: the proportion claim is now evidence, and the number claim is the conclusion. The original goes from numbers to proportions. This answer goes from proportions to numbers. Same ingredients, completely different recipe.

**D. A greater number of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs, so there are more small modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments, since a higher percentage of large Victorian apartments than of small modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (3% of students)
"So" and "since" are the giveaway. "So" points to the conclusion: there are more modern apartments. "Since" points to a premise: the proportion difference. The original argument concludes with proportions; this one concludes with total counts. Same building blocks, wrong blueprint.

**E. A smaller number of modern apartments than of Victorian apartments have freestanding bathtubs, even though there are fewer modern apartments than there are large Victorian apartments. This establishes that a higher proportion of Victorian apartments than of modern apartments have freestanding bathtubs.** — Bad Evidence Match / Bad Conclusion Match (10% of students)
The modifier goes the wrong way. The original starts with "full-length science-fiction" and then drops "full-length." This answer starts with plain "Victorian" and then adds "large." It is like trying to parallel a story about taking off a hat by telling one about putting on a hat. Same hat, opposite action. Close but not a match.
