## S3 · Q25 — Weaken

**Correct answer:** A

### Stimulus
To test the claim that vitamin C is effective in treating acne, scientists administered it to one group of subjects and a placebo to a control group. The group receiving vitamin C had less severe acne during the study than did the control group. It was subsequently discovered, however, that half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell, no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups. Therefore, we can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.

### Prompt
Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?

### Standard
**Argument breakdown**
- **Opposing Evidence:** During a study, a group of subjects receiving vitamin C had less severe acne than did the control group, which received a placebo.
- **Evidence:** Half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell [which pill they were given], no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups.
- **Conclusion:** We can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.
- **Evaluate:** Let's make sure that we're clear about what happened to the different groups that are compared in this study. We know that some subjects who received vitamin C had less severe acne than those who received a placebo. Among the subjects who didn't know which kind of pill they were given, there was no difference in the severity of acne. This means that the subjects who received vitamin C and also had less severe acne all knew that they were taking vitamin C, not a placebo. At first, the study seems to show a correlation between taking vitamin C and a reduction in acne. That's evidence—not proof, but evidence—that vitamin C reduces acne. But wait! There's a problem with the study. If people knew which pill they were taking, that could affect the results somehow. From this the argument concludes—at least tentatively—that vitamin C does not help reduce the severity of acne. This looks like an "unproven vs. proven false" flaw. Because this one particular study had a problem, it hasn't necessarily proven that vitamin C helps treat acne. But it's still possible that vitamin C does help treat acne, even if this one study hasn't proven that it does. We can't jump from the details of this one study to a general claim about the benefits of vitamin C.
- **Goal:** A correct answer might provide evidence that the vitamin C actually did help to reduce subjects' acne, in spite of the apparent problem with the way the study was conducted.

**Answer choices**
**A. The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.** — Correct (50% of students)
If the vitamin C subjects started with more severe acne than the placebo subjects, then the study results look very different. The vitamin C group had less severe acne during the study despite starting with worse acne — suggesting vitamin C actually reduced their acne enough to surpass the control group. Even among those who did not know which pill they received, having "no difference" in severity is significant if the vitamin C subgroup began with worse acne. Equal outcomes from unequal starting points suggest the treatment was effective. This undermines the conclusion that vitamin C provides no real benefit.

**B. None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.** — No Impact (5% of students)
This answer rules out a potential confounding factor — vitamin C subjects secretly taking extra vitamin C. But ruling out this possibility does not change the study's findings or the argument's reasoning. The study already showed the vitamin C group had less severe acne overall but no difference among subjects who did not know their pill. Whether anyone took extra doses does not affect this comparison. Even if they did take extra, we would need to know whether those subjects were in the knowing or unknowing subgroup to evaluate the impact.

**C. During the study, the severity of the subjects' acne was lower than the national average.** — No Impact (5% of students)
Whether all subjects had milder acne than the national average is irrelevant to the argument. The argument compares groups within the study — vitamin C versus placebo, knowing versus unknowing. External comparisons to the national average do not affect whether vitamin C was responsible for the differences observed within the study. Both groups could have below-average acne and the argument's reasoning would be unchanged.

**D. Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.** — Strengthens (32% of students)
If placebo subjects consumed foods naturally rich in vitamin C, this muddies the distinction between the two groups. The control group was effectively getting some vitamin C through diet, which could have reduced their acne. If the placebo group's acne was already being reduced by dietary vitamin C, then the absence of a difference between groups does not prove vitamin C is ineffective — it suggests both groups were receiving some vitamin C. However, this actually supports the argument's conclusion more than it weakens it: if both groups got vitamin C and there was no difference, then additional vitamin C through pills provides no extra benefit.

**E. Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.** — No Impact (9% of students)
Some subjects who knew they had placebos did not take them. This does not meaningfully affect the argument. Those subjects are in the placebo group regardless — they were not receiving vitamin C whether or not they swallowed their placebo pills. A placebo contains no active ingredient, so not taking it produces the same pharmacological result as taking it. At most, this introduces a minor methodological concern, but it does not provide any evidence that vitamin C actually reduces acne severity.

### Brief
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing acne severity.
- **Evidence:** In a study, the vitamin C group had less severe acne. But half the subjects in each group knew which pill they received. Among those who could not tell, there was no difference in acne severity.
- **Evaluate:** The argument attributes the initial positive results to a placebo-like effect — subjects who knew they were taking vitamin C may have experienced psychological benefits. But this reasoning assumes the two groups started with comparable acne severity. If the vitamin C group had worse acne to begin with, the fact that their acne was less severe during the study is more impressive.
- **Goal:** Find an answer that undermines the conclusion by suggesting vitamin C actually did help, despite the study's complications.

**Answer choices**
**A. The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.** — Correct (50% of students)
If the vitamin C group started with worse acne, then equal outcomes during the study suggest vitamin C actually helped. Equal results from unequal baselines undermines the no-benefit conclusion.

**B. None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.** — No Impact (5% of students)
Ruling out extra vitamin C doses does not change the study's findings. The comparison between knowing and unknowing subjects is unaffected.

**C. During the study, the severity of the subjects' acne was lower than the national average.** — No Impact (5% of students)
The national average is irrelevant. The argument compares groups within the study, not against external benchmarks.

**D. Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.** — Strengthens (32% of students)
If the placebo group also got vitamin C through food, the lack of difference between groups still suggests the pills added no benefit. This supports the conclusion rather than weakening it.

**E. Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.** — No Impact (9% of students)
Not taking a placebo has the same pharmacological effect as taking one — there is no active ingredient either way. This does not affect the vitamin C comparison.

### Another Way
**Argument breakdown**
- **Conclusion:** Vitamin C does not actually help with acne. The study's positive results were all in people's heads.
- **Evidence:** The vitamin C group did better overall, but half the subjects knew what they were taking. When you look only at the people who did not know, there was no difference. So the argument chalks it up to placebo effect.
- **Evaluate:** The argument makes a sneaky assumption: that both groups started with equally bad acne. But what if the vitamin C group had worse acne from the start? Then ending up at the same level as the control group would actually be evidence that vitamin C works — it just brought them down to baseline. The study's design flaw does not automatically mean the vitamin C did nothing.
- **Goal:** Find the answer that gives a reason to believe vitamin C actually helped, despite the messy study design.

**Answer choices**
**A. The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.** — Correct (50% of students)
Plot twist: the vitamin C group started with worse acne. That changes everything. The study found "no difference" between unknowing subjects — but if the vitamin C people had worse acne going in and ended up at the same level, that is evidence the vitamin C worked. It pulled them up to even. The argument assumed everyone started at the same place. This answer reveals they did not, and suddenly "no difference" looks like evidence of a treatment effect rather than evidence against one.

**B. None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.** — No Impact (5% of students)
Nobody snuck extra vitamin C. Okay. That just means the study ran as designed — everyone got what they were supposed to get and nothing more. The argument's logic is about why the benefit only showed up in people who knew they were taking vitamin C. This answer does not touch that question at all.

**C. During the study, the severity of the subjects' acne was lower than the national average.** — No Impact (5% of students)
Everyone in the study had better skin than average? Good for them. But the argument is about whether vitamin C caused the difference between groups in the study, not about how everyone compared to the general population. You could run this study on a group of supermodels and the internal comparison would work the same way.

**D. Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.** — Strengthens (32% of students)
The placebo group was eating vitamin C-rich foods? If anything, that helps the argument. If both groups were getting vitamin C (one through pills, one through diet) and there was no difference, that is more evidence vitamin C does not help with acne. This answer accidentally supports the conclusion instead of weakening it.

**E. Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.** — No Impact (9% of students)
They did not take their sugar pills. So they got zero active ingredient instead of zero active ingredient. Nothing changed. A placebo is a placebo whether it is in your stomach or in your pocket. This does not provide any reason to believe vitamin C helps with acne, which is what would be needed to weaken the argument.
